Chapter 11 · Superconductivity, magnetic screening and flux quantisation

Separate persistent current from magnetic flux expulsion and derive two measurable length and flux scales of a superconducting state.

From first principles

Connect a material current law to magnetic screening

A vanishing electrical resistance by itself allows an already existing current to persist. To predict the equilibrium field profile in a thick superconductor one needs additional physical input. In a uniform isotropic superconductor with no vortices the second London relation connects the curl of the supercurrent to the induction. Here \(n_s\) counts carriers of mass \(m\) and signed charge \(q\). Combine this constitutive law with static Ampère’s law:

\[\nabla\times\mathbf j_s=-\frac{n_s q^2}{m}\mathbf B,\]
\[\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf j_s .\]

Take a second curl of the field law and use \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). The sign from the vector identity cancels that in the London relation:

\[\nabla^2\mathbf B=\frac{\mathbf B}{\lambda_L^2},\]
\[\lambda_L=\sqrt{\frac{m}{\mu_0n_s q^2}}.\]

Fill \(x>0\) with the material and impose a parallel surface field \(B(0)=B_0\). With no field growth deep in the bulk, only one exponential survives:

\[B(x)=B_0e^{-x/\lambda_L}.\]

Thus the single measured length constrains a ratio \(m/(n_s q^2)\). This construction assumes linear local response and a field low enough for the vortex-free state and its prescribed surface condition to apply.

Background · London length
\[B(x)=B(0)e^{-x/\lambda_L}\]

This is a field profile in a thick homogeneous superconductor in a linear screening regime. A thin slab requires both boundary conditions.

Derivation and applications

Determine how deeply a parallel field penetrates a finite slab

The exponential London solution assumes a sample extending far beyond a single exposed surface. A film has two surfaces whose screening currents can overlap. Consider an infinite slab of total thickness \(2d\), occupying \(-d<x<d\). Apply a static induction \(B_a\) parallel to its faces, along \(z\), and assume a uniform London penetration length \(\lambda\). Work in the vortex-free Meissner state with fields small enough for linear response. Translational invariance parallel to the slab gives the boundary problem

\[\frac{d^2 B_z}{dx^2}=\frac{B_z}{\lambda^2},\]
\[B_z(-d)=B_z(d)=B_a .\]

A general solution has a hyperbolic cosine and a hyperbolic sine. Reflection about the midplane makes the field even, which removes the sine term. The surface field fixes the remaining coefficient. Ampère’s law then determines the screening current, whose sign reverses at the midplane,

\[B_z(x)=B_a\frac{\cosh(x/\lambda)}{\cosh(d/\lambda)},\]
\[J_y(x)=-\frac{B_a}{\mu_0\lambda}\frac{\sinh(x/\lambda)}{\cosh(d/\lambda)}.\]

A probe at the centre and a probe integrating flux over the cross-section read different quantities. The latter reports the thickness average obtained by integrating the field profile,

\[\frac{\overline B}{B_a}=\frac{1}{2dB_a}\int_{-d}^d B_z(x)\,dx=\frac{\lambda}{d}\tanh(d/\lambda).\]

For a thick slab the mean fraction tends to \(\lambda/d\), consistent with two active surface layers. For a thin slab it is \(1-(d/\lambda)^2/3\) to leading order. Thin samples can therefore exhibit weak parallel-field exclusion even when the material is fully superconducting throughout their volume.

Worked example

Compare central and average field in a thick slab

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A sample of thickness \(600\,\mathrm{nm}\) with \(\lambda=100\,\mathrm{nm}\) has \(d/\lambda=3\). In \(B_a=2.00\,\mathrm{mT}\) its central field is

\[2.00/\cosh3=0.199\,\mathrm{mT}.\]

The mean induction is

\[2.00\,\tanh(3)/3=0.663\,\mathrm{mT}.\]

The higher mean follows directly from the substantial contribution of material close to both exposed faces.

Second application

Extract a penetration length using a local probe

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A probe halfway through a \(120\,\mathrm{nm}\) film measures \(0.800 B_a\). The central-field solution gives \(d/\lambda=\operatorname{arcosh}(1.25)=0.693\), so \(\lambda=86.6\,\mathrm{nm}\). The same film should transmit a mean fraction \(\tanh(0.693)/0.693=0.866\). Comparing this value with an independent flux integral tests the assumptions of uniform thickness and a single penetration length.

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Compare two magnetic histories

A normal conductor dissipates energy from a sustained current. A superconductor can support persistent current and, in an appropriate low-field equilibrium state, expels magnetic flux from its bulk. Imagine cooling a specimen through its transition in a field and subsequently measuring its interior. Perfect conductivity alone would imply that a previously existing flux could remain frozen. Equilibrium flux expulsion supplies additional evidence about the state; sample shape, vortices and pinning affect what a real experiment detects. A resistance measurement and a magnetisation measurement therefore probe different properties.

Derive the London screening length

Let mobile condensate carriers have density \(n_s\), mass \(m_s\) and charge \(q_s\), using a consistent convention for particles or pairs. In a bulk region without vortices the London relation is

\[\nabla\times\mathbf j_s=-(n_s q_s^2/m_s)\mathbf B.\]

Combine this with magnetostatic Ampère's law \(\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf j_s\) and \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). Taking a curl gives

\[\nabla^2\mathbf B=\mathbf B/\lambda_L^2,\]
\[\lambda_L=\sqrt{m_s/(\mu_0n_s q_s^2)} .\]

For a half-space \(x>0\) with surface field parallel to the surface and deep interior field zero,

\[B(x)=B(0)e^{-x/\lambda_L}.\]

At depth \(3\lambda_L\), 4.98% of the surface amplitude remains. Taking \(n_s=2.0\times10^{28}\) m⁻³ electrons and electron mass in the simple carrier convention gives \(\lambda_L\approx37.6\) nm. Real effective masses, temperature-dependent superfluid density and nonlocality can change this value. Mixing pair density with single-electron charge would introduce a systematic factor error.

Quantise a circulating phase

A coherent condensate has a phase \(\phi\) whose change around a closed path must be \(2\pi n\) for integer \(n\) if the complex order parameter is single-valued and nonzero along the path. The gauge-invariant momentum is \(m_s\mathbf v_s=\hbar\nabla\phi-q_s\mathbf A\). Integrating around a loop relates circulation and flux. If a path lies sufficiently deep in a thick ring that its current density is negligible, the velocity term vanishes and enclosed flux comes in units \(\Phi_0=h/|q_s|\). For paired carriers \(|q_s|=2e\) the unit is \(2.068\times10^{-15}\) Wb. In thin rings where the kinetic term matters, it is the full fluxoid combination that is quantised.

Use length scales to interpret the experiment

The penetration depth characterises field screening. A separate coherence length \(\xi\) measures the distance over which the condensate amplitude recovers after a perturbation. In bulk Ginzburg–Landau theory near the transition, the ratio \(\lambda/\xi\) distinguishes type I and type II at \(1/\sqrt2\). In a type-II material between the lower and upper critical fields, flux can penetrate in quantised vortices with normal-like cores surrounded by screening currents. A specimen in this mixed state can retain superconducting regions while admitting magnetic flux. Stating field, temperature and cooling history is essential when interpreting the observed signal.

Locate the upper critical field from the linearized order parameter

The London equation assumes an established superconducting density. Near the loss of superconductivity at a high applied field, the order parameter itself becomes small and its spatial variation matters. For a bulk type-II material near its transition use Ginzburg–Landau free-energy density terms \(\alpha|\psi|^2+\beta|\psi|^4/2+|(-i\hbar\nabla-q_s\mathbf A)\psi|^2/(2m_s)\) with \(\alpha<0\), \(\beta>0\) and pair charge magnitude \(|q_s|=2e\). Very close to the upper critical field the density is small, field screening is weak and a uniform induction \(B\) is a suitable leading approximation.

Minimizing the free energy with respect to \(\psi^*\) and discarding the cubic term gives a charged-particle magnetic eigenproblem. The lowest transverse kinetic eigenvalue is the lowest Landau energy \(\hbar|q_s|B/(2m_s)\), achieved with no longitudinal variation. A nontrivial small solution is possible at the onset when this cancels \(\alpha\). Defining \(\xi^2=\hbar^2/(2m_s|\alpha|)\) and \(\Phi_0=h/|q_s|\) gives

\[B_{c2}=\frac{\Phi_0}{2\pi\xi^2}.\]

Thus a coherence length inferred from upper critical induction measures the scale of condensate recovery. Penetration depth instead controls field screening in an already developed condensate. The GL derivation is controlled near the critical temperature and for orbital suppression. Strong paramagnetic pair breaking or pronounced anisotropy can complicate the interpretation of a measured high-field transition.

Connect critical induction to vortex spacing

At a specified temperature close to the transition a bulk specimen loses its superconducting response at \(B_{c2}=1.20\) T. The inferred coherence length is

\[\sqrt{2.068\times10^{-15}/(2\pi\times1.20)}=16.6\,\mathrm{nm}.\]

An independent penetration-depth measurement at the same temperature yields \(\lambda=120\) nm, giving \(\lambda/\xi=7.24\), above the type-II boundary. Using lengths measured at unrelated temperatures would weaken that check.

At mean induction 0.200 T within the mixed state each singly quantized vortex carries one flux quantum. Its share of sample area is

\[\Phi_0/B=1.03\times10^{-14}\,\mathrm{m^2}.\]

For a triangular lattice that area also equals \(\sqrt3\,a_v^2/2\), giving vortex spacing \(a_v=109\) nm. Increasing induction fourfold halves the spacing if the array remains in the mixed state and retains its geometry. Near \(B_{c2}\) cores overlap and an isolated-vortex picture becomes unreliable even though the linearized onset scale remains useful. Comparing a measured spacing with \(\Phi_0/B\) tests flux counting independently of the inferred coherence length and of the magnetic field profile around any single vortex.

A useful unit check compares the superconducting flux quantum with the electron flux scale from ordinary Landau-level counting. A condensate pair has twice the charge magnitude of one electron, giving half the flux quantum for a phase winding. At 0.200 T a square micrometre should contain about \(BA/\Phi_0=96.7\) singly quantized vortices away from edges. Counting vortices in an image therefore gives a bulk average induction estimate independent of a local peak-field calibration. A surface barrier, pinning or an insufficiently large field of view can account for a local deviation from the average.

Imaging successively larger regions tests how well such local counts represent the bulk.

Study the full derivation

A field profile that distinguishes a superconductor from a perfect conductor · Structure, excitations and probes of solids

Zero dc resistance tells how a current can persist. Field expulsion tells how a specimen responds when it enters a superconducting equilibrium state. Derive the London length, calculate what a finite slab and a thin film actually screen, and connect magnetic and electrical measurements to the same superfluid density.

Chapter 12 · Pairing, excitation gaps and what a spectrum measures

Use a two-component excitation model to predict spectroscopic signatures, then distinguish three physically different gaps encountered across the course.

From first principles

See how pairing opens a spectroscopic gap

Begin with a single normal-state band and let \(\xi\) be its energy measured from the chemical potential. A minimal mean-field model for a uniform paired state mixes an electron amplitude with a hole amplitude through a complex pairing energy \(\Delta\). The two diagonal energies have opposite signs. To find its stationary excitations write the eigenvalue condition:

\[H=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\Delta\\\Delta^*&-\xi\end{pmatrix},\qquad \det(H-EI)=(\xi-E)(-\xi-E)-|\Delta|^2=0 .\]

It follows that the two branches are \(E_\pm=\pm\sqrt{\xi^2+|\Delta|^2}\). At the former Fermi surface \(\xi=0\), even the cheapest positive excitation costs \(|\Delta|\). To predict a tunnelling spectrum for constant isotropic gap and nearly constant normal density of states, count the normal states mapping into an energy interval \(dE\). The Jacobian from \(\xi=\pm\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2}\) gives the normalized density, with the electron and hole weights combined:

\[\frac{D_s(E)}{D_n(0)}=\frac{|E|}{\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2}}\quad (|E|>|\Delta|).\]

There are no states within the ideal gap at this level of description. Finite temperature, lifetime broadening and instrumental resolution change the measured conductance near its edges. The model assumes a nonzero \(\Delta\); it predicts consequences of pairing without deriving the attractive interaction that supports it.

Derivation and applications

Predict the weights of a particle peak and its partner

A pairing gap determines excitation energies, but a momentum-resolved spectrum also measures how strongly an electron couples to each peak. Consider a clean, uniform superconductor with a real positive gap \(\Delta\), at temperature low enough to neglect thermal broadening. Measure the normal-state energy \(\xi\) at one wavevector from the chemical potential. In the electron and hole basis the positive-energy eigenvector has components \(u,v\). The first row of its eigenvalue equation requires

\[(\xi-E)u+\Delta v=0,\]
\[\frac{v}{u}=\frac{E-\xi}{\Delta},\]
\[E=\sqrt{\xi^2+\Delta^2}.\]

Normalize the vector to unit probability. Since \((E-\xi)(E+\xi)=\Delta^2\), the squared amplitude ratio can be written without subtracting two nearly equal energies. The resulting coherence factors are

\[|u|^2=\frac{1}{1+(E-\xi)/(E+\xi)}=\frac12\left(1+\frac{\xi}{E}\right),\]
\[|v|^2=\frac12\left(1-\frac{\xi}{E}\right).\]

The negative-energy eigenvector is orthogonal to the positive one and consequently has electron weight \(|v|^2\). In the ideal single-particle spectral function at this momentum the two sharp peaks have unit total weight,

\[A(\epsilon)=|u|^2\delta(\epsilon-E)+|v|^2\delta(\epsilon+E),\]
\[\int A(\epsilon)\,d\epsilon=1 .\]

Thus the weights exchange as the wavevector passes through the normal Fermi surface. At \(\xi=0\) the two weights are equal. Well above the surface the positive-energy peak carries almost all the weight. To compare both sides quantitatively an experiment must account for occupation and matrix elements; ordinary photoemission alone samples the occupied side most directly.

Worked example

Resolve a weak partner peak

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

At a selected momentum let \(\xi=3.00\,\mathrm{meV}\) and \(\Delta=4.00\,\mathrm{meV}\). The spectral energies are \(+5.00\) and \(-5.00\,\mathrm{meV}\), with respective electron weights \(0.800\) and \(0.200\). At a momentum with the opposite \(\xi\) and the same gap the two peak energies stay fixed while their weights reverse. Such a weight exchange tests particle–hole mixing more specifically than the peak separation alone.

Second application

Reconstruct a gap using corrected peak areas

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

In a different sample the peaks are separated by \(6.00\,\mathrm{meV}\), and corrected electron spectral areas have positive-to-negative ratio \(3:2\). Their normalized weights are \(0.600\) and \(0.400\), so

\[\xi/E=0.200.\]

With \(E=3.00\,\mathrm{meV}\) this gives \(\xi=0.600\,\mathrm{meV}\) and \(\Delta=\sqrt{3.00^2-0.600^2}=2.94\,\mathrm{meV}\). Half the separation overestimates the gap here because the probed momentum lies away from the normal Fermi surface.

Try it · Bcs weights

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Build excitations on a paired state

For a conventional fully gapped superconductor, a mean-field description mixes particle and hole amplitudes near the Fermi surface. With \(\xi_k=\epsilon_k-\mu\) and gap parameter \(\Delta\), a simplified matrix for a time-reversed pair of states is

\[\begin{pmatrix}\xi_k&\Delta\\\Delta^*&-\xi_k\end{pmatrix}\binom{u_k}{v_k}=E\binom{u_k}{v_k},\qquad E_\pm=\pm\sqrt{\xi_k^2+|\Delta|^2}.\]

A positive-energy quasiparticle has minimum energy \(|\Delta|\) where the normal band crossed the chemical potential. Breaking a pair into two excitations can cost at least \(2|\Delta|\). These are different thresholds, so the probe and transition being discussed must be specified. The matrix displays level repulsion, reminiscent of the nearly free electron calculation, but its basis states and the physical origin of the coupling are different.

Transform a density of states

Suppose the normal-state density is approximately constant over the gap scale and the gap is isotropic. For \(E>|\Delta|\),

\[|\xi|=\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2},\]
\[d|\xi|/dE=E/\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2}.\]

Counting the paired branches relative to the normal-state convention yields \(D_s(E)/D_n=|E|/\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2}\) outside the gap and zero inside in the ideal zero-broadening model. The edge singularity is integrable. States removed from the gap reappear near its edges. Thermal broadening, disorder, nodes or an anisotropic gap can change the measured shape.

A low-temperature tunnel junction with a normal counterelectrode of nearly constant density can use differential conductance as a probe of this spectrum, subject to matrix-element and broadening effects. Peaks at biases \(\pm1.5\) mV suggest \(|\Delta|\simeq1.5\) meV in this geometry. Peak separation on the voltage axis is 3.0 mV. Using the weak-coupling BCS ratio \(2|\Delta(0)|/(k_BT_c)\simeq3.53\) gives \(T_c\simeq9.86\) K. Measure \(T_c\) independently; agreement with this estimate supports the simple model, while deviation calls for closer investigation.

Select a second observation that distinguishes the mechanism

A nearly free electron band gap separates eigenvalues near a zone boundary. For conduction it also matters where the chemical potential sits. A superconducting gap opens at the Fermi surface on entering an ordered state, and has magnetic screening and a characteristic temperature dependence. A photonic stop band instead excludes real propagating wavevectors in a frequency range for the specified periodic optical medium. All three can produce missing spectral weight or reduced transmission. Their energies, frequency units, occupation rules and control parameters differ.

Return to the numerical optical stack with a prediction: making both layers identical closes its first stop band even though the artificial zone boundary remains. For a proposed superconducting gap, repeat the tunnelling and magnetisation measurements as temperature crosses the transition. For an electronic band edge, relate spectroscopy to filling, dispersion and transport. This combination of an analytic limit, a controlled change and an independent observable is what makes an interpretation of a spectrum persuasive.

For continued study, see the MIT lectures on Theory of Solids, including quasiparticles and superconducting electrodynamics.

Read the phase difference across a superconducting weak link

A single-particle tunnelling spectrum can reveal an excitation gap. A weak junction between two superconductors can also carry a coherent pair current that responds directly to their relative condensate phase. Use the gauge-invariant phase difference \(\varphi\), including the vector-potential line integral across the junction. For a simple tunnel junction in the low-transparency regime, its leading coupling energy is

\[U_J=-E_J\cos\varphi.\]

Its \(2\pi\) periodicity reflects invariance under an integer phase winding and the energy minimum at aligned phase for a conventional junction.

A potential difference \(V\) between the condensates causes their relative phase to advance at \(\dot\varphi=2eV/\hbar\), choosing a consistent orientation of current and voltage. Combining electrical power \(IV\) with the rate of change of junction energy gives \(IV=E_J\sin\varphi\,\dot\varphi\), hence

\[I=I_c\sin\varphi,\]
\[I_c=\frac{2eE_J}{\hbar}.\]

Under a steady subcritical current the phase can remain constant while \(V=0\). At constant nonzero voltage the phase winds and the ideal coherent current oscillates with frequency \(f_J=2e|V|/h\). These two statements distinguish a DC pair current with no terminal voltage from an AC pair response under bias. A real junction includes quasiparticle conduction and capacitance, so its observed total current can contain additional components.

Convert a microwave frequency into a voltage

A junction biased at 5.00 μV has Josephson oscillation frequency \(f_J=(2e/h)V=2.42\) GHz. Illuminate the junction with a stable microwave reference of frequency 10.0 GHz. If its mean phase advance locks to one full winding per reference cycle, then \(\langle\dot\varphi\rangle=2\pi f_{\rm ref}\), requiring mean voltage \(hf_{\rm ref}/(2e)=20.7\) μV. Locking to two windings per cycle gives 41.4 μV. Such voltage steps probe phase dynamics and the charge associated with coherent transfer.

Suppose the same device has measured critical current \(I_c=30.0\) nA at the operating temperature. Its coupling energy is \(\hbar I_c/(2e)=9.87\times10^{-24}\) J, corresponding to \(E_J/k_B=0.715\) K. At a bath temperature of 50 mK thermal energy is appreciably smaller, although phase fluctuations can still depend on capacitance and the surrounding circuit. If either electrode is driven normal, the equilibrium phase-link energy disappears and the ideal supercurrent relation ceases to apply. Observing quantized phase locking and an excitation gap therefore accesses two different signatures of the superconducting state; their characteristic frequencies need not be numerically identical.

The two Josephson relations yield a further calibration test that does not require measuring energy directly. At fixed microwave frequency, the voltage separation of successive locking steps is \(hf_{\rm ref}/(2e)\) independently of junction area in the ideal phase model. Increasing junction area can change total critical current and the visibility or width of current intervals over which locking persists, yet should leave the quantized voltage spacing fixed. Looking at spacing and current width separately distinguishes uncertainty in the reference frequency from a change in the junction coupling or its electromagnetic environment.

Estimate a spectral gap when peaks have finite width

Add a phenomenological lifetime broadening to the isotropic quasiparticle density of states by evaluating \(z/\sqrt{z^2-1}\) at \(z=(E+i\Gamma)/\Delta\) and taking the physical nonnegative real density. Hold the gap itself fixed in all three runs.

Try it · Dynes

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Infer a superconducting gap from a tunnelling spectrum · Structure, excitations and probes of solids

A tunnel junction reports differential conductance versus voltage. Extracting a superconducting energy gap requires connecting a condensate's quasiparticle dispersion to a density of states, then accounting for temperature and finite spectral resolution before comparing peaks with the data.

Problem sheet 6 · Screening, spectra and the meaning of a gap

Work out the length and energy scales before deciding which measurement would identify the underlying order.

1. A magnetic profile below a transition

A thick superconducting half-space has \(B(120\ \mathrm{nm})=0.20 B(0)\) in a low parallel field. (a) Infer the penetration depth and predict the ratio at 240 nm. (b) If superfluid density falls by a factor four, how does the profile change at 120 nm, assuming constant mass? (c) Why could this estimate fail in a mixed state?

Use a length inferred from an amplitude

For an exponential \(\lambda=120/\ln 5=74.6\) nm. At double depth the ratio is

\[0.20^2=0.040.\]

A factor-four decrease in density doubles \(\lambda\); at the original depth the ratio becomes

\[\sqrt{0.20}=0.447.\]

Vortex penetration creates spatially varying fields and the measured signal may no longer describe screening from one flat surface.

2. Flux and a tunnelling spectrum

A thick ring encloses \(6.20\times10^{-15}\) Wb on a path with negligible current. A normal-metal–superconductor junction on the same material shows peaks at \(\pm0.90\) mV. (a) Estimate the flux integer for charge \(2e\). (b) Infer the gap and weak-coupling estimate of \(T_c\). (c) Identify two independent quantities a measurement could check.

Keep flux quantisation independent of the spectroscopic estimate

Dividing by \(2.068\times10^{-15}\) Wb gives approximately three flux quanta. The peak magnitude suggests \(\Delta=0.90\) meV. Using \(k_B=0.08617\) meV/K yields

\[T_c=1.80/(3.53\times0.08617)=5.92\,\mathrm{K}.\]

A magnetisation or heat-capacity measurement locates the transition independently; a scan over ring flux states tests the period without using the tunnelling interpretation.

3. Two missing signals with different mechanisms

A dielectric multilayer has a narrow optical transmission minimum. A separate metal film develops a low-temperature gap in tunnelling conductance. (a) Give a controlled change and a quantitative limit for the optical structure. (b) Give two independent tests of the superconducting interpretation. (c) Explain why small transmission alone cannot establish dissipation.

Test the mechanism that produced the missing signal

For the lossless two-layer stack, take both indices to \(n\) at fixed cell size. The first folded branches then meet at \(fa/c=1/(2n)\), with no forbidden interval. For the film, follow the tunnelling feature with temperature and check for low-field magnetic screening at the same transition. A lossless stack can reject transmission by reflection; testing absorption requires an energy balance including reflected power and other output channels.

Infer a Fermi surface from a transport measurement

A three-dimensional metal at low temperature has Hall coefficient −6.24 × 10⁻¹¹ m³/C and resistivity 2.00 × 10⁻⁸ Ω m. Assume a single isotropic electron band with free-electron mass and spin degeneracy two. Estimate the carrier density, Fermi wavevector, scattering time and mean free path. Explain what a second mobile carrier species would change.

Worked solution

In the one-band Drude limit R_H = −1/(ne), giving n ≈ 1.00 × 10²⁹ m⁻³. Counting occupied states in a sphere with two spin states gives n = k_F³/(3π²), so k_F ≈ 1.44 × 10¹⁰ m⁻¹. The associated Fermi speed ℏk_F/mₑ is 1.66 × 10⁶ m/s.

The conductivity is 1/ρ = ne²τ/mₑ. Therefore τ ≈ 1.77 × 10⁻¹⁴ s and ℓ = v_F τ ≈ 29.5 nm. Multiplying k_F by ℓ gives about 423, comfortably larger than unity. The lifetime only becomes a distance after choosing the velocity of carriers at the Fermi surface.

For simultaneous electron and hole conduction, the weak-field Hall signal weights the densities with squared mobilities and with opposite signs. The longitudinal conductivity instead adds their positive mobility-weighted contributions. A small Hall coefficient can then arise from compensation. Inserting it into the single-band density formula would give a misleadingly large density.

Explore these relations

  • For magnetic penetration, compare finite-slab profiles with the thick-sample exponential limit used in the question. Open the live calculation.
  • For the tunnelling spectrum, vary gap and lifetime broadening and distinguish spectral filling from the gap scale. Open the live calculation.

Chapter 13 · How alternating bonds split a one-dimensional band

Two periodic chains contain precisely the same number of atoms at the same spacing. One has equal bonds and the other alternates strong and weak bonds. The difference changes which electron energies can propagate, even without changing the on-site energy of any atom.

Choose a primitive cell that repeats the bonds

Let a cell of length \(a\) contain orbitals A and B. An electron hops between A and B in the same cell with real amplitude \(t_1>0\), and between B and A in the next cell with amplitude \(t_2>0\). Use one orbital per site, neglect interactions and set both on-site energies to zero. We can absorb an overall hopping sign into the B basis state. Translation by one cell multiplies each component by \(e^{ika}\), although the relative amplitudes on A and B remain unknown. Writing those amplitudes as \(A,B\) turns the real-space nearest-neighbour equations into

\[EA=(t_1+t_2e^{-ika})B,\]
\[EB=(t_1+t_2e^{ika})A.\]

The cell boundary crossed by the second hopping accounts for its phase. The two coefficients are complex conjugates as required by Hermiticity. Substitute the second relation into the first without dividing by energy. A nonzero component then requires

\[E^2=(t_1+t_2e^{ika})(t_1+t_2e^{-ika}).\]

The two energies at each wavevector are

\[E_\pm(k)=\pm\sqrt{t_1^2+t_2^2+2t_1t_2\cos ka}.\]

For positive hoppings their magnitude is largest at \(k=0\), namely \(t_1+t_2\), and smallest at \(ka=\pi\), namely \(|t_1-t_2|\). The gap is \(2|t_1-t_2|\). At one spinless electron per cell the lower band is full, so this is also the independent-particle excitation gap in a large periodic chain.

Read the band formation from two limits

Take \(t_1=1.0\,\mathrm{eV}\), \(t_2=0.6\,\mathrm{eV}\). The lower band runs from \(-1.6\) to \(-0.4\,\mathrm{eV}\), the upper from \(0.4\) to \(1.6\,\mathrm{eV}\). The gap is \(0.8\,\mathrm{eV}\), while each bandwidth is \(1.2\,\mathrm{eV}\). If \(t_2\) tends to zero, the cell becomes an isolated pair with two discrete bonding energies \(\pm t_1\). Bands flatten because cells can no longer exchange probability. At \(t_1=t_2=t\), the spectrum becomes \(\pm2t|\cos(ka/2)|\) in the reduced zone. The two pieces meet at its edge. The two-site construction then folds the simple uniform chain into a smaller wavevector interval.

One can also read off a velocity. Away from a crossing, differentiate \(E_\pm\) to obtain

\[v_\pm=\mp a t_1t_2\sin(ka)/[\hbar|E_\pm|].\]

At extrema of a gapped band it vanishes. At the equal-hopping crossing the derivative written as a ratio has a removable directional ambiguity, because the ordered upper and lower bands meet with opposite slopes.

Exchange the two bonds

Keep \(t_1\) fixed and move \(t_2\) through it. Compare both the gap and the bandwidth. Interchanging the numerical values of the two hoppings exactly preserves the bulk energies. The experiment in the next chapter distinguishes these patterns by opening the chain at a specified bond. A termination supplies physical information that the unordered hopping pair and the energy graph alone cannot encode.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on inferring the hopping strengths

A measured dimer band has gap \(0.50\,\mathrm{eV}\) and reaches \(1.45\,\mathrm{eV}\) above the symmetry centre at \(k=0\). Find the two hopping amplitudes and predict the upper-band width.

Work through the solution

The sum is 1.45 eV and the absolute difference is 0.25 eV. Adding and subtracting gives the unordered pair 0.85 and 0.60 eV. The upper bandwidth is

\[1.45-0.25=1.20\,\mathrm{eV}.\]

Bulk energies do not determine which hopping is interrupted by a particular surface cut.

Expand the squared band energy near the Brillouin-zone boundary for unequal hoppings. Identify the small-\(q\) dispersion and its range.

Work through the solution

Set \(ka=\pi+qa\). Since \(\cos(\pi+qa)\simeq-1+(qa)^2/2\),

\[E^2\simeq(t_1-t_2)^2+t_1t_2(qa)^2.\]

For sufficiently small \(q\) the upper edge has quadratic curvature \(E_+\simeq|t_1-t_2|+t_1t_2(qa)^2/[2|t_1-t_2|]\). The last expansion fails when its second term in the squared energy competes with the gap term.

If the same constant on-site energy \(U\) is added to every site, which inferred quantities would move in an experiment measuring energies against a fixed reference?

Work through the solution

The Hamiltonian gains \(U I\), moving both bands and their centre by \(U\). Their gap, widths, group velocities, and the hopping estimates from differences remain unchanged. The half-filling chemical potential can still lie inside the shifted gap.

Further reading · Asbóth, Oroszlány and Pályi, SSH model and bulk bands.

Chapter 14 · Finding an end state one bond at a time

The bulk spectrum of an alternating chain is unchanged when strong and weak bonds exchange places. A boundary with a named first site breaks this equivalence. The zero-energy equations provide its wavefunction without diagonalising a large matrix.

Start at an exposed A site

Use the alternating chain with intracell link \(t_1\) and intercell link \(t_2\), both positive. End the left edge at \(A_1\). The first B site connects \(A_1\) and \(A_2\). Seek an eigenstate at \(E=0\) supported only on A sites. Each A-site equation then holds because all neighbouring B amplitudes vanish. Each B-site equation requires cancellation between its two neighbouring A amplitudes. Hence

\[0=t_1 A_j+t_2 A_{j+1},\]
\[A_j=(-t_1/t_2)^{j-1}A_1.\]

Relative signs matter in the cancellation, even though a local probability measurement sees only squared magnitudes. On a semi-infinite chain the geometric series for the norm converges only for \(r=t_1/t_2<1\). Normalising gives

\[|A_1|^2=1-r^2.\]

The amplitude decay length satisfies \(e^{-a/\ell}=r\), so \(\ell=a/\ln(t_2/t_1)\). The probability decay length is half that. As the bulk gap closes, the decay length diverges and the bound state spreads into the interior.

Impose the far boundary too

A finite chain can have \(M\) A sites and \(M-1\) B sites, ending on A at both edges. Every B site still imposes exactly the recurrence above, leaving one overall free amplitude. The resulting state has exactly zero energy for any positive hoppings. Its finite norm is the sum of \(M\) successive powers of \(r^2\). At \(r=1\) the apparent zero over zero in the closed formula is resolved by summing \(M\) unit terms. The state then has probability \(1/M\) at every A site.

When \(r>1\) its norm remains finite in this finite sample and the weight accumulates at the right edge. An equal-sublattice finite chain ending on B instead has an additional boundary equation. Two exponentially localised end solutions can then hybridise, shifting their energies slightly above and below zero. Equal sublattice counts alone provide no exact zero eigenvalue. A change of boundary conditions can therefore alter the interpretation of a measured near-zero peak.

Connect the recurrence to the bulk phase

The off-diagonal matrix element \(h=t_1+t_2e^{ika}\) traces a circle centred at \(t_1\) with radius \(t_2\) as \(k\) crosses the Brillouin zone. If \(t_2>t_1\) it winds once around zero. If \(t_2<t_1\) it misses zero. Passing between the two with fixed cell choice requires the gap \(2|h|\) to close. A diagonal potential on just one sublattice would break the off-diagonal symmetry used to pin energies to zero. The winding statement and the boundary argument require a consistent assignment of the unit cell and termination.

For \(r=0.5\) the first three semi-infinite A probabilities are 0.75, 0.1875 and 0.046875. For \(r=0.90\), the first site has only 0.19 and the amplitude decay length increases from \(1.44a\) to \(9.49a\). Use the finite odd chain below to move the density from one end to the other. At reciprocal hopping ratios the two distributions must be mirror images. Check this using the endpoint readouts.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on inspecting a local measurement

Four successive A sites have relative intensities \(1,0.36,0.1296,0.0467\) near a left edge. Infer the hopping ratio and amplitude decay length. Would a local intensity probe detect the alternating signs of the state?

Work through the solution

Ratios of neighbouring probabilities equal \(r^2\), hence \(r=0.60\). The amplitude length is

\[-a/\ln(0.60)=1.96a.\]

A measurement proportional to the absolute square loses the sign. Interference with a phase reference or a measurement sensitive to the amplitude across a bond can access the phase relation.

A finite odd chain has five A sites and hopping ratio \(r=0.8\). What fraction of zero-mode probability lies on its first two A sites?

Work through the solution

Sum the finite geometric sequence. The requested fraction is

\[(1+r^2)/\sum_{j=0}^4r^{2j}=(1-r^4)/(1-r^{10})=0.661.\]

Taking the semi-infinite formula would give

\[1-r^4=0.590.\]

The far boundary changes the normalisation substantially for this short array.

Can bond disorder that preserves positive nonzero nearest-neighbour hoppings destroy the exact zero solution of a finite chain ending on A at both ends?

Work through the solution

At each B site retain the local recursion \(A_{j+1}=-(t_{1j}/t_{2j})A_j\). It produces a finite nonzero solution after choosing \(A_1\), with all B amplitudes zero. The profile is a product of local ratios and may cease to look like a single exponential. A generic A-site potential would invalidate the zero-energy equation.

Further reading · Asbóth, Oroszlány and Pályi, edge states and bulk-boundary correspondence.

Chapter 15 · Why a honeycomb lattice has Dirac cones

In an alternating chain the two paths from A to B can cancel at a special wavevector. The honeycomb lattice adds a third path. Their cancellation now occurs at isolated points in a two-dimensional Brillouin zone and produces a linear low-energy dispersion.

Add the three Bloch phases

Each honeycomb A site has three nearest B neighbours at displacements \(d(0,1)\), \(d(\sqrt3/2,-1/2)\), and \(d(-\sqrt3/2,-1/2)\), where \(d\) is the bond length. For equal real hopping magnitude \(t\), sum the translation phase for each displacement. The common off-diagonal factor is

\[f(\boldsymbol k)=e^{ik_yd}+2e^{-ik_yd/2}\cos(\sqrt3 k_xd/2),\]
\[E_\pm=\pm t|f|.\]

This uses a basis phase convention that includes the orbitals' intra-cell positions. A rephasing can change the phase of \(f\) but leaves its magnitude invariant. Along \(k_y=0\), cancellation occurs where \(1+2\cos(\sqrt3 k_xd/2)=0\). Choose \(K=(4\pi/(3\sqrt3d),0)\). The counterpart at \(-K\) is inequivalent up to primitive reciprocal vectors. Near either valley many wavevectors represent distinct states even when their energies almost coincide.

Linearise at a zero, then test the approximation

Write \(\boldsymbol k=K+\boldsymbol q\) with \(qd\ll1\). At \(K\) the cosine equals \(-1/2\) and the sine \(\sqrt3/2\). Differentiating the cosine term with respect to \(k_x\) consequently gives \(-3d/2\). Differentiating the two exponential factors with respect to \(k_y\) gives

\[id+id/2=3id/2.\]

The constant terms cancel. Therefore

\[f(K+\boldsymbol q)\simeq \tfrac{3d}{2}(-q_x+iq_y),\]
\[E_\pm\simeq\pm\hbar v_F|\boldsymbol q|,\]
\[v_F=\frac{3td}{2\hbar}.\]

With \(t=2.7\,\mathrm{eV}\), \(d=0.142\,\mathrm{nm}\) and \(\hbar=0.6582\,\mathrm{eV\,fs}\), the speed is

\[0.874\,\mathrm{nm/fs}=8.74\times10^5\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

At \(qd=0.05\) the leading energy magnitude is \(0.2025\,\mathrm{eV}\). The energy varies linearly with momentum displacement even though the microscopic Hamiltonian contains only hopping between neighbours. The two components describe sublattice amplitude, distinct from the real electron spin.

Break the equivalence of the sublattices

Assign on-site energies \(+\Delta\) to A and \(-\Delta\) to B. The characteristic equation of the two-by-two Hamiltonian becomes

\[E^2=\Delta^2+t^2|f|^2.\]

A gap \(2|\Delta|\) opens at K. For \(\Delta=0.10\,\mathrm{eV}\), the edge sits at \(0.10\,\mathrm{eV}\) and the predicted conduction energy at \(qd=0.05\) becomes about \(0.226\,\mathrm{eV}\). Far enough from the origin the slopes recover their nearly linear values. Close to it, expansion of the square root gives

\[E_+\simeq|\Delta|+\hbar^2v_F^2q^2/(2|\Delta|).\]

A band-edge mass \(|\Delta|/v_F^2\) has emerged. The expansion requires \(\hbar v_Fq\ll|\Delta|\).

Compare the exact positive-energy cut along \(q_x\) with the local cone in the graph. Unequal deviations to the right and left of K are allowed by the lattice and cannot be captured by the lowest-order isotropic cone. The reflected cut at the other valley restores the time-reversal relation.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a measured slope

A near-valley spectrum has slope \(0.55\,\mathrm{eV\,nm}\). Infer \(v_F\) and the nearest-neighbour hopping for a bond length \(d=0.142\,\mathrm{nm}\). A gap of \(80\,\mathrm{meV}\) then appears with the same asymptotic slope. What is \(|\Delta|\)?

Work through the solution

The slope equals \(\hbar v_F\). Dividing by \(0.6582\,\mathrm{eV\,fs}\) gives \(0.836\,\mathrm{nm/fs}\). Since the slope also equals \(3td/2\),

\[t=2(0.55)/(3\times0.142)=2.58\,\mathrm{eV}.\]

The two edges shift symmetrically by half the full gap, giving \(|\Delta|=40\,\mathrm{meV}\).

Consider a gapless honeycomb cone exactly at the valley centre. What does the tight-binding Hamiltonian predict for the energy and eigenvectors, and what information comes from approaching along a direction?

Work through the solution

At \(f=0\) the matrix vanishes, so every two-component vector in the degenerate subspace has energy zero. For a nearby nonzero \(f\), a chosen eigenbranch fixes a relative phase between its A and B components according to the argument of \(f\). The limiting eigenvector therefore depends on the direction of approach even though the limiting energy is always zero.

At \(q=0\), repeat the eigenvector argument with \(\Delta>0\). Predict a consequence for sublattice-resolved spectroscopy at the two band edges.

Work through the solution

At the valley centre the Hamiltonian is diagonal with entries \(+\Delta,-\Delta\). The upper state lives on A and the lower on B in this basis. A probe selective to A should see more weight at the conduction edge than at the valence edge. Farther from the valley centre the hopping mixes the sublattices again.

Further reading · Delft Topology in Condensed Matter, honeycomb Hamiltonian and Dirac points.

Chapter 16 · Counting carriers and weighing a cyclotron orbit

A conical band has no constant quadratic band mass at its touching point. A carrier on a finite-energy orbit still has a measurable cyclotron mass. Counting states fixes the Fermi circle and determines how this mass changes when a gate adds charge.

Count states in a two-dimensional disc

Put a two-dimensional sheet of area \(A=L_xL_y\) under periodic boundary conditions. Neighbouring allowed wavevectors differ by \(2\pi/L_x\) and \(2\pi/L_y\), so each state occupies reciprocal-space area \((2\pi)^2/A\). At zero temperature fill all conduction states up to radius \(k_F\), measured from a Dirac point. There are two spin states and two inequivalent valleys in ideal monolayer graphene, giving total degeneracy \(g=4\). Density relative to charge neutrality is therefore

\[n=g\frac{\pi k_F^2}{(2\pi)^2}=\frac{k_F^2}{\pi},\]
\[E_F=\hbar v_F\sqrt{\pi n}.\]

For electron and hole densities use \(|n|\) under the square root and give \(E_F\) the sign of \(n\) relative to the touching point. Differentiating the state count with respect to energy gives the density of states per area \(D(E)=g|E|/[2\pi(\hbar v_F)^2]\). It tends to zero at an ideal neutral cone. Thermal broadening, disorder and a gap all change what a finite-resolution probe observes near neutrality.

Background · Cyclotron mass
\[m_c=(\hbar^2/2\pi)\,\partial A/\partial E\]

The area is that of a closed constant-energy orbit in reciprocal space normal to the magnetic field. This is a measured orbital mass rather than a universal free-particle parameter.

Let a perpendicular field bend the orbit

For a wavepacket in a band with weak magnetic field,

\[\hbar\dot{\boldsymbol k}=-e\,\boldsymbol v\times\boldsymbol B,\]

where \(e\) denotes the positive elementary charge. An isotropic band has velocity normal to a circular constant-energy contour. The orbit is traversed with speed \(|\dot{\boldsymbol k}|=e v_F B/\hbar\). Dividing its circumference \(2\pi k_F\) by that speed gives the period. Express its angular frequency as \(eB/m_c\) to identify

\[m_c=\frac{\hbar k_F}{v_F}=\frac{|E_F|}{v_F^2}.\]

The mass measures the time needed to travel around a contour. It depends on the radius even when the magnitude of the group velocity stays fixed. For a general band area \(\mathcal A(E)\), neighbouring contours separated perpendicular to themselves by \(dk_\perp\) differ in energy by \(\hbar v\,dk_\perp\). Thus \(|d\mathcal A/dE|=\oint dl/(\hbar v)\), which gives \(m_c=\hbar^2|d\mathcal A/dE|/(2\pi)\) by integrating the same equation of motion.

Convert a gate density into an energy and a mass

At \(n=10^{12}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}=10^{16}\,\mathrm{m^{-2}}\) and \(v_F=10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\), one obtains \(k_F=1.772\times10^8\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}\), \(E_F=0.117\,\mathrm{eV}\) and \(m_c=1.87\times10^{-32}\,\mathrm{kg}=0.0205m_e\). Quadrupling the density doubles the Fermi energy and cyclotron mass. By contrast a single isotropic parabolic band \(E=\hbar^2 k^2/(2m)\) has the same counting geometry but constant \(m_c=m\). Transport mobility or disorder scattering time does not enter the state count, although scattering can obscure the cyclotron motion.

Change velocity and signed density. Raising the velocity at fixed density increases \(|E_F|\) while reducing the mass because the orbit radius stays fixed and the wavepacket moves faster. Exactly at charge neutrality, a single classical Fermi orbit no longer describes the carriers. Finite-field quantum levels replace the continuum picture in the next chapter.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a density sweep

Two cyclotron measurements on the same gapless sheet return masses \(0.024m_e\) and \(0.036m_e\). Assume the velocity is independent of density. Infer the ratio of absolute carrier densities and the change in the weak-field cyclotron frequency at fixed field.

Work through the solution

Since \(m_c\) scales as \(\sqrt{|n|}\), the second density is \((0.036/0.024)^2=2.25\) times the first. The frequency \(eB/m_c\) drops to two thirds of its first value. The masses alone cannot reveal whether either measurement used electrons or holes.

Find the weak-field cyclotron radius at the worked density \(10^{12}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2}\) and field \(0.10\,\mathrm T\). How does raising the velocity at fixed density affect it?

Work through the solution

The semiclassical radius is

\[r_c=v_Fm_c/(eB)=\hbar k_F/(eB).\]

With \(k_F=1.772\times10^8\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\),

\[r_c=1.17\,\mu\mathrm m.\]

At fixed density and field the radius stays fixed when velocity changes. The orbit period changes because the speed around that circle changes.

A measurement doubles positive \(E_F\) at constant temperature sufficiently close to zero for the step filling approximation. How do state density per area and density of states per energy change?

Work through the solution

For fixed \(v_F\), the occupied density scales as \(E_F^2\), so it rises fourfold. The derivative \(D(E_F)\) scales as \(|E_F|\) and doubles. Confusing the count with its derivative would give the wrong charge change inferred from spectroscopy.

Further reading · Novoselov and colleagues, measured energy dependence of the cyclotron mass.

Chapter 17 · Quantising the orbit and finding the zero Landau level

In a magnetic field the two kinetic momentum components cannot be specified simultaneously. In a conical band their commutator turns the two sublattice equations into coupled oscillator equations. Their spectra differ by one state, leaving an unpaired level at zero energy.

Construct the oscillator from kinetic momentum

In one valley of a gapless monolayer use the long-wavelength Hamiltonian \(H=v_F(\sigma_x\Pi_x+\sigma_y\Pi_y)\), with Pauli matrices acting on sublattices. For an electron of charge \(-e\),

\[\boldsymbol\Pi=\boldsymbol p+e\boldsymbol A.\]

In a uniform perpendicular field \(B>0\), applying \([p_j,A_k]=-i\hbar\partial_j A_k\) gives

\[[\Pi_x,\Pi_y]=-ie\hbar B.\]

Introduce \(a=(\Pi_x-i\Pi_y)/\sqrt{2e\hbar B}\). Its adjoint uses the opposite sign, giving \([a,a^\dagger]=1\). The magnetic length \(\ell_B=\sqrt{\hbar/(eB)}\) sets the spatial size of the oscillator.

\[H=v_F\sqrt{2e\hbar B}\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\a^\dagger&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad H^2=2e\hbar Bv_F^2\begin{pmatrix}aa^\dagger&0\\0&a^\dagger a\end{pmatrix}.\]

In the B component the squared Hamiltonian has oscillator numbers \(0,1,2,\ldots\). In the A component \(aa^\dagger=a^\dagger a+1\), so its sequence starts at one. For integer \(n\ge1\) combine \(|n-1\rangle\) on A with \(\pm|n\rangle\) on B in equal magnitudes. Acting with \(H\) confirms energies \(E_{n,\pm}=\pm v_F\sqrt{2e\hbar Bn}\). For \(n=0\), the B-only spinor with \(|0\rangle\) is annihilated by \(a\). Its energy vanishes. Valley and field conventions can exchange which sublattice carries that state.

Count independent centres at the same energy

Choose gauge \(A_y=Bx\) and retain translation along \(y\). Quantisation gives

\[k_y=2\pi j/L_y.\]

The kinetic momentum \(\Pi_y=\hbar k_y+eBx\) places the orbit centre at \(x_0=-\hbar k_y/(eB)\). Successive centres differ by \(2\pi\ell_B^2/L_y\). Counting centres in a width \(L_x\) gives \(L_xL_y/(2\pi\ell_B^2)\), or \(eB/h\) states per area for each spin and valley, apart from edge corrections. This degeneracy is independent of level index. Spin splitting, lattice effects and interactions are set aside here.

At \(B=1\,\mathrm T\), \(v_F=10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\), \(\ell_B=25.7\,\mathrm{nm}\) and the first positive level is \(36.3\,\mathrm{meV}\) above zero. The next sits at \(51.3\,\mathrm{meV}\). The spacing between the first two positive levels is consequently only 15.0 meV. At 4 T all these energies double while the number of orbit centres per unit area quadruples. A parabolic band would instead give equally spaced levels at fixed field.

Identify the measurable dependence

Compare the first few positive levels as field changes. Dividing \(E_n\) by \(\sqrt{Bn}\) should return one material-dependent coefficient. An optical measurement sees transitions between occupied and available states, subject to matrix-element selection rules. A measured transition energy therefore need not coincide with the energy of an individual level. At zero field this discrete-level construction loses its normalisable magnetic oscillator; the limiting energies on the graph describe the approach from finite field.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on two spectral lines

At \(B=2\,\mathrm T\) an ideal sample has a zero-to-first level spacing of \(48\,\mathrm{meV}\). Infer its velocity relative to \(10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\). Predict the first-to-second positive-level spacing at \(8\,\mathrm T\).

Work through the solution

The reference first level at 2 T is \(36.3\sqrt2=51.3\,\mathrm{meV}\), so \(v_F\simeq0.935\times10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\). Raising the field by a factor of four doubles all energies. The first level at 8 T is 96 meV, and \(E_2-E_1=96(\sqrt2-1)=39.8\,\mathrm{meV}\). This inference assumes an ungapped cone without interaction shifts.

At \(B=2\,\mathrm T\), estimate the number of orbit centres in area \(1\,\mu\mathrm m^2\) for a single level. Give both the per-flavour count and the full spin–valley count.

Work through the solution

Use \(eBA/h\), with \(A=10^{-12}\,\mathrm m^2\). This gives roughly 484 centres per flavour and 1934 across four flavours. The numbers are approximate because a finite sample admits edge corrections, whereas the area formula is a bulk degeneracy density.

At high positive level index show how the level spacing regains the semiclassical cyclotron frequency \(eB/m_c\).

Work through the solution

Write \(E_n^2=2e\hbar B v_F^2 n\). At large \(n\) the difference \(E_{n+1}-E_n\simeq dE/dn=e\hbar Bv_F^2/E_n\). Dividing by \(\hbar\) gives \(eB/m_c\) with \(m_c=E_n/v_F^2\). This connection uses many occupied levels, while the unpaired zero state is outside the semiclassical limit.

Further reading · Jiang and colleagues, infrared spectroscopy of Landau levels in monolayer graphene · Li and colleagues, spatially resolved graphene Landau levels.

Chapter 18 · How the same electrons carry heat and charge

A metal with high electrical conductivity often also conducts heat well. The quantitative relation involves an open-circuit measurement, the thermal width of the Fermi surface, and the energy dependence of the states able to carry current.

Weigh the states that can carry a current

Use an isotropic band of quasiparticles with energy \(E\), velocity magnitude \(v(E)\), density of states per volume \(D(E)\) and elastic relaxation time \(\tau(E)\). In a weak electric field or temperature gradient the departure from local equilibrium is proportional to the relaxation time times the applied driving term. For a given transport direction the angular average gives \(\langle v_x^2\rangle=v^2/3\) in three dimensions. Define \(\Sigma(E)=D(E)v(E)^2\tau(E)/3\). Electrical current weights a particle by its charge \(q=-e\), while heat current weights it by \(E-\mu\). Three moments consequently suffice for the two linear responses:

\[K_j=\int\Sigma(E)(E-\mu)^j[-\partial_E f(E)]\,dE,\]
\[j=0,1,2.\]

Let \(\mathcal E\) denote the electric/electrochemical field with any chemical-potential gradient properly included. The resulting currents along one direction are \(j_e=q^2K_0\mathcal E-qK_1\nabla T/T\) and \(j_Q=qK_1\mathcal E-K_2\nabla T/T\). The factor \(E-\mu\) in the thermal drive follows from differentiating the Fermi function with respect to temperature. The repeated first moment is a manifestation of reciprocal coupling of electrical and thermal transport in this zero-field model.

Impose the actual electrical boundary condition

A voltage probe draws negligible charge current. Set \(j_e=0\) to obtain \(\mathcal E=S\nabla T\) with \(S=K_1/(qTK_0)\). Substitute this field into the heat current to get

\[\kappa_e=(K_2-K_1^2/K_0)/T.\]

Under the positive measure \(\Sigma(-f')dE/K_0\), the bracket divided by \(K_0\) is the variance of \(E-\mu\). Hence the open-circuit electronic thermal conductivity is nonnegative. Measuring with zero imposed electric field would retain a thermally driven charge current and a different heat-flow coefficient.

For a degenerate metal with smooth \(\Sigma\), the weight \(-f'\) is a narrow even peak. Replacing \(\Sigma\) by \(\Sigma(\mu)\) gives \(K_1=0\), \(K_0=\Sigma(\mu)\), and \(K_2=(\pi^2/3)(k_BT)^2\Sigma(\mu)\). The dimensionless second moment follows by integrating \(x^2/[4\cosh^2(x/2)]\) over the real axis. With \(\sigma=q^2K_0\),

\[\frac{\kappa_e}{\sigma T}=L_0=\frac{\pi^2k_B^2}{3e^2}=2.44\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{W\,\Omega/K^2}.\]

At \(T=100\,\mathrm K\), a metal with \(\sigma=2.0\times10^7\,\mathrm{S/m}\) has estimated electronic \(\kappa_e=48.8\,\mathrm{W/(m\,K)}\). A total measured conductivity of 60 would leave about \(11\,\mathrm{W/(m\,K)}\) for other carriers if the assumptions hold. Phonons, inelastic scattering or strong energy dependence may change that inference.

Select a narrow range of transport energies

A model energy filter \(\Sigma\propto\exp[-(x-c)^2/(2w^2)]\), with \(x=(E-\mu)/(k_BT)\), \(c,w\) dimensionless, introduces a tunable departure from this metallic limit. The Seebeck coefficient is \((k_B/q)\langle x\rangle\) and the normalised Lorenz ratio is \(\operatorname{var}(x)/(\pi^2/3)\). A very narrow filter centred above the chemical potential produces strong signed thermopower but little heat transport at zero charge current, because current-carrying particles have almost identical excess energies. Those statements refer to the ratio and thermal moments; the absolute conductivity also depends on the overall transmission.

The experiment integrates these moments numerically and plots their normalised weight against the reference Fermi derivative. Vary \(c\) from positive to negative at fixed width. Predict which observable reverses sign and which is invariant before reading the numerical results.

Tutorial on open-circuit transport

For a fitted transport weight with \(\langle x\rangle=0.50,\ \langle x^2\rangle=2.25\), determine the Seebeck coefficient and \(L/L_0\). Use \(k_B/e=86.17\,\mu\mathrm{V/K}\).

Work through the solution

For electrons \(S=-43.1\,\mu\mathrm{V/K}\). The variance is

\[2.25-0.50^2=2.00.\]

Hence

\[L/L_0=2.00/(\pi^2/3)=0.608.\]

Using the second moment without subtracting the square of the first would represent the wrong electrical boundary condition.

Expand a smooth transport function to first order about \(\mu\). What controls the low-temperature sign of \(S\)?

Work through the solution

The constant term contributes nothing to \(K_1\) by symmetry. The slope gives

\[K_1\simeq\Sigma'(\mu)(\pi^2/3)(k_BT)^2.\]

Dividing by \(qTK_0\) yields

\[S\simeq[\pi^2k_B^2T/(3q)]\partial_E\ln\Sigma|_\mu.\]

For electrons a rising transport function gives negative \(S\) in the stated field convention.

A cold insulating bar can carry heat primarily through lattice vibrations. Chapter 19 · Why a polar crystal has two optical phonon frequencies

A uniform relative displacement of positive and negative ions creates an electric polarisation. A longitudinal vibration induces a macroscopic restoring field while a transverse displacement has a different electrical boundary condition. An infrared spectrum can reveal both frequencies.

Separate the ionic and electronic contributions

For one infrared-active optical mode let \(u\) be relative displacement per primitive cell, \(M\) the effective reduced mass, \(Z^*\) the effective charge in coulombs, and \(N\) the number of cells per volume. The oscillator equation driven by the macroscopic field is

\[M(\ddot u+\gamma\dot u+\omega_{\rm TO}^2u)=Z^*E.\]

The zero-macroscopic-field stiffness defines the transverse optical frequency in this oscillator model. Bound electronic response at these frequencies supplies a real relative background permittivity \(\epsilon_\infty\). The ionic polarisation is \(P_{\rm ion}=NZ^*u\), so \(D=\epsilon_0\epsilon_\infty E+P_{\rm ion}\).

With time convention \(e^{-i\omega t}\), solving for \(u\) gives

\[\epsilon_r(\omega)=\epsilon_\infty+\frac{S}{\omega_{\rm TO}^2-\omega^2-i\gamma\omega},\]
\[S=\frac{N(Z^*)^2}{\epsilon_0 M}.\]

The signed effective charge appears twice, once as drive and once in the dipole density. Its square therefore sets oscillator strength. At zero frequency the ions can follow the field and \(\epsilon_s=\epsilon_\infty+S/\omega_{\rm TO}^2\). Far above the phonon resonance but below electronic absorption the ionic term is small, making \(\epsilon_\infty\) the observed plateau.

Apply Gauss's law to a longitudinal vibration

Consider a long-wavelength, nonzero wavevector \(\boldsymbol k\) with no external free charge. Gauss's law gives

\[\boldsymbol k\cdot\boldsymbol D=0.\]

For a longitudinal field \(D_L=0\), so the ions create \(E_L=-NZ^*u/(\epsilon_0\epsilon_\infty)\). Substituting this opposing field into the undamped oscillator equation adds a restoring term. Thus

\[\omega_{\rm LO}^2=\omega_{\rm TO}^2+S/\epsilon_\infty,\]
\[\frac{\omega_{\rm LO}^2}{\omega_{\rm TO}^2}=\frac{\epsilon_s}{\epsilon_\infty}.\]

Equivalently the longitudinal collective mode is the zero of the lossless dielectric function. The transverse optical frequency is its pole. For a single isotropic mode this is the Lyddane–Sachs–Teller relation. With several infrared modes, anisotropy, damping or mobile free carriers, assigning one measured peak and one dielectric ratio to a single oscillator needs further justification.

Connect a reflectance band with the two modes

In the lossless limit rewrite \(\epsilon_r=\epsilon_\infty(\omega_{\rm LO}^2-\omega^2)/(\omega_{\rm TO}^2-\omega^2)\). Between the two frequencies this is negative. A transverse electromagnetic wave satisfies \(k^2=\epsilon_r\omega^2/c^2\), so its wavenumber becomes imaginary there in a nonmagnetic lossless crystal. At a planar vacuum boundary a semi-infinite crystal therefore has unit ideal reflectance over this interval. Finite damping makes the refractive index complex and allows absorption, smoothing the edges. The two mode frequencies have been deduced from mechanical response and electric boundary conditions; an electromagnetic wave interacting with the lattice is a coupled phonon–polariton excitation.

For \(f_{\rm TO}=6.0\,\mathrm{THz}\), \(\epsilon_\infty=4\) and \(\epsilon_s=9\), \(f_{\rm LO}=9.0\,\mathrm{THz}\). At 7.5 THz in the lossless limit \(\epsilon_r=4(81-56.25)/(36-56.25)=-4.89\). Below the mode, normal-incidence static-index reflectance is

\[[(\sqrt9-1)/(\sqrt9+1)]^2=0.25.\]

Above the phonon plateau the corresponding background reflectance approaches \(1/9\). The interactive plot evaluates the passive complex refractive index and the exact single-interface reflectance. Change the static permittivity and damping; compare the high-reflection interval with the lossless zero and pole.

Tutorial on recovering mode parameters

Optical data give \(f_{\rm TO}=8.0\) THz and \(f_{\rm LO}=10.0\) THz for a single weakly damped mode. With \(\epsilon_\infty=5.0\), estimate the static permittivity and dielectric increment.

Work through the solution
\[\epsilon_s=5(10/8)^2=7.8125.\]

The increment attributed to the optical mode is

\[7.8125-5=2.8125.\]

Angular and cyclic frequency ratios are equal, although an absolute oscillator strength \(S\) requires angular frequencies in the equation of motion.

At a fixed wavevector a probe sees displacement perpendicular to the wavevector. Why is the longitudinal restoring term absent in the electrostatic argument, and what additional coupling can still occur?

Work through the solution

Its ionic polarisation has \(\boldsymbol k\cdot\boldsymbol P_{\rm ion}=0\), hence it creates no longitudinal bulk bound charge \(-i\boldsymbol k\cdot\boldsymbol P_{\rm ion}\). There is no obligatory depolarising longitudinal field. A transverse propagating electromagnetic field can still drive the infrared-active vibration, as encoded in \(\epsilon_r(\omega)\).

Further reading · MIT solid state physics, optical phonons and dielectric response.

Chapter 20 · How one light isotope traps a lattice vibration

A perfect chain supports travelling lattice vibrations. Replace one atom by a lighter isotope while keeping every spring unchanged. Determine whether a vibration can now be confined to a few sites, calculate its frequency and decay length, and find out from a finite simulation when the word localised is justified.

What is permitted in the unperturbed crystal?

Let each atom of an infinite one-dimensional monatomic chain have mass \(M\), nearest-neighbour spring constant \(K\) and equilibrium spacing \(a\). A small longitudinal displacement \(u_j\) stretches the spring on the right by \(u_{j+1}-u_j\) and that on the left by \(u_j-u_{j-1}\). The restoring force is their difference multiplied by \(K\). Newton's equation conserves total momentum in the absence of external forces and makes uniform translation an exact zero-frequency motion.

For a travelling wave \(u_j(t)=Ue^{i(kja-\omega t)}\), the spatial difference contributes \(e^{ika}+e^{-ika}-2\). Thus

\[\omega^2(k)=\frac{4K}{M}\sin^2(ka/2).\]

This defines a continuous band from zero to \(\omega_{\max}=2\sqrt{K/M}\). At the upper edge adjacent sites move oppositely; for a fixed amplitude the springs experience their largest extension. A measured peak above that bound could not be a propagating plane wave in this model. It might indicate an incorrect force constant, another branch, an anharmonic response or a local structural change. We shall test one specific explanation using a spatial prediction as well as a spectral one.

The second difference still permits an evanescent displacement

Replace the centre atom by mass \(m=qM\). All equations except that at \(j=0\) remain the same. A vibration spatially confined to the defect should decay in both directions without any boundary driver replenishing it. The upper-band-edge sign pattern suggests \(u_j=U(-r)^{|j|}\), with \(0<r<1\). The sign alternation is crucial: using a positive decaying exponential in a monatomic acoustic branch would give a negative \(\omega^2\) for a freely oscillating stable chain. The ideal symmetric mass defect couples to an even displacement about the origin. An antisymmetric displacement would vanish there and would not feel a change of its inertia.

For \(j\ne0\), substitute the ratio \(u_{j+1}/u_j=-r\) on the right half chain into the equation of motion at frequency \(\omega\). At the centre the neighbours on either side both equal \(-rU\). These two equations must determine the same frequency:

\[\frac{M\omega^2}{K}=2+r+\frac1r,\]
\[\frac{m\omega^2}{K}=2(1+r).\]

Eliminating \(\omega\) gives \(q(1+r)^2=2r(1+r)\), or \(r=q/(2-q)\) when the decaying solution exists. The physical restriction \(0<r<1\) then requires \(0<q<1\). The complete result is

\[\omega_{\rm loc}=\frac{2\sqrt{K/M}}{\sqrt{q(2-q)}},\]
\[\frac{\ell}{a}=-\frac{1}{\ln r}.\]

The second expression measures the decay length of displacement amplitude. The envelope of intensity or squared amplitude decays twice as fast and has half that length. A group velocity evaluated by inserting a real wave number into the ordinary band cannot describe energy transport away from this mode. Its spatial decay replaces a real propagation phase by a complex lattice wave number with real part \(\pi/a\).

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Look at what happens when the defect barely differs from its host

Write \(q=1-\eta\) with a small positive mass reduction \(\eta\). The frequency ratio to the band edge is \(1/\sqrt{1-\eta^2}\simeq1+\eta^2/2\). The displacement ratio is \((1-\eta)/(1+\eta)\), giving \(\ell/a\simeq1/(2\eta)\). Two consequences go in opposite directions. A small mass mismatch always gives a mathematical above-band solution in this infinite one-dimensional model, but its frequency is hard to separate from the band and its spatial extent becomes large. An instrument with finite spectral width or a simulation with few atoms may therefore see little evidence of confinement.

Take \(q=0.60\). Here \(r=3/7\simeq0.429\); after three sites the signed amplitude relative to the centre is about \(-0.0787\). The decay length is about \(1.18a\). In frequency units \(\sqrt{K/M}\), the mode sits at 2.18218, clearly above the edge at 2. For \(q=0.98\), by contrast, the excess frequency is only about 0.020 per cent and the decay length is approximately \(25a\). An 80-site ring in the latter case lets the two tails meet at a distance comparable to their decay length. Interpreting the finite eigenvalue as an isolated impurity in an infinite crystal requires a size check.

Try it · Impurity

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Open this live calculation

Download the corresponding editable notebook. The browser recalculates the reduced equations directly; the notebook exposes the full calculation and its checks.

For an ideal isotope change the force constants are held fixed. A chemical substitution can change a bond's stiffness along with its mass, and a crystal with more than one atom per unit cell has additional bands. A heavy impurity can have other local or resonant behaviour in more general systems. The result here concerns an above-band normalisable mode of one mass defect in this harmonic acoustic chain. Simply plugging a value \(q>1\) into the algebraic frequency expression discards the decay condition and can give a spurious interpretation.

Construct the finite eigenproblem from kinetic and elastic energies

For a periodic ring write \(\mathcal M\) for the positive diagonal matrix of masses and \(\mathcal K\) for the cyclic second-difference stiffness. Elastic energy is \(u^\mathsf T\mathcal K u/2\), the sum of positive spring energies. Kinetic energy is \(\dot u^\mathsf T\mathcal M\dot u/2\). The variational equations give

\[\mathcal K u=\omega^2\mathcal M u.\]

Directly forming \(\mathcal M^{-1}\mathcal K\) yields a nonsymmetric matrix in ordinary Euclidean coordinates. It has the right eigenvalues mathematically, but feeding it to a symmetric eigensolver without transforming it would ask the solver to process a different matrix.

Set \(w=\mathcal M^{1/2}u\). The equivalent symmetric eigenproblem has operator \(\mathcal M^{-1/2}\mathcal K\mathcal M^{-1/2}\). An orthonormal eigenvector \(w\) corresponds to a physical displacement \(u=\mathcal M^{-1/2}w\). Consequently a detector measuring displacement at one site and a map of kinetic participation do not plot the same quantity. At fixed mode frequency the mean kinetic-energy weight at site \(j\) is proportional to \(m_j|u_j|^2\). The notch in the mass at the centre matters even after the spatial shape of displacement has been found.

The notebook forms the ring stiffness from two shifted identity matrices and uses an even site count so that the unperturbed zone edge is an allowed wave number. It reports both the frequency and the impurity's kinetic weight. Validate the result by substituting the recovered displacement into the original force balance at every site, including the two periodic endpoints. Check also the zero-frequency translation mode. A small defect residual and a correct band edge test different aspects of the assembly. The optional OneSimulation cell independently invokes its symmetric eigenproblem interface on the mass-weighted matrix and checks the residual of the highest mode.

Which experiment distinguishes localisation from a stiff crystal?

Suppose spectroscopy resolves an extra high-frequency line. Increasing every spring constant would shift the entire host band by the same square-root scaling. Changing a small set of isotopic masses makes an extra feature with large amplitude near those sites and leaves much of a large sample nearly unperturbed. A local displacement measurement moving away from the known defect therefore discriminates between two explanations that can be adjusted to match one peak frequency. The sign reversal is accessible to a phase-sensitive measurement; an intensity-only experiment determines the envelope but cannot directly establish that successive displacements are out of phase.

One can estimate the weight concentrated at the impurity analytically. With central displacement one, the mass-weighted norm divided by \(M\) is \(q+2\sum_{j\ge1}r^{2j}=q+2r^2/(1-r^2)\). The centre fraction is its first term divided by this sum. Even a pronounced peak need not contain all the energy, and changing the arbitrary normalisation of an eigenvector cannot change such a fraction. This gives a second scalar check more sensitive to errors in the eigenvector than the frequency alone.

Tutorial on localisation and finite rings

A measured displacement envelope falls by a factor of four over two lattice spacings. Within the one-defect model, infer the impurity mass and the frequency relative to the band edge.

Work through the solution

Two spacings multiply amplitude by \(r^2=1/4\), hence \(r=1/2\). The inverse formula gives

\[q=2r/(1+r)=2/3.\]

The frequency ratio is

\[1/\sqrt{(2/3)(4/3)}=3/\sqrt8\simeq1.061.\]

If the reported quantity were intensity rather than amplitude the inference would change, so the physical meaning of the recorded ordinate belongs with the number.

For a mass ratio of 0.6, compute the fraction of time-averaged kinetic energy at the impurity. Why is it different from a simple count of squared displacement?

Work through the solution

With \(r=3/7\), the sum over both sides is

\[2r^2/(1-r^2)=0.45.\]

The denominator of the mass-weighted norm is \(0.6+0.45=1.05\), giving a centre fraction of \(0.6/1.05=4/7\). Counting squared displacements alone would give \(1/1.45\), which overestimates the central kinetic contribution because that atom carries less mass.

The measured light defect has \(q=0.98\). Estimate a ring size for which the amplitude of one infinite-chain tail at the antipodal site is below one per cent of its central value.

Work through the solution

Require \(\exp[-Na/(2\ell)]<0.01\), hence \(N>2(\ell/a)\ln100\). With about 25 spacings per decay length this gives a little over 230 sites. This is a geometric tail criterion, not an error bound on every eigenvalue. For higher accuracy compare two larger rings and test the residual against the infinite-chain formula.
