Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 7. A field profile that distinguishes a superconductor from a perfect conductor

Zero dc resistance tells how a current can persist. Field expulsion tells how a specimen responds when it enters a superconducting equilibrium state. Derive the London length, calculate what a finite slab and a thin film actually screen, and connect magnetic and electrical measurements to the same superfluid density.

Separate frozen flux from equilibrium screening

For mobile carriers of mass \(m\), charge \(q\) and number density \(n_s\), a simple inertial equation \(m\dot{\mathbf v}=q\mathbf E\) gives

\[\partial_t\mathbf j_s=n_s q^2\mathbf E/m.\]

This first London equation explains dissipation-free acceleration by an electric field in the ideal response. Take its curl and use Faraday's law: \(\partial_t[\nabla\times\mathbf j_s+(n_s q^2/m)\mathbf B]=0\) at fixed uniform \(n_s\). The expression in brackets is time independent. This derivative equation by itself allows an arbitrary initial curl–field combination, so it does not force a previously existing static induction to leave a conductor.

In a simply connected, vortex-free equilibrium superconductor the second London relation supplies the missing condition,

\[\nabla\times\mathbf j_s=-(n_sq^2/m)\mathbf B.\]

A condensate phase argument gives the same curl relation locally: mechanical pair momentum is \(\hbar\nabla\varphi-q\mathbf A\); on a patch without a vortex \(\nabla\times\nabla\varphi=0\). With \(\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf j_s\) and \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\), take the curl once more to obtain

\[\nabla^2\mathbf B=\mathbf B/\lambda_L^2,\]
\[\lambda_L^2=m/(\mu_0 n_s q^2).\]

The pair of signs yields exponential decay, not sinusoidal oscillation into a bulk specimen.

The effective mass, charge and density must describe the same carriers. For Cooper pairs of mass about \(2m_e\), charge magnitude \(2e\), and pair density \(n_{\rm electron}/2\), the combination \(n_s q^2/m\) agrees with the expression in terms of participating electron density, charge \(e\) and mass \(m_e\). Mixing a pair charge with an electron density and electron mass overpredicts the stiffness by a factor of four. A material effective mass or nonlocal response may alter an inferred density, so a penetration-depth measurement fixes the stiffness combination rather than each microscopic parameter separately.

Solve a finite slab in a parallel applied field

A slab fills \(-d/2<x<d/2\) and a uniform applied induction \(B_a\hat z\) is imposed parallel to both faces. Assume the Meissner regime, negligible demagnetising fields, and a thickness large compared with microscopic dimensions needed for continuum electrodynamics. The even London solution is

\[B_z(x)=B_a\cosh(x/\lambda_L)/\cosh[d/(2\lambda_L)].\]

Its current is \(j_y=-(1/\mu_0)\partial_xB_z\), of opposite signs on the two faces. The mean induction is

\[\frac{\bar B}{B_a}=\frac{2\lambda_L}{d}\tanh\frac{d}{2\lambda_L}.\]

In a thick slab the centre field is exponentially small while the average falls only as \(2\lambda_L/d\), because a surface layer from each face still contains field. A probe at the centre and one measuring total flux through the cross section thus report different thickness dependence. In a thin slab the average is \(1-d^2/(12\lambda_L^2)+\cdots\), giving weak screening of a parallel field. This is not the perpendicular-field screening problem for a thin film, where fields outside the sample and lateral geometry lead to another length scale.

For \(\lambda_L=100\,{\rm nm}\), \(d=400\,{\rm nm}\), the centre ratio is \(\operatorname{sech}2=0.266\) and the mean ratio is

\[\tanh2/2=0.482.\]

With \(B_a=0.50\,{\rm mT}\) the current magnitude just inside a face is

\[B_a\tanh2/(\mu_0\lambda_L)=3.84\times10^9\,{\rm A/m^2}.\]

It flows only over a thin scale. If a material's critical current or critical field is exceeded, this linear vortex-free solution ceases to describe the experiment. Driven hysteresis or trapped vortices can then make the same external field produce different internal profiles after different preparation histories.

Connect kinetic energy to a sheet inductance

A thin uniform strip of length \(l\), width \(w\), thickness \(d\ll\lambda_L\) carries a current nearly uniformly through its thickness in a suitable transport geometry. The carrier kinetic energy per volume is

\[mn_s v^2/2=m j_s^2/(2n_s q^2)=\mu_0\lambda_L^2j_s^2/2.\]

Integrating over volume and using \(j_s=I/(wd)\) gives \(E_{\rm kin}=L_k I^2/2\) with \(L_k=\mu_0\lambda_L^2 l/(wd)\). Thus the sheet kinetic inductance is \(L_{k,\square}=\mu_0\lambda_L^2/d\), and the number of squares is \(l/w\). It is distinct from geometric inductance storing energy in the magnetic field around the wire.

For \(d=20\,{\rm nm}\), \(\lambda_L=100\,{\rm nm}\) the sheet value is about \(0.628\,{\rm pH}\) per square. A strip containing two thousand squares gives \(1.26\,{\rm nH}\) of kinetic inductance. Its resonance with a capacitor probes the sum of this inductance and geometric or stray contributions. If the superfluid density falls while the effective mass stays approximately fixed, \(\lambda_L^2\) increases and the resonant frequency tends to decrease. Measuring several lengths and widths can distinguish a distributed kinetic contribution from an approximately constant lead contribution. At frequencies approaching the gap scale a purely nondissipative London description is insufficient.

Determine what a thickness series can identify

A thin-film measurement sometimes fits the sheet kinetic inductance at a single thickness and quotes a bulk condensate density. In the uniform-film limit the measured product is \(L_{k,\square}d=\mu_0\lambda_L^2\). A thickness calibration error therefore rescales the inferred stiffness even if the resonance frequency is measured extremely accurately. For the \(20\,{\rm nm}\) example, a systematically inactive layer of \(2.0\,{\rm nm}\) leaves only \(18\,{\rm nm}\) contributing. Substituting the nominal thickness into a stiffness inference underestimates the conducting carrier density by ten percent if the other model assumptions hold. A series of films and an independent estimate of the active thickness can test this interpretation.

Compare with the parallel-field slab calculation by changing the ratio of thickness to penetration depth in the live profile. At a ratio of one, the centre and surface inductions differ only modestly. At a ratio of ten the interior is nearly field free while a substantial fraction of the averaged flux still resides near the two faces. The programme graphs \(B/B_a\) across the physical thickness and reports both centre and mean, which are different experimental weightings of the same field. Compare those numbers with an independently measured kinetic inductance before attributing a temperature shift entirely to an altered carrier density. Higher kinetic inductance can also reflect an increased effective mass or a reduced active cross section.

Open this experiment online

Compare parallel screening with a perpendicular-field measurement

For a wide film in perpendicular field the magnetic flux lines bend around the edges, and the one-dimensional parallel-field cosh solution no longer applies. Sample width, aspect ratio and the field outside the superconducting material enter the response. Rotate the same thin film through ninety degrees and an apparent change in susceptibility may therefore be largely geometrical. Verify orientation and demagnetising conditions before combining the two datasets into one estimate of penetration depth.

Tutorial on comparing probes

A thick slab shows a centre field less than one percent of the applied parallel field. What lower bound on \(d/\lambda_L\) follows from the local London solution?

Work through the solution

Require \(\cosh[d/(2\lambda_L)]>100\), hence \(d/\lambda_L>2\operatorname{arcosh}(100)\approx10.60\). The mean field at this threshold would still be about \(2/10.60=0.189\) of the applied field. A centre measurement at one percent therefore does not imply a one-percent cross-section average.

If the superfluid charge stiffness \(n_sq^2/m\) falls by half, how do penetration depth and sheet kinetic inductance change for the same film?

Work through the solution

The penetration depth increases by \(\sqrt2\), and the sheet kinetic inductance doubles because it scales as \(\lambda_L^2\). A resonance in which kinetic inductance is only one quarter of total inductance will shift less than the \(1/\sqrt2\) expected if all inductance doubled. The initial circuit partition must be estimated.

A ring retains flux after cooling in a field. Does this disprove the equilibrium slab result?

Work through the solution

The slab derivation assumed simple connectivity, a vortex-free state and prescribed fields on two faces. A ring permits nontrivial phase winding and fluxoid quantisation. A multiply connected circuit can maintain trapped flux even with local London screening in its material. Compare with a simply connected specimen under controlled zero-field cooling and field-cooling protocols before interpreting the ring as a direct slab test.

The University of Zurich solid-state course includes superconductivity and its magnetic signatures.

Compare ordinary resistive field diffusion with an equilibrium London profile.

Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 8. Infer a superconducting gap from a tunnelling spectrum

A tunnel junction reports differential conductance versus voltage. Extracting a superconducting energy gap requires connecting a condensate's quasiparticle dispersion to a density of states, then accounting for temperature and finite spectral resolution before comparing peaks with the data.

Diagonalise the paired-state Hamiltonian

In a simple isotropic weak-coupling description, pair a state of normal-state energy \(\xi_k=\epsilon_k-\mu\) with the time-reversed hole amplitude. Take a real gap \(\Delta>0\) by a phase choice. The two-component mean-field matrix has rows \((\xi_k,\Delta)\) and \((\Delta,-\xi_k)\), giving eigenvalues \(\pm E_k\) with \(E_k=\sqrt{\xi_k^2+\Delta^2}\). The positive-energy quasiparticle mixes particle-like and hole-like amplitudes with squared coefficients \((1+\xi_k/E_k)/2\) and \((1-\xi_k/E_k)/2\). Their sum is one. Near the normal-state Fermi surface they are equal, while far above it the excitation becomes predominantly particle like.

The minimum positive excitation energy is \(\Delta\). Creating two independent quasiparticles requires at least \(2\Delta\) in the ideal isotropic spectrum. These two thresholds refer to different probes. In an ideal normal-metal–insulator–superconductor tunnel junction, a bias can transfer one electron into an allowed quasiparticle state; the zero-temperature threshold is \(|eV|=\Delta\). In a junction between two identical gapped electrodes, lowest-order quasiparticle tunnelling at zero temperature instead begins near \(2\Delta\), aside from pair tunnelling and other processes. Confusing the physical junction type can double the reported gap.

Transform the normal-state density of levels

Assume approximately constant normal electronic density near the Fermi level over an energy range much larger than \(\Delta\). The quasiparticle dispersion flattens near \(\xi=0\). Mapping the two normal-state branches \(\xi=\pm\sqrt{E^2-\Delta^2}\) onto a positive energy and accounting for their electron spectral weights gives the normalised superconducting tunnelling density

\[\frac{N_s(E)}{N_n(0)}=\begin{cases}0,&|E|<\Delta,\\ |E|/\sqrt{E^2-\Delta^2},&|E|>\Delta.\end{cases}\]

The divergence at the gap edge is integrable. Indeed integrating from \(\Delta\) to \(A>\Delta\) gives \(\sqrt{A^2-\Delta^2}\), tending toward the corresponding normal integral at large cutoff. The edge feature represents redistribution of states from the gap, rather than creation of an infinite number of states. In a finite normal band the constant-density approximation is only local, so statements about total spectral weight should use a consistent large-energy model.

For \(\Delta=1.50\,{\rm meV}\), the ideal density at \(E=2.00\,{\rm meV}\) is \(2/\sqrt{4-2.25}=1.51\) times the normal level. At \(5.0\,{\rm meV}\) it is approximately \(1.048\), illustrating the slow approach to the normal baseline. A normalising window too close to the edge can bias the whole inferred curve downward. A comparison with a normal-state spectrum taken with the same junction can control a varying normal density or voltage-dependent transmission better than a single distant conductance point.

Derive the thermal convolution measured by a normal probe

In lowest-order tunnelling with a featureless normal tip and approximately constant matrix elements, the current is proportional to \(\int N_s(E)[f(E-eV)-f(E)]\,dE\). The two Fermi factors count occupied initial and available final states. Differentiating in bias gives normalised conductance \(G(V)/G_n=\int[N_s(E)/N_n][-f'(E-eV)]\,dE\). Here \(-f'(x)=\operatorname{sech}^2[x/(2k_BT)]/(4k_BT)\) has unit integral. At zero temperature it becomes a delta function and the conductance reproduces the density. At finite temperature it samples a window: conductance can be nonzero inside the underlying hard gap without any actual subgap states.

The full width at half height of this derivative is \(4k_BT\,\operatorname{arcosh}\sqrt2\approx3.53k_BT\). At \(T=2.0\,{\rm K}\) this is \(0.608\,{\rm meV}\), appreciable compared with a \(1.50\,{\rm meV}\) gap. The electron temperature at the tunnel contact can exceed the temperature reading of a nearby bath. Voltage noise and a finite lock-in excitation add other convolution kernels. A fit that varies gap, thermal width and lifetime broadening all freely against a single narrow window can have strongly correlated parameters. Measure an independent thermal reference or repeat with deliberately varied excitation to separate instrumental from material broadening.

Compute the ideal convolution with an edge-safe quadrature

Numerically sampling the divergent density at one grid point exactly on the edge gives an artificial infinity. A variable transformation removes it. For \(E>\Delta\) set \(\xi=\sqrt{E^2-\Delta^2}\). Then \((E/\sqrt{E^2-\Delta^2})\,dE=d\xi\). Integrate over positive \(\xi\) and include the negative-energy mirror:

\[G/G_n=\int_0^\infty[K_T(\sqrt{\xi^2+\Delta^2}-eV)+K_T(-\sqrt{\xi^2+\Delta^2}-eV)]\,d\xi.\]

Both terms are smooth at zero for positive temperature. This numerical reformulation follows directly from state counting.

The live spectrum uses this transformed integral with energies in meV and voltages in mV for a single electron. First choose a low but finite temperature and locate the two coherence features. Increase temperature at fixed \(\Delta\) to isolate the effect of the measurement kernel. A physically heated sample would generally also have a temperature-dependent gap; this controlled calculation keeps the intrinsic spectrum explicit. Compare the zero-bias conductance, peak height and high-bias baseline. A change in one feature alone seldom establishes the source of broadening.

Open this experiment online

Identify a spectral asymmetry that the symmetric model cannot fit

For a featureless normal tip and a particle–hole symmetric superconducting spectrum, the convolution with the even thermal kernel gives

\[G(V)=G(-V).\]

The average of opposite bias measurements estimates the even component, while half their difference isolates an odd component. A small bias-offset calibration error \(V_0\) produces an apparent odd part approximately \(-V_0\,dG_{\rm even}/dV\) for small shifts. It is therefore largest on steep gap-edge flanks and changes sign when the bias offset reverses. An energy-dependent normal background can generate a smoother intrinsic asymmetry; a temperature sweep through the transition and an independent voltage-zero calibration help distinguish these mechanisms.

Suppose the spectral flanks have slope \(1.8G_n/{\rm mV}\) and the measured odd part peaks near \(0.036G_n\). A voltage offset of only \(0.020\,{\rm mV}\) could generate that size, much smaller than the illustrative gap. Comparing the sign pattern on both flanks tests the derivative shape rather than fitting one data point. A single shifted peak position can reflect offset, thermal broadening or changing gap amplitude. Record both bias directions, both coherence peaks and their separation as well as their midpoint. A common shift of both peaks changes the midpoint but ideally leaves their separation unchanged.

The model also assumes a normal electrode with roughly flat density. If the tip itself is superconducting, the current is a convolution of both electrode densities and their occupation difference. In that case symmetric features can shift even when the sample gap is unchanged, requiring an independent tip calibration or a different junction model.

Tutorial on tunnelling data

An ideal normal-tip spectrum at very low temperature has gap edges separated by \(3.20\,{\rm mV}\). Estimate \(\Delta\), and compare with a weak-coupling transition temperature using \(2\Delta/(k_BT_c)\approx3.53\).

Work through the solution

The symmetric single-particle edges lie near \(\pm\Delta/e\), hence \(\Delta\approx1.60\,{\rm meV}\). Inserting \(k_B=0.08617\,{\rm meV/K}\) gives \(T_c\approx10.5\,{\rm K}\). This ratio is a weak-coupling benchmark for the isotropic model, not an exact conversion for every material. Verify the actual transition and junction type independently.

An experiment at \(4.2\,{\rm K}\) tries to distinguish two gaps differing by \(0.10\,{\rm meV}\) using peak positions alone. Which scale should be compared first?

Work through the solution

The thermal derivative width is \(3.53(0.08617)(4.2)\approx1.28\,{\rm meV}\), much larger than that gap difference. High signal quality and a constrained model can sometimes infer parameters finer than a raw linewidth, but separate visible peaks are unlikely from thermal resolution alone. Lower electron temperature and calibrated voltage modulation add stronger discriminating evidence.

What check distinguishes a quadrature error from true thermal subgap conductance?

Work through the solution

Refine the transformed integration grid, extend its energy cutoff and confirm the conductance is even in voltage for this symmetric model. A normal reference with \(\Delta=0\) should yield unity at every bias when the kernel is fully integrated. The computed zero-bias value for positive gap should fall as temperature decreases. A subgap signal sensitive to whether an energy grid happens to land on an edge is a numerical artifact.

The University of Zurich solid-state course develops superconducting excitations and their physical consequences.

Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 9. Read twist and mismatch from the moiré of two lattices

Stack two square grids that differ slightly in orientation or pitch. The slowly varying registry map is visible over many unit cells. Derive its scale and direction in reciprocal space, and find which combinations of layer parameters a single period measurement can resolve.

Subtract two spatial phases

One family of planes in layer one carries a density modulation proportional to \(\cos(\mathbf G_1\cdot\mathbf r)\), with \(|\mathbf G_1|=2\pi/a\). Layer two has spacing \(a(1+\delta)\) and is rotated by \(\theta\). Multiplying their modulations, as in a local registry correlation, produces half the sum of two cosines. The rapidly varying term contains \(\mathbf G_1+\mathbf G_2\); averaging over several original cells leaves the term with \(\Delta\mathbf G=\mathbf G_1-\mathbf G_2\). A distance parallel to \(\Delta\mathbf G\) that advances the resulting phase by \(2\pi\) spans one full registry period.

Taking the dot product of the rotated reciprocal vectors gives the exact geometric scale

\[\frac{\Lambda}{a}=\left[1+\frac{1}{(1+\delta)^2}-\frac{2\cos\theta}{1+\delta}\right]^{-1/2}.\]

The second perpendicular family of a square lattice rotates and rescales the same way. Its difference vector is perpendicular to the first and has the same magnitude. Their two slow cosines generate a square registry pattern. For small mismatch and small twist measured in radians the period is approximately \(a/\sqrt{\delta^2+\theta^2}\). The interference construction specifies a local continuum period. An exact common atomistic supercell would require additional commensurability conditions.

Interpret a long measured period

Take \(a=0.246\) nm, mismatch 1.3 percent and twist two degrees. In units of \(2\pi/a\) the horizontal family's difference vector is \((1-\cos\theta/(1+\delta),-\sin\theta/(1+\delta))\), approximately \((0.0134,-0.0345)\). Its length is about 0.0370, so the registry period reaches about 6.65 nm. Each layer still has a spacing near a quarter nanometre. The larger scale is the distance over which small phase differences accumulate.

If an image measures only this period and the first layer spacing, it supplies a single scalar for the two unknowns \(\delta\) and \(\theta\). A reciprocal-space measurement referenced to the first lattice supplies direction as well. In the simple isotropic model this resolves the rotated and rescaled vector by \(\mathbf G_2=\mathbf G_1-\Delta\mathbf G\). Additional distortions or strain require more directions before an inversion is possible.

Exercise · determine a twist from pure rotation

The lattice spacings have been verified equal at 0.246 nm. What is the magnitude of the twist if the registry spacing is 10.0 nm?

Work through the solution

With \(\delta=0\), the reciprocal vectors form an isosceles triangle and \(\Lambda=a/[2|\sin(\theta/2)|]\). The small positive solution is \(2\arcsin(0.246/20.0)=0.02460\) rad or 1.41 degrees. Changing the twist sign reflects the pattern without changing its period.

Exercise · make the same spacing with no twist

Use the same first-layer spacing and measured 10.0 nm period. What positive mismatch would explain the observation for aligned layers?

Work through the solution

At \(\theta=0\) and positive mismatch,

\[\Lambda=a(1+\delta)/\delta.\]

Solving gives \(\delta=a/(\Lambda-a)=0.02522\), about 2.52 percent. Comparing this result with the previous exercise shows explicitly why period alone cannot identify a twist.

Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 10. From bonding energy to lattice spacing and sound speeds

Diffraction gives a spacing and ultrasound gives a stiffness. One measures where an energy curve is stationary, the other measures its curvature. Building the simplest energy of an ionic solid gives both observables and shows which quantities a spring model has already assumed.

Decide which degrees of freedom contribute to binding

In an ionic crystal the leading energy is the electrostatic interaction of charged sublattices. In a covalent solid electron sharing favours specific bond directions, as in a tetrahedrally coordinated network. In a metal the mobile electronic states extend across the lattice and contribute an energy that depends on occupation and interatomic spacing. Closed-shell atoms can attract through fluctuating dipoles even when they have no permanent dipole. At small separations overlapping electronic clouds incur a rapidly rising energy. These mechanisms can coexist. No single pair potential reproduces every elastic or optical property.

For an alternating line of charges separated by \(r\), the electrostatic potential at a positive ion receives two contributions at each distance \(jr\). Summing nearest positive and negative shells in neutral groups gives the dimensionless lattice sum \(2(1-1/2+1/3-\cdots)=2\ln 2\). In three dimensions the corresponding Madelung constant depends on structure. Neutral summation or a convergent Ewald construction is needed for a Coulomb lattice. A naive rapidly growing box of uncompensated charges can give a spurious result.

Find the energy minimum and infer the short-range repulsion

For one formula unit of a 1:1 ionic lattice take \(\Phi(r)=-A/r+B/r^n\) with \(A=\alpha e^2/(4\pi\epsilon_0)\) and \(n>1\). Counting pair energy once gives this coefficient per monovalent ion pair. The stationarity condition at measured nearest-neighbour spacing \(r_0\) eliminates \(B\). The cohesion and curvature at the same point become

\[B=\frac{A r_0^{n-1}}{n},\]
\[\Phi(r_0)=-\frac{A}{r_0}\left(1-\frac1n\right),\]
\[\Phi''(r_0)=\frac{(n-1)A}{r_0^3}.\]

The energy zero here is separated ions. Getting the cohesive energy relative to separated neutral atoms additionally requires the ionisation and electron-affinity terms. For a rock-salt structure \(\alpha=1.7476\) and the volume per formula unit is \(v=2r^3\). At zero pressure \(K=v\,d^2\Phi/dv^2=v\Phi''/(dv/dr)^2\). Substitution gives

\[K=\frac{\Phi''(r_0)}{18r_0}=\frac{(n-1)A}{18r_0^4}.\]

For \(r_0=0.282\) nm, \(n=9\) and \(e^2/(4\pi\epsilon_0)=1.440\) eV nm, the model gives \(A=2.517\) eV nm, binding relative to separated ions of 7.93 eV per pair, and \(K\simeq28.3\) GPa. Inverting the curvature using an independently measured modulus tests the assumed repulsive exponent.

Recall · Radial curvature and a bulk modulus have different units

The curvature of one pair energy has units N/m. An elastic modulus has units N/m² and refers to energy per volume. The volume per formula unit and the density of bonds determine the conversion.

Resolve the stiffness tensor using three acoustic polarisations

For a crystal write \(\sigma_{ij}=C_{ijkl}\varepsilon_{kl}\). Substitute a displacement \(\mathbf u=\mathbf e\,e^{i(k\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf x-\omega t)}\) into \(\rho\ddot u_i=\partial_j\sigma_{ij}\). The strain symmetry gives a symmetric three-dimensional eigenproblem. Its eigenvectors give mode polarisations and its eigenvalues give measurable phase speeds.

\[\Gamma_{ik}e_k=\rho c^2e_i,\]
\[\Gamma_{ik}=C_{ijkl}n_j n_l .\]

Cubic symmetry leaves \(C_{11}, C_{12}, C_{44}\). Along [100] the eigenvalues are \(C_{11},C_{44},C_{44}\). Along [110] they are \((C_{11}+C_{12}+2C_{44})/2\), \((C_{11}-C_{12})/2\), and \(C_{44}\). Under hydrostatic load \(K=(C_{11}+2C_{12})/3\). A bulk modulus from ionic compression supplies just one combination. Shear-wave data probe independent curvature directions.

OneSimulation experiment · Three acoustic branches in a cubic crystal

Try a three-dimensional acoustic-speed surface

Rotate the 3D surface to compare propagation along an edge, a face diagonal and a body diagonal. Its radius in a direction is the selected phase speed in that direction. The 2D plot shows the three sorted eigenvalues in the (001) plane; the branch selector changes which one is drawn in 3D.

Set C44 to 51 GPa to satisfy C11 − C12 = 2C44. Predict the shape of the surface before moving the slider. Then multiply density by four and check the predicted factor of two in speed. C11 = 166 GPa and C12 = 64 GPa stay fixed. Every surface point solves the symmetric 3 × 3 Christoffel eigenproblem with a browser Jacobi iteration. The full-direction notebook below independently computes the entire surface with OneSimulation, including its [100], [110] and [111] cuts and an isotropic-limit check.

The surface gives phase velocity. In an anisotropic crystal the energy-flow direction can differ from the wave-normal direction, so this surface is not a point-source wavefront.

Open this experiment online

Full three-dimensional OneSimulation result

Download the executed 3D notebook · Download the parameterised 3D script. The script command above follows all three controls. Every surface vertex solves the Christoffel eigenproblem in its own propagation direction.

from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import symmetric_eigenvalues
# n is a unit propagation direction; stiffnesses are in GPa.
gamma = (64. + c44) * np.outer(n, n)
gamma += np.diag(c44 + (102. - 2*c44) * n**2)
velocity = np.sqrt(symmetric_eigenvalues(gamma) * 1e9 / density) / 1000
surface_point = velocity[branch] * n
[100] 5.859587 5.859587 8.440654 km/s
[110] 4.678505 5.859587 9.148278 km/s
[111] 5.102666 5.102666 9.372287 km/s
Isotropic directional invariance: verified

Reproduce with OneSimulation

Rotate the propagation direction without changing density or stiffness. Predict which two modes coincide along a cube edge and compare the computed [110] speeds with the three analytic eigenvalues. Then impose the isotropic condition.

Run the downloaded notebook with OneSimulation, NumPy and Matplotlib installed. The output below was recorded with the displayed calculation.

"""Sound velocities when a wave rotates through the (001) plane of a cubic solid."""
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import symmetric_eigenvalues

c11, c12, c44 = np.array([166, 64, 80]) * 1e9  # Pa
density = 2330.0  # kg/m^3

def speeds(angle):
    n = np.array([np.cos(angle), np.sin(angle), 0])
    gamma = (c12+c44) * np.outer(n, n)
    gamma += np.diag(c44 + (c11-c12-2*c44)*n**2)
    return np.sqrt(symmetric_eigenvalues(gamma)/density)

angle = np.linspace(0, np.pi/2, 181)
velocity = np.array([speeds(a) for a in angle]) / 1000
print("Wave speeds in km/s, sorted by magnitude")
for label, a in [("[100]", 0), ("[110]", np.pi/4)]:
    print(label, " ".join(f"{v/1000:.3f}" for v in speeds(a)))
for branch, label in enumerate(["Slow shear", "Fast shear", "Quasi-longitudinal"]):
    plt.plot(np.degrees(angle), velocity[:,branch], label=label)
plt.xlabel("Direction from [100] toward [010] (degrees)")
plt.ylabel("Sound speed (km/s)")
plt.legend()
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig("physics-cubic-sound.png", dpi=160)

Computed output

Wave speeds in km/s, sorted by magnitude
[100] 5.860 5.860 8.441
[110] 4.679 5.860 9.148

Tutorial on identifying a binding law

1 · Recognise a false equilibrium

Work through the solution

With only \(-A/r\) the energy falls monotonically as ions approach. A measured lattice spacing and a real sound speed require an energy minimum with positive curvature. Attraction alone supplies neither.

2 · Recover one modulus from a transverse mode

Work through the solution

At density 2330 kg/m³ an out-of-plane shear mode along [110] travels at 5.86 km/s. Hence \(C_{44}=\rho c^2\simeq80.0\) GPa. The slower in-plane shear mode instead measures \((C_{11}-C_{12})/2\).

3 · Decide whether an isotropic model could fit both shear waves

Work through the solution

Cubic shear degeneracy for all directions requires \(C_{11}-C_{12}=2C_{44}\). The notebook uses 102 versus 160 GPa, so a scalar shear modulus cannot fit both [110] speeds. Alter \(C_{44}\) to 51 GPa and predict the angular plot before rerunning.

For further study see Kittel, Introduction to Solid State Physics, 8th edition, chapters 1–5 on crystal structure, binding, elastic constants and acoustic lattice modes.

Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 11. Collective charge oscillations, bound excitons and lattice-dressed electrons

A loss peak in an electron spectrum, a line below an optical gap and an enhanced carrier mass call for different kinds of excitations. Start each model with its moving charges and its restoring interaction. That identifies what a measurement can determine.

Displace an electron fluid against its background

Consider a wide slab with carrier number density \(n\), band mass \(m^*\) and immobile neutralising positive background. Displace the electron cloud uniformly through a small distance \(u\) normal to the faces. The opposite surface charges have magnitude \(ne u\) per area and generate a restoring field \(E=ne u/(\epsilon_0\epsilon_\infty)\) inside. The factor \(\epsilon_\infty\) accounts for faster electronic degrees of freedom excluded from the carrier model.

\[m^*\ddot u=-\frac{ne^2}{\epsilon_0\epsilon_\infty}u,\]
\[\omega_p^2=\frac{ne^2}{m^*\epsilon_0\epsilon_\infty}.\]

In a homogeneous bulk the same long-wavelength mode follows from continuity, carrier momentum and Gauss's law for a longitudinal density fluctuation. A longitudinal self-sustaining oscillation occurs at a zero of the dielectric function. With damping time \(\tau\) the relative permittivity in this model is \(\epsilon(\omega)=\epsilon_\infty[1-\omega_p^2/(\omega(\omega+i/\tau))]\) for \(e^{-i\omega t}\). Electron energy loss is sensitive to \(-\mathrm{Im}(1/\epsilon)\), while transmission also depends on interfaces.

For \(n=10^{24}\) m⁻³, \(m^*=0.20m_e\) and \(\epsilon_\infty=12\), the model gives \(\omega_p\simeq3.64\times10^{13}\) rad/s, or 5.80 THz. Four times the density doubles this frequency if mass and background screening are unchanged.

Solve an effective electron–hole relative-coordinate problem

In a three-dimensional direct-gap semiconductor a photon can create a conduction electron and a valence hole. Separate their centre of mass from their relative coordinate. Near parabolic band edges the relative mass obeys \(\mu^{-1}=1/m_e^*+1/m_h^*\). Approximate screening by a constant \(\epsilon_r\) over the length and time scales of the pair. The relative Hamiltonian is then hydrogenic.

\[H_{\rm rel}=-\frac{\hbar^2\nabla^2}{2\mu}-\frac{e^2}{4\pi\epsilon_0\epsilon_r r},\]
\[E_n=E_g-\frac{R^*}{n^2}.\]

Minimising the expectation for an exponential radius \(a\) gives kinetic energy \(\hbar^2/(2\mu a^2)\) and potential \(-e^2/(4\pi\epsilon_0\epsilon_r a)\). The minimum yields \(a^*=(\epsilon_r m_e/\mu)a_0\) and \(R^*=(\mu/m_e)13.606\,{\rm eV}/\epsilon_r^2\). With \(m_e^*=0.20m_e\), \(m_h^*=0.50m_e\), \(\epsilon_r=10\), these give \(\mu=0.1429m_e\), \(a^*=3.70\) nm and \(R^*=19.4\) meV. The most strongly bound line lies this distance below the unbound continuum. A large radius relative to the unit cell supports the effective-mass model. A sub-gap peak alone can also come from a defect.

Try it · Exciton

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Open this live calculation

Let a single carrier relax a lattice coordinate

Take a local displacement \(Q_i\), stiffness \(K\), and local occupation \(n_i\). Each site's lattice energy and coupling are \(KQ_i^2/2-g Q_i n_i\). At a fixed occupation distribution minimise over each displacement. In the adiabatic classical distortion model,

\[Q_i=g n_i/K,\]
\[E_{\rm relax}=-\frac{g^2}{2K}\sum_i n_i^2 .\]

A carrier with all its weight on one site gains \(g^2/(2K)\). With uniform weight across \(N\) sites the gain is smaller by \(N\). Hopping competes with this localisation energy. For \(g=2.0\) eV/nm and \(K=20\) eV/nm² the one-site relaxation is 0.10 eV and the optimum displacement 0.10 nm. Compare with the bandwidth before concluding that a small polaron will form. Quantised phonons, tunnelling of the distortion and long-range polar coupling are additional physics.

Recall · Screening has a frequency scale

A bound pair and a fast plasma oscillation need not see the same dielectric constant. Identify which lattice and electronic modes can follow each perturbation before importing a tabulated static permittivity.

Tutorial on assigning a measured feature

1 · Extract an exciton binding scale from two lines

Work through the solution

In the hydrogenic series \(E_2-E_1=3R^*/4\). A splitting of 12 meV gives \(R^*=16\) meV and continuum \(E_g=E_1+16\) meV. Check another member or the absorption onset to distinguish an accidental pair of defect peaks.

2 · Separate two density dependencies

Work through the solution

A mode increasing from 5.8 to 8.2 THz as the carrier density doubles follows the plasmon square-root trend. The simple isolated exciton model has no such density law. At high density screening or many-body effects can also modify the exciton spectrum.

3 · Test the localisation argument

Work through the solution

For equal occupation of two sites the distortion energy gain is half the one-site gain. A two-site bonding state can lower the electronic kinetic energy by a hopping energy \(t\). If \(t\) is large compared with the difference in relaxation energies, localisation cannot be inferred from the static distortion gain alone.

For further study see Kittel, Introduction to Solid State Physics, 8th edition, chapters 14–15 on collective excitations, polarons and optical response.

Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 12. Ferromagnetic resonance from angular momentum and free-energy curvature

A uniformly magnetised thin specimen can absorb microwave power at a frequency far from the bare spin Larmor frequency. The precessing magnetic moment creates its own field. Its geometry and the anisotropy determine which restoring torques appear in the measured resonance.

Linearise the torque around the equilibrium orientation

Consider a saturated, uniformly magnetised ellipsoid with equilibrium along \(x\). Write the dimensionless unit magnetisation as \(\mathbf m\simeq(1,m_y,m_z)\). To second order use a free energy density \(F=F_0+M_s(B_y m_y^2+B_z m_z^2)/2\) with both curvature fields positive in tesla. They include bias, demagnetising fields and anisotropy. A small applied microwave field \(b_y(t)\) adds \(-M_s b_y m_y\). Define the effective field by \(-\partial F/\partial\mathbf m\) divided by \(M_s\).

\[\dot{\mathbf m}=-\gamma\,\mathbf m\times\mathbf B_{\rm eff},\]
\[\dot m_y=-\gamma B_z m_z,\]
\[\dot m_z=\gamma B_y m_y-\gamma b_y .\]

Differentiating the first equation and eliminating \(m_z\) gives \(\ddot m_y+\gamma^2B_y B_z m_y=\gamma^2 B_z b_y\). The free precession frequency is \(\omega_0=\gamma\sqrt{B_yB_z}\). The orbit generally has unequal semiaxes. In the limit of equal curvature fields the circular bare-spin result is recovered.

Obtain the thin-film frequency in an in-plane field

In a wide thin film, uniform in-plane rotation creates little demagnetising field. An out-of-plane tilt costs approximately \(\mu_0 M_s^2m_z^2/2\). A bias \(B_0\) contributes \(-M_sB_0m_x\simeq-M_sB_0+M_sB_0(m_y^2+m_z^2)/2\). Neglect crystalline anisotropy and edge corrections. Thus \(B_y=B_0\) and \(B_z=B_0+\mu_0 M_s\). With \(\gamma/(2\pi)=28\) GHz/T, \(\mu_0M_s=1.00\) T and \(B_0=0.10\) T we predict \(f_0=9.29\) GHz. Using \(28B_0\) alone would give 2.80 GHz.

Recall · Compare B-like and H-like parameters consistently

In these formulas all stiffness fields are in tesla and \(\gamma\) is in radians per second per tesla. If a field is in A/m it must first be converted where appropriate with \(\mu_0\). The static demagnetising field depends on shape as well as material.

Derive the absorbed-power peak and its width

Gilbert damping adds \(+\alpha\mathbf m\times\dot{\mathbf m}\) to the torque equation. To first order in small \(\alpha\) one obtains the damped denominator \(\omega_0^2-\omega^2-i\alpha\gamma(B_y+B_z)\omega\). With convention \(e^{-i\omega t}\) the average loss from a real drive of complex amplitude \(b\) is proportional to \(\omega\,\operatorname{Im}(\chi)|b|^2/2\). Near resonance the absorptive susceptibility has a Lorentzian line shape. Its angular-frequency full width at half maximum to this order is

\[\Delta\omega_{\rm FWHM}\simeq\alpha\gamma(B_y+B_z).\]

For the model film at \(B_0=0.10\) T and \(\alpha=0.010\) the frequency-swept width is about 0.336 GHz. A field-swept experiment must divide the frequency width by \(|df_0/dB_0|\) and distinguish the width of the absorption peak from a derivative peak-to-peak linewidth. Inhomogeneous fields and mode overlap broaden real lines as well.

Tutorial on extracting magnetic parameters

1 · Recover a saturation magnetisation

Work through the solution

For an in-plane film at 0.12 T, a uniform resonance at 10.0 GHz gives \(\mu_0M_s=(10/28)^2/0.12-0.12=0.943\) T in this approximation. Repeating the measurement at several fields tests the assumed geometry.

2 · Infer an intrinsic damping estimate

Work through the solution

For curvature fields 0.20 and 1.20 T and a 0.392 GHz full width, \(\alpha\simeq0.392/[28(1.40)]=0.0100\). A field-independent broadening that also contributes to the observed width would bias this single-frequency estimate upward.

3 · Explain a soft mode

Work through the solution

As one curvature approaches zero at a loss of equilibrium stability, the predicted restoring frequency approaches zero while the other curvature can remain finite. A broad or shifted low-frequency peak calls for checking domains and the validity of the uniform-magnetisation assumption.

For further study see Kittel, Introduction to Solid State Physics, 8th edition, chapters 11–13 on collective magnetic response and magnetic resonance.

Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 13. Lattice dislocations and structure without long-range order

A missing atom, a slipped lattice plane and a glass can all broaden a measurement, through very different mechanisms. Geometry distinguishes a line defect from a local vacancy. Correlation functions make the distinction between short-range order and crystallinity quantitative.

Construct a Burgers circuit and the field of a screw dislocation

Follow a closed sequence of lattice steps around a suspected defect, well outside its core. Repeat the same sequence in a perfect reference lattice. The closure failure defines the Burgers vector \(\mathbf b\) up to the agreed sense of the circuit and line orientation. Its component parallel to the dislocation line is screw character and its perpendicular component is edge character. Smooth elastic deformation without a dislocation gives no persistent closure failure.

For a straight screw line along \(z\), with Burgers magnitude \(b\) along \(z\), an elastic displacement outside the core is \(u_z=b\phi/(2\pi)\) in cylindrical coordinates. After one revolution the displacement changes by \(b\). The engineering shear is \(\gamma_{\phi z}=r^{-1}\partial_\phi u_z=b/(2\pi r)\), giving \(\sigma_{\phi z}=\mu b/(2\pi r)\). Integrating the energy density \(\mu\gamma^2/2\) in an annulus gives an energy per unit line length

\[\frac{E}{L}=\int_{r_c}^{R}\frac{\mu}{2}\left(\frac{b}{2\pi r}\right)^2 2\pi r\,dr=\frac{\mu b^2}{4\pi}\ln(R/r_c).\]

The core radius limits continuum elasticity at small radius. Nearby defects or surfaces set the outer cutoff. For \(\mu=30\) GPa, \(b=0.25\) nm and \(R/r_c=10^4\), the elastic term is \(1.37\times10^{-9}\) J/m. A core energy must be added to infer formation energies.

Determine how an applied shear does work on a line

A dislocation of length \(L\) moving a distance \(dx\) sweeps area \(Ldx\). Across the swept area the material slips by \(b\). A resolved shear stress \(\tau\) supplies work \(\tau b Ldx\), hence the glide force per unit length is \(\tau b\). At \(\tau=20\) MPa and \(b=0.25\) nm this gives \(5.0\times10^{-3}\) N/m. Obstacles and other dislocations oppose this driving force. Diffusion-assisted climb exchanges atoms or vacancies and depends on temperature in a different way.

Recall · Edge dislocations affect the number of planes

In an edge geometry the terminating extra half-plane ends at the dislocation line. A vacancy has no Burgers vector around a sufficiently large circuit. Concentration alone cannot describe a line's orientation, connectivity and mobility.

Recover pair order from scattering without indexing Bragg peaks

For particles of mean number density \(\rho\), define \(g(r)\) by the conditional count \(dN=4\pi r^2\rho g(r)\,dr\) about an atom in an isotropic bulk sample. The first-shell coordination is the integral from the first-neighbour peak to its first minimum, including the correct \(r^2\) weight. Scattering measures amplitudes before averaging the squared total. After averaging orientations the pair phase factor becomes \(\sin(qr)/(qr)\).

\[S(q)=1+4\pi\rho\int_0^\infty [g(r)-1]\,\frac{\sin(qr)}{qr}\,r^2dr .\]

A glass may have a sharp first-neighbour peak and further damped shells with \(g(r)\to1\). This permits strong local coordination without periodic translational order. An ideal crystal produces Bragg peaks in reciprocal space. A limited sample size or instrumental resolution also broadens crystalline peaks. Compare several peak widths and a real-space correlation length before assigning amorphous structure.

A glass transition is observed when a structural relaxation time becomes comparable to a measurement or cooling time. A slower scan permits structural rearrangement to lower temperatures. The measured transition temperature may therefore shift with rate. The sharp equilibrium melting point of a pure crystal and the observed glass-transition interval answer different experimental questions.

Tutorial on disorder measurements

1 · Identify a dislocation character

Work through the solution

A line runs along \(z\) while the closure failure points along \(x\). Its screw projection is zero, so the line is pure edge. An inclined segment on a curved line generally has mixed character even with the same conserved Burgers vector.

2 · Estimate a coordination number

Work through the solution

Approximate a first shell by \(g=3\) between 0.23 and 0.27 nm with \(\rho=60\) nm⁻³ and zero elsewhere within that shell. Then \(N=4\pi\rho[0.27^3-0.23^3]\simeq5.67\). Truncating below the first minimum or failing to subtract scattering backgrounds changes the estimate.

3 · Separate line broadening from amorphous order

Work through the solution

A single broad powder maximum is insufficient. Examine whether higher peaks can be indexed on a consistent lattice, vary instrumental resolution, and estimate the range of oscillatory pair correlations. Compare thermal evolution and cooling-rate dependence for independent evidence.

For further study see Kittel, Introduction to Solid State Physics, 8th edition, chapters 19–21 on amorphous structure, point defects and dislocations.

Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 14. Why some alloys demix while others order on two sublattices

A binary alloy can cease to look random by separating into compositionally distinct regions or by arranging unlike atoms on alternating sites. A free energy for composition answers the first question. A second order parameter is needed for the second.

Count configurations and derive an energy of mixing

Let \(N\) lattice sites contain \(Nx\) atoms of B. The number of random arrangements is \(N!/[(Nx)!(N(1-x))!]\). Stirling's approximation gives a configurational entropy per site \(-k_B[x\ln x+(1-x)\ln(1-x)]\). In a random alloy each of \(zN/2\) nearest-neighbour bonds is unlike with probability \(2x(1-x)\). With one bond energy \(\epsilon_{ij}\) per pair, subtract the energies of separated pure phases at the same overall composition.

\[\Omega=z\left[\epsilon_{AB}-(\epsilon_{AA}+\epsilon_{BB})/2\right],\]
\[\Delta g=\Omega x(1-x)+k_BT[x\ln x+(1-x)\ln(1-x)].\]

For \(\Omega>0\), unlike neighbours cost energy. The curvature at fixed temperature is \(g''=-2\Omega+k_BT/[x(1-x)]\). A homogeneous state is locally unstable when this curvature is negative. Equal chemical potentials across coexisting phases give a common tangent to \(g(x)\). For the symmetric model \(x_2=1-x_1\), the tangent is horizontal and the binodal obeys \(\Omega(1-2x)+k_BT\ln[x/(1-x)]=0\).

At \(x=1/2\) the spinodal reaches its highest temperature \(T_c=\Omega/(2k_B)\). For \(\Omega=0.10\) eV the mean-field \(T_c\) is 580 K. At 400 K the spinodal satisfies \(x(1-x)=0.1723\), or \(x\simeq0.221\) and \(0.779\). A composition between the binodal and spinodal can still have a positive local curvature while phase separation would lower the total energy after nucleation.

Try it · Regular solution

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Open this live calculation

Construct an order parameter for preferential unlike neighbours

For an equiatomic alloy on a bipartite lattice let the A fractions of the two sublattices be \((1+\eta)/2\) and \((1-\eta)/2\). Then \(\eta=0\) is a random occupation and \(|\eta|=1\) is perfect order. Assuming independent occupation within each sublattice, the probability of an unlike nearest-neighbour bond is \((1+\eta^2)/2\). Put \(w=\epsilon_{AB}-(\epsilon_{AA}+\epsilon_{BB})/2\). The energy change from the random state is \(zw\eta^2/4\). Adding both sublattice mixing entropies gives the leading free-energy terms

\[g(\eta)-g(0)=\left(\frac{zw}{4}+\frac{k_BT}{2}\right)\eta^2+\frac{k_BT}{12}\eta^4+O(\eta^6).\]

For \(w<0\) the energetic gain favours order below \(T_{\rm ord}=-zw/(2k_B)\). Domains can choose opposite signs and have the same composition. A superlattice diffraction reflection weights the difference of sublattice scattering amplitudes. Its amplitude is proportional to \((f_A-f_B)\eta\), so its ideal intensity scales as \(\eta^2\). A measurement of the average composition alone overlooks the transition.

Recall · What makes this a mean-field model?

The product of single-site probabilities omits short-range correlations between neighbours. Clustering can therefore precede macroscopic separation and diffuse scattering can precede a sharp superlattice peak. Long-range elastic effects, unequal sublattice sizes or composition-dependent strain modify these predictions.

Distinguish an equilibrium phase boundary from slow atomic exchange

Composition changes by transport, which conserves the total fraction of each species. In a weak composition gradient a mobility \(M>0\) gives a flux proportional to \(-\nabla\mu\) with \(\mu=\partial g/\partial x\) in a locally uniform approximation. Negative curvature can drive atoms uphill in composition, amplifying fluctuations. A positive interfacial penalty proportional to \(|\nabla x|^2\) prevents indefinitely fine segregation. In ordering at fixed composition, local exchanges can develop alternating occupation without transporting a macroscopic excess from one end of a specimen to the other.

Tutorial on alloy diffraction and stability

1 · Determine the sign of the interaction

Work through the solution

If unlike bonds are 10 meV lower than the average like bond and coordination is eight, \(w=-0.010\) eV gives a mean-field ordering temperature of \(0.080/(2k_B)=464\) K on a suitable bipartite lattice. Demixing is disfavoured in this nearest-neighbour calculation.

2 · Infer an order parameter

Work through the solution

A superlattice reflection has one quarter of its intensity in a perfectly ordered single domain after correcting for geometry and thermal motion. The inferred \(|\eta|\) is 0.5. Unequal domain volumes or poor scattering contrast demand a more complete intensity model.

3 · Diagnose two microstructures with similar average composition

Work through the solution

In an equiatomic sample, composition mapping that resolves B-rich and A-rich regions indicates separation. Alternating-site superlattice peaks at essentially uniform local composition indicate ordering. Simultaneous data help when both mechanisms occur on different length scales.

For further study see Kittel, Introduction to Solid State Physics, 8th edition, chapter 22 on alloys and ordered structures.

Structure, excitations and probes of solids · 15. What extremal Fermi-surface orbits reveal about a metal

A single magnetic oscillation frequency gives one area in reciprocal space. Many different surfaces can share that area. Rotating the field and following how quantum oscillations and conductivity change turns the measurement into a three-dimensional inference problem.

Derive the plane of motion and the measured cross section

For semiclassical electrons of band energy \(\epsilon(\mathbf k)\) in a uniform static magnetic field with zero applied electric field, \(\dot{\mathbf r}=\nabla_{\mathbf k}\epsilon/\hbar\) and \(\hbar\dot{\mathbf k}=-e\dot{\mathbf r}\times\mathbf B\). Dotting the second equation with \(\mathbf B\) shows that the orbit stays in a fixed plane perpendicular to the field. Dotting with \(\nabla_{\mathbf k}\epsilon\) also shows that it remains on a constant-energy surface. A degenerate metal probes the intersection of these two surfaces near the Fermi level.

For a closed orbit of area \(A(E,k_\parallel)\) the semiclassical area quantisation condition is \(A=2\pi eB(n+\gamma)/\hbar\). At fixed Fermi energy adjacent crossings at increasing \(1/B\) then have spacing \(2\pi e/(\hbar A)\). Bulk signals sum oscillations from many neighbouring planes. Rapid phase variation cancels most contributions on averaging, leaving extrema of \(A\) along \(k_\parallel\) particularly visible.

\[F=\frac{\hbar}{2\pi e} A_{\rm ext},\]
\[m_c=\frac{\hbar^2}{2\pi}\frac{\partial A}{\partial E}\bigg|_{\rm orbit}.\]

The second relation follows by differentiating the area quantisation with respect to energy and comparing successive-level spacing to \(\hbar eB/m_c\). A measured area does not by itself give its energy derivative. Temperature damping or an independent mass measurement contains additional information.

Predict the angular dependence for two competing surfaces

For an ellipsoid of semiaxes \(a,b,c\) in wave-number units, a linear scaling from a unit sphere gives a central section normal to \(\mathbf n\) of area \(\pi abc/\sqrt{a^2n_x^2+b^2n_y^2+c^2n_z^2}\). One way to see the denominator is to take a thin slab bounded by parallel planes. Under the inverse scaling the distance between the transformed planes changes by the inverse magnitude of the scaled normal. Equating volume before and after the transformation fixes the area factor.

For an axially symmetric ellipsoid take \(a=b\) and tilt the field through angle \(\theta\) from \(z\). A circular cylinder of radius \(a\) instead has section area \(\pi a^2/|\cos\theta|\) so long as a closed orbit exists. At normal incidence both predict the same frequency. For \(c=2a\), the ellipsoid predicts \(F(60^\circ)/F(0)=2/\sqrt{0.75+1}=1.512\), while the cylinder predicts 2. An ellipsoid closes with a finite side-on area. A perfectly unwarped cylinder has open trajectories for a field in its plane.

Recall · Open orbits in a periodic crystal

In a reduced-zone drawing a trajectory can reappear at the opposite zone edge. Unfold the reciprocal lattice to see whether it closes or runs through successive zones. Such open orbits do not give an ordinary closed-area oscillation frequency and can strongly affect anisotropic transport.

Try it · Fermi area

Allow necks, multiple sheets and unequal visibility

A weakly warped quasi-two-dimensional sheet can have a belly and a neck. Model their areas as \(A_0(1+\eta)\) and \(A_0(1-\eta)\) for field along the layer normal. Then \(F_0=(F_b+F_n)/2\) and \(\eta=(F_b-F_n)/(F_b+F_n)\). For 500 and 440 tesla, these give 470 tesla and a relative corrugation of 0.0638. With similar amplitudes the sum of the two oscillations contains an envelope \(\cos[\pi(F_b-F_n)/B]\). The spacing of successive envelope nodes in inverse field is \(1/|F_b-F_n|\).

Several frequencies can instead come from different electron and hole pockets. Measure the angular branches continuously and compare their effective masses, crystal symmetry and independent Hall and transport data. A heavy or strongly scattered orbit may be too weak to see. Assigning the entire carrier density to the most visible frequency can miss whole sheets. For an isotropic band with a spherical spin-degenerate pocket, the additional geometric assumption gives \(n=k_F^3/(3\pi^2)\). Its boundary should be stated when reporting a density.

Tutorial on competing metal models

1 · Convert an oscillation frequency to an area

Work through the solution

At \(F=500\) T, \(A=(2\pi e/\hbar)F=4.77\times10^{18}\) m⁻². If the orbit is circular the radius is \(\sqrt{A/\pi}=1.23\times10^9\) m⁻¹. The first number follows without assuming a circular shape.

2 · Estimate a beat period

Work through the solution

With the two measured frequencies separated by 60 T, successive envelope zeros are separated by 0.0167 T⁻¹. A recording window of only 0.005 T⁻¹ cannot resolve the splitting convincingly. Vary the inverse-field window and check harmonic peaks.

3 · Infer an ellipsoid axis ratio from a side-on rotation

Work through the solution

For \(a=b\) the side-on to normal frequency ratio of a closed ellipsoid is \(c/a\). A ratio 1.7 implies \(c/a=1.7\). If the signal disappears before reaching side-on, the missing end point leaves this inference underdetermined. Seek independent sections or a second rotation plane.

For further study see Kittel, Introduction to Solid State Physics, 8th edition, chapters 6–9 on electrons in metals, band structure and Fermi surfaces.

From microscopic moments to measured magnetisation

Magnetism, induction and field dynamics

A magnetised sample can make a compass turn without a power supply attached. The macroscopic theory assigns it bound currents in its bulk and on its surface. These are calculated sources for the magnetic field. We will reconstruct them from magnetic moments, predict signals from finite samples, and ask what an exterior field measurement can identify.

Define a material field at an intermediate scale

Choose a volume large enough to average over microscopic moments and small enough to resolve the sample's macroscopic variation. Let \(\boldsymbol M(\boldsymbol r)\) be the vector sum of moments within it divided by its volume. Its units are \(\mathrm{A/m}\). A permanent aligned collection may maintain \(\boldsymbol M\) without an applied field. An induced collection may point with or against the applied field. Electric charges need not traverse the entire sample in a closed macroscopic wire for either mechanism. Electron orbital moments and spin contribute to the measured moment; for a classical exterior field calculation any small magnetic dipole has the same leading field once its moment is specified.

A bar of volume \(8.0\,\mathrm{cm^3}\) with uniform \(M=2.0\times10^4\,\mathrm{A/m}\) has total moment \(m=0.160\,\mathrm{A\,m^2}\). A torsion balance in a uniform \(10\,\mathrm{mT}\) transverse field would measure a maximum torque \(1.60\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{N\,m}\) if its moment stayed fixed. Dividing a measured torque by field and sample volume infers the component normal to the external field. Alignment of a previously soft material could instead alter the very moment being measured. Distinguishing fixed-source and induced-response experiments will matter when we calculate mechanical energy.

Derive the bound sources by integration by parts

Sum the dipole vector potentials over a material region \(V\). With observation point outside the matter, a volume element contributes \((\mu_0/4\pi)\boldsymbol M(\boldsymbol r')\times(\boldsymbol r-\boldsymbol r')dV'/R^3\), where \(R=|\boldsymbol r-\boldsymbol r'|\). Note that \(\nabla'(1/R)=(\boldsymbol r-\boldsymbol r')/R^3\). Apply the identity \(\nabla'\times(\boldsymbol M/R)=(\nabla'1/R)\times\boldsymbol M+(\nabla'\times\boldsymbol M)/R\). The desired first cross product is minus the first term on the right. A volume integral of a curl is the surface integral of outward normal crossed with the vector. This yields

\[\boldsymbol A=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\left[\int_V\frac{\nabla'\times\boldsymbol M}{R}\,dV'+\oint_{\partial V}\frac{\boldsymbol M\times\boldsymbol n'}{R}\,dA'\right].\]

Comparing with the ordinary current potential identifies \(\boldsymbol J_b=\nabla\times\boldsymbol M\), in A/m², and \(\boldsymbol K_b=\boldsymbol M\times\boldsymbol n\), in A/m. The first can vanish throughout a uniformly magnetised object, while the second closes around its exterior. A literal set of microscopic loops illustrates the sign. Neighbouring loop-edge currents cancel between equal adjacent moments, whereas their edges on the outer boundary remain uncompensated. When the moments vary slowly, interior cancellation is incomplete and yields the curl.

An alternative treatment extends \(\boldsymbol M\) by zero outside. Its distributional curl already includes the surface sheet at the discontinuity. Use either this full derivative or the separate bulk and surface integrals; including both descriptions simultaneously doubles the boundary source. At an internal interface with chosen normal from material 1 to 2 the two surface contributions combine to \((\boldsymbol M_1-\boldsymbol M_2)\times\boldsymbol n\). This expression is unchanged by interchange of the two labels accompanied by reversal of the normal.

Work through a uniformly magnetised cylinder

Let a finite circular cylinder of radius \(a\) and length \(2l\) have \(\boldsymbol M=M\hat{\boldsymbol z}\). On the curved side \(\hat{\boldsymbol z}\times\hat{\boldsymbol s}=\hat{\boldsymbol\varphi}\), hence \(K_\varphi=M\); on its end faces the cross product vanishes. The cylinder is equivalent, for its magnetic field, to a finite solenoid having turn-current per unit length \(M\). A strip of sidewall of height \(dz'\) carries ring current \(M\,dz'\). Insert this into the axial finite-loop field from the first chapter and integrate from \(-l\) to \(l\).

\[B_z(z)=\frac{\mu_0 M}{2}\left[\frac{z+l}{\sqrt{a^2+(z+l)^2}}-\frac{z-l}{\sqrt{a^2+(z-l)^2}}\right] .\]

At the centre \(B_z=\mu_0M l/\sqrt{a^2+l^2}\). For \(a=10\,\mathrm{mm}, l=20\,\mathrm{mm}\) and \(M=1.0\times10^4\,\mathrm{A/m}\), the value is \(11.24\,\mathrm{mT}\), below the long-cylinder limit \(12.57\,\mathrm{mT}\). At the end centre \(z=l\), only the first term contributes, giving \(6.10\,\mathrm{mT}\). A probe just outside the end and one just inside see the same normal \(\boldsymbol B\) in the limit, even though the auxiliary normal \(\boldsymbol H\) can jump because \(\boldsymbol M\) ends abruptly. The field near an edge is not uniform and can vary strongly with the sensor's position. Stating the axis and position is therefore essential when inferring magnetisation from a reading.

Use a nonuniform sample to separate bulk and surface effects

For an infinitely long cylinder set \(\boldsymbol M=M_0(s/a)\hat{\boldsymbol\varphi}\) for \(s<a\), zero outside. The cylindrical curl gives \(J_{b,z}=s^{-1}\partial_s(sM_\varphi)=2M_0/a\) in the bulk. The side normal is \(\hat{\boldsymbol s}\), so \(K_{b,z}=-M_0\). Integrated over a cross section the bulk current is \(2\pi aM_0\); the axial sheet supplies \(-2\pi aM_0\). Their cancellation means an exterior Amperian circle sees zero enclosed current. Its zero field follows from symmetry and the absence of externally imposed fields.

Inside, a circle at \(s\) encloses \(2M_0\pi s^2/a\). Dividing its circulation by \(2\pi s\) gives

\[B_\varphi=\mu_0M_0s/a.\]

Thus \(\boldsymbol H=\boldsymbol B/\mu_0-\boldsymbol M=0\) both inside and outside here, despite nonzero interior \(\boldsymbol B\). For \(a=20\,\mathrm{mm},M_0=500\,\mathrm{A/m}\), the bulk current density is \(5.0\times10^4\,\mathrm{A/m^2}\), total bulk current \(62.8\,\mathrm A\), and field just inside the rim \(0.628\,\mathrm{mT}\). A magnetometer outside reports zero within this ideal geometry. Such a null measurement establishes neither zero \(\boldsymbol M\) nor absence of all microscopic moments.

Compare a field map and a pickup-coil measurement

A stationary inductive pickup near a stationary permanent magnet has no emf from that static flux alone. An experiment can move the sample, rotate it or modulate a known magnetising field to produce \(-N\,d\Phi/dt\). A Hall sensor or calibrated magnetometer can instead respond to a static field. To compare methods, place a small pickup near the axis of the preceding finite cylinder and oscillate its axial position as \(z=z_0+\zeta\cos\omega t\). For \(\zeta\) small, the flux per turn is approximately \(A_p[B_z(z_0)+\zeta B'_z(z_0)\cos\omega t]\). The voltage amplitude is \(N A_p\zeta\omega |B'_z(z_0)|\). It measures a gradient multiplied by displacement amplitude, whereas a static Hall value measures the field itself.

At the symmetric cylinder centre \(B'_z(0)=0\), so the first harmonic vanishes even though the field is strong. Expanding one more order gives a constant shift and a second harmonic proportional to \(B''_z(0)\zeta^2\), since \(\cos^2\omega t=(1+\cos2\omega t)/2\). Finding a very small first harmonic at the centre is therefore a check on positioning and symmetry. It is not a calibration of zero magnetisation. Far from the ends, a measurement of the whole sample's dipole field instead yields \(\int\boldsymbol M\,dV\). Different internal distributions can share that integral while producing different nearer fields. Report the geometry, the actual observable and the assumed uniformity whenever quoting a single recovered value of \(M\).

Examine bound currents and field measurements in a magnetised sample

Use fictitious poles to compute a measurable field

There is a second useful representation of a given magnetisation. In a simply connected region without free current, \(\nabla\times\boldsymbol H=0\), so choose \(\boldsymbol H=-\nabla\psi\). Substitution into \(\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol H+\boldsymbol M)=0\) gives

\[\nabla^2\psi=\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol M.\]

The potential therefore behaves mathematically as if it were produced by volume density \(\rho_m=-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol M\) and surface density \(\sigma_m=\boldsymbol M\cdot\boldsymbol n\). The surface term follows by extending the magnetisation by zero across the boundary, or by requiring continuity of \(B_n=\mu_0(H_n+M_n)\). Integrate these densities with kernel \(1/(4\pi R)\) to obtain \(\psi\); there is no factor of \(\epsilon_0^{-1}\) because these conventions assign \(\psi\) units of amperes. The resulting \(\boldsymbol H\) must be converted back to \(\boldsymbol B\), adding \(\mu_0\boldsymbol M\) inside matter.

For the uniformly magnetised finite cylinder the pole representation puts densities \(+M\) and \(-M\) on the two end faces, none on the sides. Earlier the bound-current representation placed current on the curved side and none on the ends. They predict the same physical field. A single uniformly loaded disk of pole density \(\sigma_m\) at \(z=0\) has on-axis scalar potential \(\psi=\sigma_m[\sqrt{z^2+a^2}-|z|]/2\), obtained by integrating \(2\pi s\,ds/[4\pi\sqrt{s^2+z^2}]\). Its derivative gives

\[H_z=(\sigma_m/2)[\operatorname{sgn}(z)-z/\sqrt{z^2+a^2}].\]

At the disk plane the two one-sided fields jump by \(\sigma_m\), precisely the normal-\(H\) jump demanded by the magnetisation.

At the midpoint of the cylinder add the two oppositely charged disks. Their axial fields both point opposite the magnetisation and total \(H_z=-M[1-l/\sqrt{a^2+l^2}]\). Adding \(M\) yields the same \(B_z\) as the side-current integral. At \(l=2a\), this internal demagnetising field is \(-0.1056M\); for a very short cylinder \(l\ll a\) it approaches \(-M\). A uniformly specified magnetisation in a thin disc therefore produces only a small normal \(B\) at its centre despite the large \(M\) and \(H\) terms that nearly cancel. In practice, a soft magnetic material in such a shape may choose a nonuniform magnetisation or domains. The calculation describes the field of the imposed distribution; it does not establish that the distribution is a stable material state.

The 'poles' are convenient mathematical source terms, not isolated magnetic charges: adding the effective volume and surface poles over a finite bounded sample gives

\[-\int\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol M\,dV+\oint\boldsymbol M\cdot\boldsymbol n\,dA=0.\]

North and south effective poles may be far apart, but a surrounding closed Gaussian surface still has zero net magnetic flux. If a model predicts a \(1/r^2\) radial field of one sign through an entire distant sphere around a fully included bar magnet, test whether the opposite face or volume contribution has been accidentally dropped.

Infer the field of a spherical void by subtracting a known sample

Superposition can calculate a local cavity field before introducing a detailed microscopic model. Imagine a large body with uniform imposed magnetisation \(\boldsymbol M\) near an interior point, and remove a small spherical piece. The magnetic field after removal is the original macroscopic field minus the contribution of the excised sphere, provided the surrounding magnetisation stays unchanged. A uniformly magnetised sphere in otherwise empty space alone has uniform internal \(H=-M/3\), as can be derived from its pole distribution \(\sigma_m=M\cos\theta\). At the sphere centre \(\boldsymbol H\) follows by integrating the field from surface elements \(-\sigma_m\hat{\boldsymbol r}\,dA/(4\pi a^2)\). The \(z\)-component involves the spherical average \(\langle\cos^2\theta\rangle=1/3\); transverse contributions vanish by symmetry. The unique dipolar harmonic extends that central value uniformly throughout the sphere.

The sphere's own internal \(B\) is therefore \(2\mu_0M/3\). Immediately after its removal the field in the empty cavity is

\[B_{\rm cav}=B_{\rm bulk}-2\mu_0M/3=\mu_0(H_{\rm bulk}+M/3).\]

This assumes the sphere is small relative to the variation scale of the pre-existing magnetisation and far from the sample's external boundary on its own size scale. A different cavity shape changes the subtraction factor, and a physical bore can rearrange the surrounding magnetisation or admit demagnetising fields from its ends. Local-field interpretations depend on probe geometry as well as material properties.

As a numerical illustration, suppose a permanent or pinned surrounding material has local \(M=1500\,\mathrm{A/m}\) and \(H_{\rm bulk}=400\,\mathrm{A/m}\), aligned. The macroscopic bulk field is \(\mu_0(1900)=2.39\,\mathrm{mT}\). A small spherical empty cavity under the stated frozen-distribution approximation has \(B_{\rm cav}=\mu_0(900)=1.13\,\mathrm{mT}\). Both readings can be correct for the same initial material. A Hall element inserted into a drilled hole measures the field in that hole; labelling it 'the internal material field' without modelling the probe cavity can cause a substantial discrepancy. Small hole volume does not by itself make the local cavity field correction small, since area and inverse-square field factors cancel in the spherical surface integral.

For a final comparison, cut a very thin flat slit perpendicular to \(\boldsymbol M\) instead of a sphere, idealising its lateral dimensions as large relative to its thickness. The facing effective pole sheets produce a strong additional field within the slit. Normal \(B\) stays continuous across its faces, so the vacuum reading tends towards the original normal \(B_{\rm bulk}\) when edge effects and magnetisation changes are negligible. A long narrow cavity parallel to the magnetisation instead tends towards continuity of tangential \(H\) on its long sides. Comparing the sphere and slit is an operational way to understand why \(B\) and \(H\) are different macroscopic variables rather than two symbols for one ambiguous 'magnetic intensity'.

Read the current implied by a magnetisation

Change the magnetisation and compare interior and exterior fields at the surface. Check which tangential or normal field condition is relevant.

Open this experiment online

Reconstruct an invisible current distribution in a magnetised film

An extended magnetic film fills the slab \(-a<z<a\). A fabrication process leaves a tangential magnetisation \(M_x(z)\hat x\). Let the magnetisation vanish outside. There is no free current in this calculation, and there is no applied field at infinity. A field measurement above the centre of a very wide film can then give nearly zero even when the magnetisation inside varies substantially. Work out both the bound currents and the local magnetic field before deciding what an external zero reading means.

Inside the material the bound volume current is

\[J_{{\rm b},y}=dM_x/dz.\]

The upper face has outward normal \(+\hat z\), so its bound sheet current is \(-M_x(a^-)\hat y\). On the lower face the outward normal reverses and the sheet current is \(+M_x(-a^+)\hat y\). Integrating the volume current through the thickness gives \(M_x(a^-)-M_x(-a^+)\). Thus the combined sheet and volume currents sum to zero per unit length across the entire film. This cancellation is independent of how much structure the magnetisation has inside the slab. An alternative derivation extends \(M_x\) by zero beyond the faces and differentiates that piecewise function as a distribution. The jumps generate both surface terms with their proper signs.

For an explicit depth profile take \(M_x=M_0(1+z/a)\). There is a uniform bulk current \(M_0/a\), no lower surface current, and an upper surface current \(-2M_0\). Planar symmetry allows only \(B_x(z)\). Ampère's law gives

\[dB_x/dz=\mu_0 J_y.\]

Integrating from below the film, where the field vanishes, gives \(B_x=\mu_0 M_0(1+z/a)\) inside. At the upper face the field jumps back to zero. The result also follows directly from \(H_x=B_x/\mu_0-M_x\): its tangential component is continuous wherever there is no free surface current, and there is no volume source for its variation. In this geometry \(H_x=0\) throughout.

Take \(a=0.20\,{\rm mm}\) and \(M_0=2.0\times10^4\,{\rm A/m}\). The interior current density is \(1.0\times10^8\,{\rm A/m^2}\). That large number represents microscopic circulating moments after coarse graining. It does not imply a transport current passing through external electrical contacts. The peak interior field is approximately \(50.3\,{\rm mT}\) just below the upper face, and the average is about \(25.1\,{\rm mT}\). An exterior sensor over the central region of the infinite idealisation reads zero at any height. With a finite lateral extent, currents must close around the film edges. The resulting fringe signal contains shape information and becomes measurable as the probe approaches the rim.

Rotate the magnetisation through ninety degrees so that it now points in the \(z\) direction while its magnitude keeps the same depth profile. Each local curl vanishes. Each face has parallel magnetisation and normal, giving zero bound sheet current. Consequently the solution has zero \(B\) everywhere in the ideal infinite geometry. Within the film \(H_z=-M_z\). In the magnetic-pole construction the two surface pole densities and the bulk density \(-dM_z/dz\) provide precisely that auxiliary field. These two slab examples illustrate a real inverse problem: very different internal magnetic states can produce the same exterior measurement. Knowing the material orientation or imposing a changed external field supplies additional information.

Can two depth profiles with the same average magnetisation be distinguished by an ideal measurement of the tangential magnetic flux through the film thickness?

Work through the solution

Choose a rectangular sensing surface with its normal along \(x\), its height equal to the film thickness, and its length \(w\) along \(y\). The ideal internal flux is \(w\mu_0\int_{-a}^{a}M_x(z)\,dz\). This measurement determines one weighted integral. A constant \(M_0\) and the linear example above give identical results even though the constant profile has only surface currents. A depth-selective measurement would weight the profiles differently. For example, integrating over just the upper half gives \(\mu_0waM_0\) in the constant case and \(3\mu_0waM_0/2\) in the linear case. Actual pickup loops must close outside the sample, so include their full geometry when inferring an internal flux.

Suppose the linear profile relaxes uniformly in amplitude after an aligning field is removed. What information would the integrated voltage of a calibrated pickup provide?

Work through the solution

If the pickup links \(N\) turns with the preceding full-thickness flux and \(M_0\) decays from its initial value to zero, the time-integrated emf has magnitude \(2N\mu_0waM_0(0)\). Faraday's law supplies this integral independently of whether the decay is exponential or has several relaxation times. A finite integration window that ends before zero magnetisation underestimates the initial amplitude unless the residual flux is measured. Leakage from the changing applied alignment field can also drive the loop directly. A reference winding of the same area, measured without the specimen, allows that part of the transient to be estimated.

David Tong, Electromagnetism in Matter, on macroscopic magnetisation and bound currents.

Tutorial on reconstructing material sources

In the nonuniform cylinder let \(M_\varphi=M_0(s/a)^3\) instead. Obtain the bulk current density, the side sheet, the total bound current through the full cross section and the interior field.

Work through the solution
\[J_{b,z}=s^{-1}\partial_s(M_0s^4/a^3)=4M_0s^2/a^3.\]

At the wall \(K_{b,z}=-M_0\). Integration gives \(2\pi aM_0\) in the bulk and its opposite on the wall. For an interior circle the enclosed bulk current is \(2\pi M_0s^4/a^3\), giving \(B_\varphi=\mu_0M_0(s/a)^3\). Both total cancellation and dimensional consistency check the result.

Two adjoining media have tangential magnetisations \(400\hat{\boldsymbol x}\) and \(100\hat{\boldsymbol x}\,\mathrm{A/m}\). The normal from 1 to 2 is \(+\hat{\boldsymbol z}\). Find the net bound sheet current and the associated tangential jump of \(\boldsymbol B\) if no free surface current is present.

Work through the solution

The equivalent sheet is

\[300\hat{\boldsymbol x}\times\hat{\boldsymbol z}=-300\hat{\boldsymbol y}\,\mathrm{A/m}.\]

Tangential \(H_x\) is continuous, so

\[B_{2x}-B_{1x}=\mu_0(M_{2x}-M_{1x})=-0.377\,\mathrm{mT}.\]

Crossing this difference with the chosen normal gives \(\mu_0\boldsymbol K_b\), matching the microscopic Ampère law.

A long solenoidal sample has known \(M\) only to 5 percent. Can a single Hall reading at the centre establish that its magnetisation is uniform over the entire volume to that accuracy?

Work through the solution

No. The reading is one geometry-weighted integral of the source. Reversing magnetisation can remove a stable external offset, and measurements at several axial positions can test the uniform-cylinder shape, but the single central datum cannot uniquely determine a spatially resolved source. Agreement of a fitted constant at one point supplies no independent uniformity test.

Interfaces, demagnetising fields and the work of magnetisation

Magnetism, induction and field dynamics

Two samples made of the same material can respond very differently to an externally applied field if one is a long rod and the other is a thin disc. The induced moment depends on the internal field, which the magnetised sample helps create. The same distinction reappears when one tries to infer a force from stored magnetic energy.

Introduce the auxiliary field by removing a known current

With magnetisation present the total steady current is \(\boldsymbol J_f+\nabla\times\boldsymbol M\), where the curl includes any sheet contribution if \(\boldsymbol M\) is extended distributionally. Define \(\boldsymbol H=\boldsymbol B/\mu_0-\boldsymbol M\), giving \(\nabla\times\boldsymbol H=\boldsymbol J_f\). A small ribbon crossing an interface with normal \(\boldsymbol n\) directed from 1 to 2 gives \(\boldsymbol n\times(\boldsymbol H_2-\boldsymbol H_1)=\boldsymbol K_f\), and a thin pillbox gives

\[\boldsymbol n\cdot(\boldsymbol B_2-\boldsymbol B_1)=0.\]

The boundary conditions distinguish the field whose circulation counts free current from the field whose flux has no sources.

If both media are linear, isotropic and local,

\[\boldsymbol B_i=\mu_i\boldsymbol H_i.\]

In the absence of free surface current \(H_t\) and \(B_n\) are continuous. For angles measured from the normal, \(\tan\theta=B_t/B_n\) therefore gives \(\tan\theta_2/\tan\theta_1=\mu_2/\mu_1\) when the nonzero components permit the ratio.
