For instance, going from vacuum into a medium with \(\mu_r=5\), let \(B_{1n}=2\,\mathrm{mT}\) and \(B_{1t}=1\,\mathrm{mT}\). The new components are \(2\) and \(5\,\mathrm{mT}\), tilting the local field from \(26.6^\circ\) to \(68.2^\circ\). A mounted probe sensitive only to the normal component would register no jump. This local argument says which components can jump; it does not solve the complete field of a finite piece of material.

Solve a sphere to find its internal field

Put a uniform isotropic sphere of radius \(a\) and permeability \(\mu_r\mu_0\) in an originally uniform external \(\boldsymbol H_0=H_0\hat{\boldsymbol z}\). There is no free current in or near it, so locally \(\boldsymbol H=-\nabla\psi\). In each uniform domain \(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol B=0\) implies Laplace's equation for \(\psi\). Spherical symmetry of the geometry and the linear far-field potential select the angular harmonic \(\cos\theta\). The finite interior solution is \(-C r\cos\theta\); the exterior is \(-H_0r\cos\theta+D\cos\theta/r^2\). Tangential \(H\) agrees at the surface when \(\psi\) is continuous up to a chosen common constant. Normal \(B\) enforces the second equation:

\[C=H_0-D/a^3,\]
\[\mu_r C=H_0+2D/a^3,\]
\[C=\frac{3H_0}{\mu_r+2}.\]

Inside, \(H_{\rm int}=C\) is uniform. Multiplying by susceptibility \(\chi=\mu_r-1\) gives

\[M=\chi C.\]

Rearranging yields the memorable self-consistency relation \(H_{\rm int}=H_0-M/3\). The factor \(1/3\) came from solving both boundary conditions for a sphere and is not a universal material property. The dipolar exterior carries moment \(m=4\pi D=(4\pi a^3/3)M\), as expected from summing the uniform magnetisation. An ellipsoid with a principal axis parallel to the drive can likewise have uniform magnetisation and \(H_{\rm int}=H_0-N_dM\) with a geometric demagnetising factor; an arbitrary block usually has spatial variations near edges.

Take \(\mu_r=10\) and \(H_0=200\,\mathrm{A/m}\). The sphere has \(H_{\rm int}=50\,\mathrm{A/m}\), \(M=450\,\mathrm{A/m}\) and \(B_{\rm int}=0.628\,\mathrm{mT}\). A naive use of \(M=9H_0\) would give four times the actual moment. A long narrow specimen parallel to the drive, with very small \(N_d\), comes much closer to that latter value if the material remains linear. For the sphere \(\mu_r\to\infty\) gives \(B_{\rm int}\to3\mu_0H_0\) rather than infinity; meanwhile \(H_{\rm int}\to0\). These two trends are compatible because \(B/H\) is growing. Calling either simply a 'field enhancement' without specifying the measured field obscures the limiting result.

Derive the incremental energy for a reversible medium

Imagine increasing the current in a winding slowly while holding all material boundaries fixed. Faraday's law makes the terminal voltage, apart from resistance, \(d\lambda/dt\), where \(\lambda\) is the flux linkage. The source delivers incremental energy \(I\,d\lambda\). In a uniform toroidal core of mean path length \(\ell\), area \(A\), and \(N\) turns,

\[H=NI/\ell,\ \lambda=NBA.\]

Therefore \(I\,d\lambda=(A\ell)H\,dB\). In a lossless quasistatic medium the magnetic energy density accumulated along a reversible constitutive path is \(w(B)=\int_0^B H(B')\,dB'\). When \(B=\mu H\) with constant \(\mu\), this reduces to \(BH/2\). The corresponding coenergy \(w'(H)=\int_0^H B(H')\,dH'\) satisfies \(w+w'=BH\) for a single-valued curve through the origin.

As a nonlinear example, use \(H=\alpha B+\beta B^3\) with \(\alpha=10^3\,\mathrm{A/(m\,T)}\) and \(\beta=4.0\times10^3\,\mathrm{A/(m\,T^3)}\) over the measured range. At \(B=0.50\) T, \(H=1000\,\mathrm{A/m}\). The integrated energy is

\[w=\alpha B^2/2+\beta B^4/4=187.5\,\mathrm{J/m^3};\]

half the endpoint product gives \(250\,\mathrm{J/m^3}\) and misses the curvature. Coenergy is \(312.5\,\mathrm{J/m^3}\), compensating the difference. The differential permeability is \(dB/dH=(\alpha+3\beta B^2)^{-1}=2.50\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{H/m}\), whereas the ratio \(B/H=5.00\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{H/m}\). A small alternating signal about a DC operating point samples the differential value. These numbers are properties of the model curve and should not be mistaken for a universal ferromagnetic law.

Add an air gap and localise the energy

Give a toroidal magnetic circuit a thin gap of length \(g\), neglecting fringing, leakage and flux crowding. For equal cross sections the same normal \(B\) passes through core and gap. Ampère's law gives

\[NI=H_c\ell_c+B g/\mu_0.\]

For linear \(\mu_r\), this yields

\[B=\mu_0NI/(\ell_c/\mu_r+g).\]

With \(\ell_c=0.20\,\mathrm m,\ g=1.0\,\mathrm{mm},\ \mu_r=1000,\ NI=100\) A-turns the result is \(0.1047\) T. The gap consumes \(83.3\) A-turns; the much longer core consumes \(16.7\). Vary the two lengths separately to see why high permeability cannot remove the gap contribution.

Their energy ratio follows from \(B^2/(2\mu)\) times volume, giving \(U_g/U_c=g\mu_r/\ell_c=5.0\). For area \(A=1.0\,\mathrm{cm^2}\), the gap stores \(0.436\,\mathrm{mJ}\) and the core \(0.0873\,\mathrm{mJ}\). The simple force scale at one nearly uniform normal gap face is

\[B^2A/(2\mu_0)=0.436\,\mathrm N.\]

Decreasing \(g\) at fixed current changes the total flux, so differentiating only the instantaneous gap energy while holding everything else implicitly fixed need not reproduce the mechanical force. One may use a vacuum stress surface or a coenergy derivative at prescribed current. Both methods require a clear statement about what is held fixed during the imagined displacement.

Vary the gap and the permeability independently

Distinguish reversible storage from a measured loop

Real ferromagnetic samples can have history-dependent magnetisation. If \(B\) and \(H\) trace a closed hysteresis loop, the integral \(\oint H\,dB\) measures energy per volume delivered in one quasistatic cycle, with a positive sign for a dissipative cycle under passive conventions. A single-valued energy density of \(B\) alone cannot reproduce this nonzero path integral. At finite frequency conductive eddy losses and relaxation can broaden a measured loop, so a loop measured at one rate alone cannot isolate a purely quasistatic hysteresis loss.

For an approximately parallelogram-shaped cycle spanning switching fields \(\pm120\,\mathrm{A/m}\) and switching induction swing \(0.80\,\mathrm T\) from lower to upper branch, its approximate enclosed area is \(240\times0.80=192\,\mathrm{J/m^3}\) per cycle. At 50 Hz a \(12\,\mathrm{cm^3}\) sample dissipates approximately \(0.115\,\mathrm W\). The stated swing here is the full separation, eliminating a common factor-of-two ambiguity. A power meter on the terminals also sees the winding's \(I^2R\) and any energy leaving the apparatus; a separate resistance measurement and a frequency sweep help isolate the material contribution.

Reconstruct an anisotropic interface from boundary data

At an interface with a single crystal the relation between \(\boldsymbol H\) and \(\boldsymbol B\) may be tensorial. The formulas for angle refraction derived earlier assume scalar permeability; the underlying boundary conditions do not. Take a planar interface with normal \(+\hat{\boldsymbol z}\), vacuum below and a linear reciprocal crystal above. Choose units such that its relative permeability in the \(x,z\) plane is the symmetric matrix with entries \(\mu_{xx}=4,\mu_{xz}=\mu_{zx}=1,\mu_{zz}=2\). Its positive determinant and positive diagonal make the associated magnetic energy positive. Suppose the vacuum drive near the boundary has \(H_{1x}=100\,\mathrm{A/m}\) and \(B_{1z}/\mu_0=200\,\mathrm{A/m}\), with no \(y\) component and no free sheet current. These are local interface data, not a complete boundary condition for a finite crystal.

Continuity of tangential \(H\) gives \(H_{2x}=100\). Continuity of normal \(B\) gives \(100+2H_{2z}=200\), hence \(H_{2z}=50\,\mathrm{A/m}\). The crystal's tangential flux density then follows from \(B_{2x}/\mu_0=4(100)+50=450\,\mathrm{A/m}\); \(B_{2z}/\mu_0=200\) by construction. In the crystal \(\boldsymbol H\) and \(\boldsymbol B\) point at different angles to the normal, with tangent ratios 2 and 2.25 respectively. The local reversible magnetic energy density is

\[\boldsymbol H\cdot\boldsymbol B/2=\mu_0[100(450)+50(200)]/2=0.0346\,\mathrm{J/m^3}.\]

Replacing the tensor by a scalar fitted to the normal component would give the wrong tangent field and energy even though that fitted scalar reproduces one measurement by design.

For a general symmetric matrix, the cross energy in \(w(\boldsymbol H)=\boldsymbol H\cdot\boldsymbol\mu\boldsymbol H/2\) involves both off-diagonal elements. Its derivative with respect to one component yields the corresponding component of \(\boldsymbol B\). One can test reciprocity by preparing two small field increments about the same reversible operating point and interchanging their directions. The mixed incremental energy should agree. Large hysteresis loops, saturation along an easy axis or externally biased nonreciprocal media require more information before equating the two cross responses. The scalar material tables often used for a gapped iron core therefore encode a particular direction and operating regime.

Determine a demagnetising factor or an intrinsic susceptibility

For an ellipsoidal sample along a principal axis, combine \(H_{\rm int}=H_0-N_dM\) with \(M=\chi H_{\rm int}\). Define apparent susceptibility \(\chi_{\rm app}=M/H_0\). Rearrangement gives

\[1/\chi_{\rm app}=1/\chi+N_d.\]

This linear relation suggests a comparative measurement that can separate material and shape without having a probe inside either sample. Prepare geometrically distinct ellipsoids from one linear isotropic material, measure their moments along specified axes in the same calibrated uniform field, and divide by their respective volumes. If their demagnetising factors are independently known and the material history has not changed, the quantities \(1/\chi_{\rm app}-N_d\) should agree.

Take intrinsic \(\chi=20\). A sphere with \(N_d=1/3\) gives \(\chi_{\rm app}=2.609\); a slender ellipsoid with \(N_d=0.050\) gives \(\chi_{\rm app}=10.0\). One might attribute their nearly fourfold moment ratio to different compositions if the shapes were omitted. Suppose the measured slender-sample value is \(9.0\) instead. Its inferred intrinsic susceptibility would be \(1/(1/9-0.05)=16.36\). Comparing the sphere's inferred value of 20 tests whether the discrepancy exceeds shape, volume and magnetometer uncertainties. The inversion is especially ill-conditioned near the large-\(\chi\) apparent saturation \(1/N_d\); small errors in a heavily demagnetised sample then produce large errors in the recovered intrinsic response.

The sensitivity can be quantified without introducing a fitting convention. Differentiate \(\chi=\chi_{\rm app}/(1-N_d\chi_{\rm app})\), giving \(d\chi/d\chi_{\rm app}=1/(1-N_d\chi_{\rm app})^2\). At the preceding spherical point this derivative is nearly 59, so an absolute error 0.02 in the apparent value shifts the recovered intrinsic result by about 1.18. For the slender sample the derivative is only four at the stated point. The large spherical amplification follows from subtraction of nearly equal quantities, not poor electrical calibration. An elongated specimen may therefore yield a more informative intrinsic measurement even if a sphere is easier to machine and align.

Check a material explanation against mechanical and electrical work

Imagine measuring a single coil fitted with an adjustable magnetic insert. At a particular position the current ramp up from zero to 0.50 A produces linkage \(\lambda(I)=aI+bI^3\) with \(a=0.040\,\mathrm H\), \(b=-0.020\,\mathrm{H/A^2}\) over this restricted range. Differential inductance is \(a+3bI^2\) and stays positive here. Stored energy is \(\int I\,d\lambda=aI^2/2+3bI^4/4\), whereas coenergy is \(\int\lambda dI=aI^2/2+bI^4/4\). At the stated end current they are \(4.06\) and \(4.69\) mJ. Their sum \(8.75\) mJ equals \(I\lambda\). A measured terminal waveform can integrate the first expression directly by accumulating \(I(v-RI)dt\); independently plotting \(\lambda\) versus \(I\) allows the second by planimetric integration. These two areas have different shapes on a nonlinear constitutive curve.

If the insert moves, coenergy at each position can be obtained by repeating the reversible \(\lambda(I)\) scan. Its spatial difference divided by travel distance estimates the average force for fixed end current. Simply using half the product of the end current and linkage would predict neither energy correctly in this example. A hysteretic insert follows different curves on raising and lowering the current, so a measured mean force or cycle loss must state the prepared branch. A force predicted by one reversible branch can be checked with a load cell at the same position and current; an electrical scan at another bias is not enough to test the mechanical boundary condition.

Put the magnetic energy on the correct side of a gap

Change the reluctance of a narrow air gap and inspect which section controls flux. Check the assumptions needed before replacing the complete field with a circuit.

Open this experiment online

Derive the directional permeability of a stack of thin layers

A material assembled from alternating sheets can respond differently to fields along and across the sheets even when each constituent is isotropic. Consider many parallel layers of two linear, reversible magnetic materials with absolute permeabilities \(\mu_1\) and \(\mu_2\). Their volume fractions in a representative period are \(f\) and \(1-f\). Start far from the outer edges. Suppose the imposed field varies only on lengths much longer than a period, and take the frequency low enough that eddy currents do not change the field distribution. These conditions make a comparison with homogeneous bulk measurements meaningful.

First apply a field tangent to the layers. The tangential field \(H_t\) is continuous at each interface because no free sheet current flows there. Each material has a different magnetic induction \(B_{it}=\mu_i H_t\). Averaging the induction over one period and dividing by the shared field gives

\[\mu_\parallel=f\mu_1+(1-f)\mu_2.\]

This is an arithmetic mean. A heavily permeable layer can carry a large fraction of the tangential flux through only part of the period. No condition requires equal tangential induction on the two sides of the internal interface.

Next drive the magnetic flux through the stack in the normal direction. Normal induction is continuous because magnetic flux lines cannot end at a layer. The normal field in each layer is \(B_n/\mu_i\). Averaging the field at fixed common induction now gives a harmonic mean,

\[\frac{1}{\mu_\perp}=\frac{f}{\mu_1}+\frac{1-f}{\mu_2}.\]

A small fraction of a weakly permeable spacer can account for most of the magnetomotive drop across a stack. One can derive the same result by adding the magnetic reluctances of a column of common area. That circuit calculation works here because the interface conditions guarantee a common induction. Tilting the applied field creates both normal and tangential components and requires both averages at once. In axes attached to the layers,

\[\langle B_t\rangle=\mu_\parallel\langle H_t\rangle,\]
\[\langle B_n\rangle=\mu_\perp\langle H_n\rangle.\]

The averaged field vectors are generally not parallel.

For a stack containing \(90\%\) by volume of a material with relative permeability \(1000\) and \(10\%\) of a spacer with relative permeability one, the two effective relative permeabilities are \(900.1\) and approximately \(9.91\). Suppose a fixture produces equal average components \(H_t=H_n=10\,{\rm A/m}\). The induction components are then about \(11.31\,{\rm mT}\) and \(0.125\,{\rm mT}\). Their ratio corresponds to an induction angle only \(0.63^\circ\) above the layers while the average field points at \(45^\circ\). A probe sensitive to a single component could therefore return an apparently inconsistent permeability when the orientation of an anisotropic specimen is ignored.

Verify the energy before using the homogenised material law. For a purely normal induction the microscopic average energy density is

\[fB_n^2/(2\mu_1)+(1-f)B_n^2/(2\mu_2)=B_n^2/(2\mu_\perp).\]

For tangential field the microscopic average is

\[H_t^2[f\mu_1+(1-f)\mu_2]/2=\mu_\parallel H_t^2/2.\]

Adding both contributions gives \(\langle\mathbf B\rangle\cdot\langle\mathbf H\rangle/2\) for this linear model. Directly replacing each constituent field by the spatially averaged field before evaluating its energy would give an incorrect normal contribution. The field concentration in the spacer is a physical effect that survives passage to an effective continuum.

Estimate how precisely the spacer fraction must be known if a low-frequency normal-induction calibration is to be reproducible.

Work through the solution

Write \(s=1-f\) and \(\mu_{\perp,r}=[(1-s)/1000+s]^{-1}\). Differentiating its logarithm gives \(d\ln\mu_{\perp,r}/ds=-0.999/[0.001+0.999s]\), about \(-9.90\) at \(s=0.10\). Increasing the spacer volume fraction by one absolute percentage point therefore reduces normal permeability by roughly ten percent to first order. Direct substitution at \(s=0.11\) gives \(9.02\), or a reduction of about nine percent, showing the size of the linearisation error for that relatively large change. The same change reduces the tangential relative permeability from \(900.1\) to \(890.1\), only about one percent.

How does local saturation change the normal and tangential experiments?

Work through the solution

In the normal test the induction is shared. A high-permeability layer reaches a specified saturation induction at approximately the same common induction one measures outside a suitably shaped test column. In the tangential test the field is shared and the high-permeability phase can have a much larger local induction than the reported average. For an infinitesimal variation around a bias point, repeat the interface argument with the local differential permeabilities \(dB_i/dH_i\). The differential effective response obeys the same arithmetic or reciprocal averaging rules under the one-dimensional hypotheses. A finite path that traverses a hysteresis loop needs its history and dissipation as well; no single constant differential permeability specifies its loop area.

David Tong, Electromagnetism in Matter, on constitutive laws and material interfaces.

Tutorial on geometry and constitutive response

A spherical sample has apparent susceptibility \(M/H_0=2.4\). Infer its intrinsic linear susceptibility and the internal field fraction. Can an apparent value of 3.5 arise in this model with positive intrinsic susceptibility?

Work through the solution

For a sphere \(M/H_0=\chi/(1+\chi/3)\). Inversion gives

\[\chi=2.4/(1-0.8)=12,\]
\[H_{\rm int}/H_0=1/5.\]

Positive finite \(\chi\) gives apparent values less than three. A measured larger result would call for checking shape, units, background or the linear homogeneous assumption.

An unknown material is placed in a long gapped circuit. At a given operating point twice the current gives only 1.6 times the old induction. Can a fixed linear permeability with unchanged geometry explain it?

Work through the solution

A fixed linear reluctance at fixed geometry gives exactly proportional induction. One can infer \(H_c=(NI-Bg/\mu_0)/\ell_c\) at each point and compare the results with a measured constitutive curve. Field-dependent permeability, leakage, fringing or a changing physical gap would each need evaluation before calling the effect saturation.

An interface has no sheet current, the normal field happens to vanish on both sides, and the tangential magnetic flux density differs by a factor of eight. What material ratio follows if both media are scalar and linear? Does this require a discontinuity in \(H_t\)?

Work through the solution

Continuity of \(H_t\) gives \(B_{2t}/B_{1t}=\mu_2/\mu_1=8\) if the tangential drive is nonzero. No tangential \(H\) jump is needed. The magnetisation jump can supply an equivalent bound surface current producing the jump in \(B_t\).

Measure how quickly a dielectric follows an electric field

Fields and the response of matter

Derive the time and frequency responses of a relaxing polarisation, locate the loss maximum and decide whether a measured sample contains more than one relaxation process.

From a delayed polarisation to a constitutive equation

A slab of insulating material is placed between parallel electrodes. At first the measured charge increases very quickly when a small voltage is switched on. It then continues to rise slowly even though the applied voltage is fixed. A constant real relative permittivity can describe the final charge but cannot predict this record. The experiment reveals at least two relevant time scales. For a measurement window that resolves only the slower process, treat the fast electronic response as instantaneous and describe the slower polarisation \(P\) by its approach to an equilibrium value.

At fixed weak field the equilibrium slow polarisation is \(\epsilon_0\Delta\epsilon E\), with \(\Delta\epsilon>0\). Assume the rate of change is proportional to the distance from that equilibrium, with one positive relaxation time \(\tau\). Then

\[\tau\dot P+P=\epsilon_0\Delta\epsilon E(t).\]

This is a phenomenological material law. Its assumptions include linear response about the prepared state, a time-independent \(\tau\), and a sample whose constituents can be described by one dominant relaxation. Testing those assumptions is part of the experiment.

For \(E(t)=E_s\) switched on at zero from an unpolarised state, solve using the integrating factor \(e^{t/\tau}\). The result is

\[P=\epsilon_0\Delta\epsilon E_s(1-e^{-t/\tau}).\]

After one \(\tau\), 63.21% of the slow response has developed; after \(3\tau\), 95.02%. Measuring an absolute fraction requires subtracting the fast initial jump and knowing the eventual plateau. A long baseline drift can look like a second relaxation if those references are not controlled.

Derive the complex dielectric response

Now use peak phasors with time dependence \(e^{-i\omega t}\). Differentiation contributes \(-i\omega\), so

\[P/E=\epsilon_0\Delta\epsilon/(1-i\omega\tau).\]

Include the high-frequency relative permittivity \(\epsilon_\infty\), where “high” means above the slow process but below additional electronic resonances. The real and imaginary parts of relative permittivity are

\[\epsilon'=\epsilon_\infty+\frac{\Delta\epsilon}{1+(\omega\tau)^2},\]
\[\epsilon''=\frac{\Delta\epsilon\,\omega\tau}{1+(\omega\tau)^2}.\]

At fixed field amplitude the polarisation current is

\[\dot P=-i\omega P.\]

The mean electrical work per unit time and volume is

\[\tfrac12\operatorname{Re}(E^*\dot P)=\tfrac12\omega\epsilon_0\epsilon''|E|^2.\]

Loss therefore has positive \(\epsilon''\) in the chosen time convention. Using \(e^{+i\omega t}\) reverses the sign of the imaginary part of permittivity but leaves mean absorbed energy positive when the current convention is changed consistently.

Set \(u=\omega\tau\). Differentiating \(u/(1+u^2)\) gives \((1-u^2)/(1+u^2)^2\). Hence \(\epsilon''\) peaks at \(u=1\), with height \(\Delta\epsilon/2\), and the real part lies halfway between its low- and high-frequency plateaux. The peak identified here is in the imaginary *permittivity*. A plot of dissipated power at fixed field includes an additional factor of frequency and need not have a peak at the same location.

Tie a frequency sweep to a step test

Suppose a material has \(\epsilon_\infty=2\), \(\Delta\epsilon=6\), and a measured loss peak at 1 kHz. Then \(\tau=1/(2\pi\,1000)\simeq159\ \mu\mathrm s\). At the peak the relative permittivity is \(5+3i\) in our convention. A time-domain voltage step should show a slow charge transient reaching 63.2% of its added charge in roughly 159 μs. This comparison connects two experiments that weight electrode offsets and parasitic impedances differently.

An independent shape check follows by eliminating \(u\). Let \(X=\epsilon'-\epsilon_\infty\) and \(Y=\epsilon''\). From the formulas \(X^2+Y^2=\Delta\epsilon X\). Thus \((X-\Delta\epsilon/2)^2+Y^2=(\Delta\epsilon/2)^2\): a single Debye relaxation traces a semicircle in the complex plane. Departures from a semicircle can reflect several relaxation times, spatially inhomogeneous samples, leakage or imperfect fixture correction. They do not uniquely identify the microscopic mechanism.

Resolve a second process only when the data require it

Open the live experiment

The synthetic sample has two groups contributing a fixed total \(\Delta\epsilon=6\). Change the fraction in the slower group and its relaxation time relative to the faster group. At equal relaxation times the groups give exactly the single-process response whatever their fractions. When the times differ, the imaginary curves broaden and may separate. Observe the real dispersion steps as well. A broad lossy peak alone can be fitted by more than one pair of times and strengths.

In a practical measurement the low-frequency tail can contain DC conductivity. In the \(e^{-i\omega t}\) convention,

\[\sigma E-i\omega\epsilon_0\epsilon E=-i\omega\epsilon_0[\epsilon+i\sigma/(\epsilon_0\omega)]E.\]

This added imaginary part is proportional to \(1/\omega\), whereas the low-frequency side of an isolated Debye relaxation rises as \(\omega\). Change electrode separation and use a DC conduction measurement to challenge the interpretation. An electrode interface can add a further time scale, so an observed fit parameter deserves an uncertainty and a physical validation step.

Tutorial

The loss maximum is at 2 kHz and its height is 4 in relative-permittivity units. The high-frequency plateau is 3. Predict the low-frequency plateau and the slow response at \(t=100\,\mu\mathrm s\) after a step.

Work through the solution

The single-time model gives \(\Delta\epsilon=8\) and the low-frequency plateau 11. The relaxation time is \(1/[2\pi(2000)]\simeq79.6\,\mu\mathrm s\). The slow fraction after 100 μs is \(1-e^{-100/79.6}\simeq0.715\). Repeat at another step amplitude to check linearity.

At the loss peak, is the phase difference between the *total* displacement and the electric field always 45°?

Work through the solution

The slow polarisation alone has a 45° phasor angle at \(\omega\tau=1\). The total displacement includes \(\epsilon_\infty E\). Its angle is \(\arctan[(\Delta\epsilon/2)/(\epsilon_\infty+\Delta\epsilon/2)]\), generally less than 45°.

Why does observing only a high-frequency tail constrain a ratio of parameters more tightly than the individual parameters?

Work through the solution

For large \(\omega\tau\),

\[\epsilon''\simeq\Delta\epsilon/(\omega\tau).\]

Many values with the same ratio \(\Delta\epsilon/\tau\) have very similar tails. Measure the peak or both plateaux to distinguish the two parameters.

Compare with material measurements

Inspect the orientational response and dielectric-loss treatment in MIT's material-properties course. Use the phasor convention of each source to predict the sign assigned to loss.

Identify mobile charge with a transverse field

Fields and the response of matter

Derive a Hall-voltage measurement from transverse force balance, reverse current and field to reject offsets and explore what fails when two carrier types conduct.

Choose signs from a drawing

A rectangular conducting sample has its long axis along \(x\), width \(w\) along \(y\), and thickness \(t\) along \(z\). A uniform magnetic field \(B\hat{\mathbf z}\) is applied. An external circuit maintains conventional current \(I>0\) in \(+x\). High-impedance transverse probes draw negligible current. Define the measured signal as \(V_H=V(y=0)-V(y=w)\). For uniform transverse field this convention gives

\[V_H=E_yw.\]

Reversing the voltmeter leads reverses the reported sign; writing a carrier sign without a lead convention is incomplete.

With one charge carrier species of number density \(n\) and signed charge \(q\), the drift velocity satisfies

\[j_x=nqv_x=I/(wt).\]

If the transverse velocity vanishes, its magnetic force component is \(-qv_xB\), opposed by \(qE_y\). Thus \(E_y=v_x B=j_xB/(nq)\). The resulting port measurement is

\[V_H=\frac{IB}{nq t}.\]

The width cancels for a uniform bar; changing the contact geometry or using a current source with a nonuniform injection profile can spoil that simplification near the terminals. The cancellation also exposes the sign. For electrons \(q<0\), positive conventional current means that the mobile charge drifts in \(-x\). The inferred transverse field is negative for positive \(B\) in the chosen convention.

Reconstruct density and mobility from separate observations

Take \(t=0.50\) mm, \(I=5.0\) mA, \(B=0.20\) T and a transverse signal of \(-1.25\) mV. The inferred signed Hall coefficient is

\[R_H=tV_H/(IB)=-6.25\times10^{-4}\ {\rm m^3/C}.\]

Using singly charged electrons gives

\[n=1/(e|R_H|)\simeq1.00\times10^{22}\ {\rm m^{-3}}.\]

A longitudinal voltage measurement, after correcting for probe separation, can yield the material conductivity. If \(\sigma=80\) S/m, the single-carrier mobility is

\[\mu=\sigma |R_H|=0.050\ {\rm m^2/(V\,s)}.\]

These operations use different pieces of information. The longitudinal measurement alone gives only the product \(n|q|\mu\). The transverse measurement supplies a sign and, in the one-carrier model, the density independently of mobility. This is an inverse experiment whose two observables have different sensitivities, not a more precise measurement of the same scalar.

One can derive the same result from a stationary Drude equation \(0=q(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)-m\mathbf v/\tau\). The longitudinal drag gives

\[E_x=mv_x/(q\tau);\]

force balance in \(y\) gives the transverse field just obtained. In a homogeneous single-carrier sample, \(E_y/E_x=q\tau B/m\), the signed Hall angle tangent. At fixed sample and field, doubling \(I\) doubles both voltages in this linear regime and leaves their ratio unchanged. Significant current-dependent apparent density can indicate heating or nonlinear contacts.

Isolate the transverse voltage from a misaligned contact

Practical side contacts seldom sit at exactly the same \(x\). A fraction of the longitudinal voltage can leak into the transverse pair. Write the readout at a fixed current as \(V_\mathrm{m}(B)=V_0+\alpha V_{xx}+V_H(B)\). A background that is even in field cancels in \([V_\mathrm m(B)-V_\mathrm m(-B)]/2\). For example, readings of 3.05 mV and 5.55 mV at \(+B,-B\) return \(-1.25\) mV Hall signal and 4.30 mV of even background. Taking a single measured sign would have diagnosed the wrong carriers.

Open the live experiment

The plot deliberately superposes a constant 4.3 mV pickup on a signed Hall signal. Vary density and current independently before reading the pair at \(B=\pm0.2\) T. Large density weakens the signal; current increases it. Verify that field reversal recovers the correct transverse voltage even when both instrument readings have the same sign. If the unwanted background changes between readings, reverse the field several times and check for repeatable drift.

With both current and field reversal, the double-odd combination \([V(+I,+B)-V(+I,-B)-V(-I,+B)+V(-I,-B)]/4\) isolates terms odd in both. A thermal emf that is independent of current cancels along with a stable contact offset. This symmetry decomposition is a physical assumption about the parasitics, so preserve all four readings and inspect the rejected channels too.

When a Hall density stops being a particle count

With electrons and holes, longitudinal conductivities add. In a weak magnetic field their transverse contributions also depend on squared mobilities. For positive mobilities \(\mu_e,\mu_h\) and electron and hole densities \(n,p\), the two-carrier weak-field coefficient in the sign convention above is \(R_H=(p\mu_h^2-n\mu_e^2)/[e(p\mu_h+n\mu_e)^2]\). To see where the square appears, note that one factor of mobility converts the longitudinal field into drift; a second converts the resulting transverse magnetic force into a drift contribution. Summing species currents and imposing zero total transverse current gives the denominator management through the summed longitudinal conductivity.

Equal electron and hole densities with equal mobilities give zero Hall signal while current still flows readily. A single-species inversion would suggest unbounded density. At equal densities but unequal mobilities, the faster species can set the sign even though neither species is more numerous. Additional field dependence, temperature variation and independent structural information are needed before treating a Hall number as a carrier census.

Tutorial

A measurement doubles the width \(w\) at fixed thickness, total current and field in a uniform sample. Predict both \(E_y\) and \(V_H\).

Work through the solution

The current density halves, so \(v_x\) and \(E_y\) halve. The voltage integrates that halved field over twice the width and stays the same. If fixed current density were held instead, the transverse voltage would double.

A nominal thickness is overestimated by 5%. What bias does this cause in the single-carrier density inferred from a fixed transverse signal?

Work through the solution

\(n=|IB|/(e t|V_H|)\), so using \(1.05t\) gives \(n_\mathrm{fit}=n/1.05\), approximately 4.8% low. This systematic error is common to all repeated field sweeps.

Why might a metal yield a smaller Hall voltage than a semiconductor with the same dimensions at the same current?

Work through the solution

In the one-carrier regime the signal is inversely proportional to carrier density. A large density allows the imposed current at a very small drift speed, requiring a correspondingly small transverse balancing field. Conductivity alone cannot determine this comparison without mobility information.

Follow the transport model

Compare the one-carrier assumptions with the treatment of mobile carriers in David Tong's solid-state physics course. Connect an independently measured conductivity with the signed transverse response before interpreting an effective mass or scattering time.

When does a polar crystal change its preferred state?

Fields and the response of matter

Derive stable and unstable dielectric responses from a free-energy landscape. Separate a reversible small-signal capacitance from a switching experiment.

Why one voltage can support several measured polarisations

Some insulating crystals develop a spontaneous electric polarisation as temperature is lowered. The high-temperature phase may be symmetric under inversion so that states with polarisation \(+P\) and \(-P\) are equivalent in zero field. Near a continuous transition, treat one component of polarisation as an order parameter. A local free-energy density compatible with the symmetry is \(f(P)=a(T-T_c)P^2/2+bP^4/4\) with \(a,b>0\). The omitted terms limit how far in temperature or field this truncated expansion is reliable. The positive quartic term keeps the energy bounded when the quadratic coefficient is negative.

For a device whose applied electric field \(E\) is controlled, the appropriate potential in this simple model is \(g(P;E)=f(P)-EP\). The linear term records external work; changing what is held fixed changes the energy to minimise. Differentiation gives the constitutive equation \(E=a(T-T_c)P+bP^3\). An algebraic root is an equilibrium candidate. A stable local response also requires positive second derivative \(a(T-T_c)+3bP^2\). A very accurate rootfinder can return an unstable stationary point if this second test is omitted.

Find the equilibrium branches and their response

Introduce scaled polarisation \(p\), field \(e\) and temperature parameter \(s\) such that \(g=p^4/4+sp^2/2-ep\). Then \(e=p^3+sp\). For \(s>0\), the slope \(3p^2+s\) stays positive. Each field has one stable solution. At \(e=0\), \(p=0\) and the small-signal susceptibility \(dp/de=1/s\) increases when approaching the ideal critical point from above.

For \(s=-1,\ e=0\), stationary values are \(-1,0,+1\). The middle root has curvature −1 and is a local maximum. Each outer root has curvature 2 and is a minimum. The susceptibility about a selected outer state is \(1/2\) in the scaled units. The fact that the two domains can carry opposite zero-field polarisation does not make the harmonic response negative. The mere existence of a negative-slope segment in the cubic constitutive curve is insufficient to infer a stable isolated small-signal device with that differential response.

Derive the limit of a metastable homogeneous state

Imagine preparing the negative-polarisation state at \(s=-1\), then increasing the applied field slowly. A positive field lowers the energy of the positive state. The negative state can persist locally behind a barrier even after it ceases to be the global minimum. A model of perfectly homogeneous evolution without early nucleation follows this metastable branch until the curvature vanishes. Solve \(3p^2-1=0\), choose \(p=-1/\sqrt3\), and insert it into \(e=p^3-p\). The positive limiting field is

\[e=2/(3\sqrt3)\simeq0.3849.\]

At the opposite spinodal the signs reverse.

This is a limit of the particular energy model. Real switching can occur earlier through domain walls, nucleation, defects or thermally activated barrier crossing. Observing a smaller coercive field in a specimen is not evidence that the cubic equation was solved inaccurately. To compare an actual hysteresis loop with a calculation, state the sweep rate, boundary circuit, sample preparation and a dynamical or nucleation model in addition to equilibrium free energy.

Read a landscape rather than tracing an unlabelled loop

Open the live experiment

Set \(s=-1\). At zero applied field, locate the two equal wells. Increase positive \(e\) gradually and watch the negative well become shallower. The browser locates real stationary points of the cubic, classifies them by curvature and reports their positions. Its plotted potential shows the energy barrier separating basins. Choose \(s>0\) and repeat to see why fitting the same S-shaped hysteresis loop above a continuous transition would be inappropriate.

A below-transition sample can also form spatially varying domains. Adding a positive gradient term \(K(\nabla P)^2/2\) penalises interfaces, while depolarising fields can favour a mixture with reduced net surface charge. This competition sets new length scales absent from the homogeneous model. An electrode measuring total charge can return near-zero mean polarisation from oppositely oriented domains even though each domain is strongly polarised. A local probe and a bulk capacitance measurement therefore constrain different parts of the model.

Turn a capacitance measurement into a consistency test

For a small harmonic voltage imposed about a prepared local minimum, the differential response is \(\delta P/\delta E=1/[a(T-T_c)+3bP^2]\), provided the driving frequency is slow enough for this local variable to respond and the amplitude is small enough to stay in the same well. Add the vacuum and fast polarisation terms when converting to total displacement \(D\). In a parallel-plate geometry capacitance per area then follows from \(\delta D/\delta E\) divided by thickness.

A large-signal charge–voltage measurement crosses different branches and may dissipate work around a loop. The energy per volume over a closed polarisation cycle is \(\oint E\,dP\) with the sign convention for electrical work. A single equilibrium graph of \(f(P)\) returns to its initial state with no prescribed loop area. Measured hysteresis thus carries dynamical information that a polynomial fit to one reversible branch cannot uniquely reconstruct.

Tutorial

For \(s=-4\) in the scaled model, find zero-field minima and the positive homogeneous spinodal field.

Work through the solution

Zero-field minima lie at \(p=\pm2\). The negative spinodal is

\[p=-\sqrt{4/3}=-2/\sqrt3.\]

Substitution gives positive field \(16/(3\sqrt3)\simeq3.08\). The \( |s|^{3/2}\) dependence follows from the cubic.

A Newton solver initialised at \(p=0\) reports exact convergence for \(s=-1,e=0\). What additional observation rejects that result as the prepared stable state?

Work through the solution

Its residual is exactly zero but the curvature \(3p^2+s=-1\) is negative. A small perturbation lowers the potential. Check a measured weak-field slope or the relaxation after a perturbation, and compare the energy of the two minima.

Why can the homogeneous coercive field be much larger than the measured coercive field?

Work through the solution

The calculation removes a local barrier only when a spatially uniform state ceases to exist. A real material can switch by creating a small favourable domain and moving a wall; electrodes and defects can lower the needed activation energy. The relevant material and time scales differ.

Compare material descriptions

Use MIT's ferroelectrics and polarisation material to distinguish a microscopic source of displacement from the macroscopic free-energy model used here.

Distinguish magnetic screening from electrical diffusion

Fields and the response of matter

Compare the frequency dependence, phase and thickness dependence of field penetration in a normal metal and a superconducting slab.

Perfect conductivity and magnetic expulsion are different statements

A normal conductor has current density \(\mathbf J=\sigma\mathbf E\). Combining Faraday's and low-frequency Ampère laws in a homogeneous medium gives

\[\partial_t\mathbf B=(\mu\sigma)^{-1}\nabla^2\mathbf B.\]

A step in field diffuses inwards over a length of order \(\sqrt{t/(\mu\sigma)}\). Under sustained sinusoidal drive the skin depth is \(\delta=\sqrt{2/(\mu\sigma\omega)}\). As \(\omega\) tends to zero, this skin depth tends to infinity. An ordinary nonmagnetic conductor in its final static equilibrium generally admits a uniform applied magnetic field.

The London description of an isotropic superconductor in a Meissner state makes a distinct, equilibrium constitutive assumption:

\[\nabla\times\mathbf J_s=-\mathbf B/(\mu_0\lambda_L^2),\]

with penetration length \(\lambda_L\). In a homogeneous specimen carrying supercurrent and at frequencies where displacement current and normal losses may be neglected,

\[\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf J_s.\]

Curl once more and use \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). The resulting field satisfies

\[\nabla^2\mathbf B=\mathbf B/\lambda_L^2.\]

Unlike the diffusive skin depth, the equilibrium length remains finite for a static applied field.

Taking the formal limit \(\sigma\to\infty\) in a normal conductor suppresses the electric field for a finite current and can freeze a pre-existing flux during changes. That argument does not determine which static field a specimen adopts when cooled through its transition. Magnetic expulsion is extra thermodynamic physics. The comparison below assumes a weak enough field and a suitable state, omitting flux vortices, trapped flux and nonlinear or nonlocal effects.

Solve a slab with equal applied fields on both faces

Let a broad slab fill \(-d/2<x<d/2\), and impose identical tangential magnetic fields \(B_a\hat{\mathbf z}\) on its two faces. Symmetry requires \(B'(0)=0\). Applying both boundaries to the London equation gives

\[B(x)=B_a\frac{\cosh(x/\lambda_L)}{\cosh[d/(2\lambda_L)]},\qquad\]

\frac{\overline B}{B_a}=\frac{2\lambda_L}{d}\tanh\frac{d}{2\lambda_L}.

Differentiating gives the screening current \(J_y=-B'_z/\mu_0\). Currents flow in opposite directions at opposite faces; their net transport current across the thickness is zero. For a thick slab each face approaches a half-space with exponential decay. For \(d\ll\lambda_L\), the two surface layers overlap. Expanding the average gives \(1-d^2/(12\lambda_L^2)+\cdots\), so even a superconductor can have a substantial mean internal field in a sufficiently thin specimen under this parallel-field geometry.

Take \(d=100\) nm and \(\lambda_L=50\) nm. The centre field is \(B_a/\cosh 1\simeq0.6481 B_a\); the mean is \(\tanh1\simeq0.7616 B_a\). The distinction between centre value and volume average is already larger than many instrumental error bars. A measurement of magnetic moment in a finite non-slab geometry additionally requires its demagnetising field. Do not directly substitute the infinite-slab mean into an arbitrary sample-shape calibration.

Obtain the equilibrium length as an energy minimum

The same static profile follows from minimising \(\int_{-d/2}^{d/2}[B^2+\lambda_L^2(B')^2]\,dx/(2\mu_0)\) with boundary values imposed. The first term is magnetic field energy; the second is the energy associated with the screening current in this effective description. Vary \(B\) by a function \(\eta\) vanishing at the faces. Integrating \(B'\eta'\) by parts leaves an interior factor \(B-\lambda_L^2B''\). Its vanishing returns the London equation. This route makes the physical tradeoff clear: complete expulsion would require extremely sharp gradients and costly currents, while uniform penetration pays magnetic energy throughout the bulk.

The reduction assumes a fixed superconducting condensate amplitude. Near a critical temperature or critical field, allowing that amplitude to change introduces an additional coherence length and may alter both the field state and the validity of London theory. A numerical fit giving a stable value of \(\lambda_L\) at one temperature does not prove this approximation over an entire phase diagram.

Compare two experimental controls

Open the live experiment

Change the thickness and the penetration length independently. Find two different specimens with the same \(d/\lambda_L\); their normalised profiles against fractional thickness match, even though their local physical gradients and current densities can differ. If all you can measure is a single normalised profile, geometry calibration is part of the inference of \(\lambda_L\).

A frequency sweep supplies another distinction. In a normal sheet the complex field has both attenuation and phase lag with depth, and \(\delta\) changes as \(f^{-1/2}\). The static London profile above is real and determined by a material length. At higher frequencies superconductors can also exhibit complex response and dissipation, so measuring a single attenuation length in one frequency band cannot by itself establish static flux expulsion. Use the normal-film impedance experiment for a controlled comparison with its different rear boundary condition.

Tutorial

In a thick specimen, how far from one isolated face must a probe be to see 1% of the applied static field? How does the answer depend on field amplitude in this model?

Work through the solution

The half-space profile is \(e^{-x/\lambda_L}\). Set it to 0.01 to obtain

\[x=\lambda_L\ln100\simeq4.605\lambda_L.\]

Linearity makes this scaled distance independent of \(B_a\), until the assumed Meissner and weak-field conditions fail.

For a symmetric slab with \(d=4\lambda_L\), compute the centre and mean fractions.

Work through the solution

The centre gives \(1/\cosh2\simeq0.266\). The mean is \(\tanh2/2\simeq0.482\). Both surface layers contribute substantially to the average even when the centre field is much smaller.

A phase-sensitive measurement detects a substantial depth-dependent phase lag in a slowly driven slab. Does the static London solution predict this?

Work through the solution

The static real solution has no phase lag. Assess normal-fluid losses, vortex motion, measurement phase reference and the finite drive frequency. Comparing only magnitudes can conceal these distinct mechanisms.

Read the equilibrium model

The MIT applied-superconductivity course places London's laws alongside microscopic and order-parameter treatments. Use that ordering to identify the assumptions needed when taking a static limit.

Why rotating a layered dielectric changes a capacitance measurement

Fields and the response of matter

Derive the two principal permittivities of a fine laminate, rotate the constitutive tensor and distinguish the applied electrode condition from an assumed field direction.

Compare the same material in two orientations

Build a specimen by repeating layers with scalar permittivities \(\epsilon_1,\epsilon_2>0\), occupying fractions \(f\) and \(1-f\). The period is much smaller than both the specimen scale and the spatial variation of the applied field. Suppose both materials are linear, free of mobile charge and effectively lossless in the measurement band. An observer using a large capacitor wants one law relating averaged displacement and electric field. A scalar average cannot describe both arrangements of the same layers relative to the plates.

Place the field tangential to all internal interfaces first. A narrow rectangular electrostatic loop straddling an interface gives continuity of tangential \(E\). Both layers see the same tangential field, but their displacements differ in proportion to permittivity. Averaging gives

\[\overline D_t=[f\epsilon_1+(1-f)\epsilon_2]\overline E_t.\]

The tangent-direction effective permittivity is an arithmetic mean. This is a parallel connection of capacitances at shared voltage in the special capacitor geometry.

Apply a purely normal displacement instead. No free charge lies at the internal interfaces, so their normal displacement is continuous. Normal fields differ as \(E_i=D_n/\epsilon_i\). Averaging the voltage drops gives

\[\overline E_n=[f/\epsilon_1+(1-f)/\epsilon_2]\overline D_n,\]

with a harmonic-mean effective permittivity. The local continuity condition changes which field is shared between constituents and thereby changes the relevant averaging operation.

Check the amount of anisotropy from field energy

For \(\epsilon_1=2\epsilon_0,\epsilon_2=10\epsilon_0\) at equal fractions,

\[\epsilon_t=6\epsilon_0,\]
\[\epsilon_n=(10/3)\epsilon_0.\]

The arithmetic-to-harmonic ratio is 1.8. A constituent does not need to be crystalline or anisotropic for the composite to show distinct principal responses. The geometry of the internal boundaries is enough.

Average the energy density for the tangential experiment,

\[u=f\epsilon_1E_t^2/2+(1-f)\epsilon_2E_t^2/2=\epsilon_t E_t^2/2.\]

For the normal experiment,

\[u=D_n^2[f/\epsilon_1+(1-f)/\epsilon_2]/2=D_n^2/(2\epsilon_n).\]

These expressions supply a check independent of the capacitor-circuit analogy and identify which energy should be used when field or free charge is controlled. Energy positivity follows directly from the positive constituent permittivities. It does not require guessing an effective law from the largest measured component.

Rotate the law, then specify what the electrodes control

Use principal axes \(\hat n,\hat t\) and suppose a macro-field makes angle \(\theta\) to the layer normal. If \(\overline{\mathbf E}=E(\cos\theta\,\hat n+\sin\theta\,\hat t)\), then \(\overline{\mathbf D}=E(\epsilon_n\cos\theta\,\hat n+\epsilon_t\sin\theta\,\hat t)\). Project along the imposed field and divide by \(E\):

\[\epsilon_E(\theta)=\epsilon_n\cos^2\theta+\epsilon_t\sin^2\theta .\]

The perpendicular displacement is \(E(\epsilon_t-\epsilon_n)\sin\theta\cos\theta\). It is generally nonzero. A device that instead fixes the direction of averaged displacement while allowing a transverse field measures \(\epsilon_D(\theta)=[\cos^2\theta/\epsilon_n+\sin^2\theta/\epsilon_t]^{-1}\). These two directional responses correspond to different constraints. They agree at principal axes and for isotropic material. A pair of unspecified “angled capacitor” measurements cannot be interpreted uniquely without describing the contacts and edge conditions.

At 45° in the example, the field-controlled projection gives about \(4.667\epsilon_0\), while the displacement-controlled projection gives \(4.286\epsilon_0\). These are both legitimate scalar ratios from one symmetric positive-definite tensor. Neither supplies a universal scalar permittivity for the rotated material. The complete vector response also predicts displacement tilt and field energy for any orientation.

Test the measurement against the unit-cell model

Open the live experiment

Change the volume fraction while keeping the constituent values fixed. At a pure phase all orientations and both control conditions must collapse to a single value. At intermediate fractions, find the angle where the transverse component would be largest. It occurs where \(|\sin\theta\cos\theta|\) is largest, but its magnitude also reflects the material contrast. Now reduce the contrast mentally to one and predict all curves without recomputing a single cell.

A numerical field model offers an independent homogenisation experiment. Use periodic tiling in the material directions and impose a macroscopic voltage difference plus periodic fluctuations on opposite faces. For a unit imposed field along each coordinate direction, average the resulting displacement over the cell. The resulting vectors supply the tensor columns. A grounded box on every side is a different boundary problem and can suppress the tangential response. Increase the number of material periods at fixed specimen scale and refine the individual period separately. These two limits test homogenisation and numerical representation, respectively.

How far can the effective law travel?

Electrostatic homogenisation assumes the cell fields respond quasistatically. In a wave experiment near the Bragg gap, phase changes substantially across one period and a local constant tensor is inadequate. A finely layered capacitor and a dielectric mirror can be made from the same constituents while requiring different models at different frequencies. Test the Bloch spectrum to identify the regime where layering begins selecting propagation bands. A low-frequency effective index alone cannot predict that gap.

Large spatial contrast also places a practical demand on mesh and interface handling. A normal gradient can jump strongly even when potential and displacement are continuous. A smooth-looking interpolated voltage surface is weak evidence for an accurate energy density. Compare constituent fields, total flux and energy consistency before accepting the extracted effective tensor.

Tutorial

Show that \(\epsilon_t-\epsilon_n\) is nonnegative for every positive pair of constituents without appealing to a named mean inequality.

Work through the solution

Put both terms over the positive denominator \(f\epsilon_2+(1-f)\epsilon_1\). The numerator after cancellation is \(f(1-f)(\epsilon_1-\epsilon_2)^2\), so the difference is nonnegative and vanishes for identical materials or a pure phase.

Equal fractions give measured relative values 6 tangentially and \(10/3\) normally. Can you infer both constituents if the laminate assumptions hold?

Work through the solution

Their sum is 12. The harmonic mean is \(2ab/(a+b)=10/3\), giving product 20. The positive roots of \(x^2-12x+20=0\) are 2 and 10. The data do not identify which label belongs to which phase without another observation.

Why can a constituent occupy very little volume yet strongly affect the normal response?

Work through the solution

Every normal field line in this lamination crosses each layer. The inverse response weights \(f/\epsilon\); even a thin, low-permittivity layer can contribute a large voltage drop. A field along the layers distributes displacement differently and may be much less sensitive to that same phase.

How a magnetic field rotates the polarisation of transmitted light

Fields and the response of matter

Resolve a linearly polarised wave into circular eigenmodes, infer a refractive-index difference from an analyser and distinguish nonreciprocal rotation from a simple change of reference axes.

Derive rotation from propagation rather than drawing an arrow

Let monochromatic light propagate along \(+z\) through a transparent magneto-optic sample, with an externally prepared axial magnetic field. Define fixed laboratory vectors \(\mathbf e_\pm=(\hat x\pm i\hat y)/\sqrt2\) and use phasors \(e^{i(kz-\omega t)}\). These definitions specify the handedness algebraically. Calling a vector simply “right circular” without stating the viewing and time conventions can reverse a reported rotation sign.

An input linearly polarised in \(x\) is \((\mathbf e_++\mathbf e_-)/\sqrt2\). Suppose the medium supports wave numbers \(k_\pm=2\pi n_\pm/\lambda_0\) for the two bases, with negligible absorption. After a distance \(L\), factor out the common phase \(e^{i(k_++k_-)L/2}\). The remaining Cartesian phasor is \(\hat x\cos\Delta-\hat y\sin\Delta\), where \(\Delta=(k_+-k_-)L/2\). Thus the polarisation angle in the fixed \(x,y\) plane is

\[\theta=\frac{\pi L}{\lambda_0}(n_- - n_+).\]

Equal transmissions of the two components preserve a linearly polarised output. Unequal absorption makes the circular amplitudes unequal and generally produces an ellipse. Rotating a linear analyser then finds a finite minimum, even for a perfectly coherent field. Residual intensity at the nominal extinction angle can also arise from stray light or imperfect polariser extinction, which must be measured independently.

Derive a magneto-optic eigenbasis

For axial magnetisation, a simple lossless transverse relative-permittivity block is \(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\epsilon&i g\\-i g&\epsilon\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) with real \(g\) at the frequency of observation. Acting on \((1,i)\) gives \((\epsilon-g)(1,i)\); acting on \((1,-i)\) gives \((\epsilon+g)(1,-i)\). For relative permeability near one, \(n_+^2=\epsilon-g\), \(n_-^2=\epsilon+g\). At small \(g\) their difference \(n_- - n_+\simeq g/\sqrt\epsilon\). Reversing magnetisation reverses the sign of the off-diagonal coefficient and interchanges the two eigenvalues. Both the existence of circular modes and the sign-reversal test now follow from a constitutive model.

If \(n_--n_+\) is proportional to magnetic field over a specified range, write \(\theta=VBL\), with Verdet constant \(V\) in rad/(T m). This is a measurable material and wavelength dependent coefficient. It is not determined by the mean refractive index alone. A strong nearby absorption line can give substantial frequency dependence and dichroism, invalidating a lossless narrow-band extrapolation.

Work through an analyser calibration

For \(\lambda_0=600\) nm, \(L=0.20\) mm and measured index difference \(5.0\times10^{-4}\), the rotation is \(\pi(2.0\times10^{-4})(5.0\times10^{-4})/(6.0\times10^{-7})=\pi/6\), or 30°. An ideal analyser at angle \(\alpha\) transmits \(I/I_\mathrm{in}=\cos^2(\alpha-\theta)\). Its maxima occur at \(30^\circ\) and \(210^\circ\); an intensity measurement determines a linear polarisation angle modulo 180°. For a sample thick enough to rotate several half turns, an intensity maximum alone cannot give an unwrapped \(\theta\). Vary field or path length continuously from a known reference.

If the applied field is independently 0.40 T in that example, \(V=\theta/(BL)\simeq6.54\times10^3\) rad/(T m). The arithmetic is dimensionally correct, but the large value warrants a physical check of thickness, field calibration, units and material spectrum before reporting it as an ordinary glass coefficient. Degrees substituted for radians would make the inferred material coefficient too large by \(180/\pi\).

Use field reversal to reject alignment errors

Open the live experiment

The experiment shows the predicted analyser traces for opposite index splitting, with the same input polariser. Increase sample thickness to see why tracking the maximum by one measurement can select the wrong branch. Reversing the splitting reverses the tilt in fixed laboratory axes. A fixed analyser offset shifts both inferred angles equally and cancels in half their difference, provided the branch has been unwrapped consistently.

For small rotation, an analyser exactly at a crossed position transmits intensity proportional to \(\theta^2\). Its response has no sign sensitivity close to zero. Set the analyser at 45° instead:

\[\cos^2(\pi/4-\theta)=[1+\sin(2\theta)]/2\simeq1/2+\theta.\]

The difference between fields \(+B\) and \(-B\) then measures a signed signal linear in small rotation. This trades extinction of the background for sensitivity to sign. Balance the two orthogonal analyser outputs if source intensity fluctuates appreciably during a sweep.

Ask what happens on a return pass

For the ideal Faraday medium at fixed magnetisation, the circular eigenmodes relative to a fixed laboratory basis are determined by its transverse material tensor. On propagation back through the sample, the accumulated positive propagation path again distinguishes the same laboratory circular basis after accounting for the reflection boundary. With a suitable simple mirror at normal incidence, the resulting material rotation doubles on a round trip in fixed transverse coordinates. In a reciprocal optically active material, the eigenvalue assignment with respect to the laboratory basis reverses on reversing propagation, and the single-pass rotation cancels on the ideal return. Write both transformations in one fixed basis before comparing signs.

A real double-pass setup includes mirror phase shifts, stress birefringence of windows and possible ellipticity. Reversing magnetic field, removing the sample and recording both analyser quadratures provide separate checks. A nonzero rotation in one transmitted beam proves only that its polarisation changed; establishing nonreciprocity requires comparing a reversed propagation experiment with a stated polarisation convention.

Tutorial

Two analyser maxima occur at 47° and 39° for opposite weak fields. If the response is linear and the same branch is tracked, what field-induced rotation and static offset do they imply?

Work through the solution

Half the difference gives 4 degrees of signed rotation for the first field relative to zero. The mean is 43 degrees, the combined input polarisation and analyser zero. Conversion to a Verdet constant additionally needs the applied field and path length.

In the 600 nm example, what thickness uncertainty corresponds to 1 degree of rotation uncertainty at fixed index difference?

Work through the solution

Rotation is proportional to thickness. Relative uncertainty \(1/30\) therefore gives about \(0.20/30=0.0067\) mm, or 6.7 μm. Uncertainty in wavelength or field would add additional contributions.

An ideal analyser gives a minimum 4% of its maximum. Can this be explained solely by a rotation of an ideal linearly polarised monochromatic input in a lossless circularly birefringent sample?

Work through the solution

Pure rotation preserves a linear state, so an ideal analyser should extinguish it completely. Differential loss, ellipticity, depolarisation, finite bandwidth or a detector background are possible alternatives. Use circular-analysis or full Stokes measurements and an empty-path baseline to distinguish them.

Compare with a full polarisation measurement

Use the Stokes reconstruction experiment to determine which intensities reveal a circular component. A fitted linear rotation alone cannot measure that missing component.

Adding angular momentum and predicting which lines can appear

Quantum states, dynamics and measurement

A three-component orbital wavefunction combines with a two-component spin. Derive the allowed total angular momenta, construct their amplitudes explicitly and predict both forbidden transitions and relative strengths in a polarisation-resolved spectrum.

Read the angular momentum algebra as a statement about rotations

Rotate a prepared quantum state through a small angle \(\delta\theta\) about a unit laboratory direction \(\mathbf n\). A continuous norm-preserving transformation has form \(U=I-i\delta\theta\,\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf J/\hbar+O(\delta\theta^2)\), with Hermitian generators \(J_i\). Rotations about different axes need not commute. Comparing the commutator of two infinitesimal rotations with the rotation around the third axis gives

\[[J_i,J_j]=i\hbar\epsilon_{ijk}J_k.\]

The sum \(J^2=J_x^2+J_y^2+J_z^2\) commutes with each component. One can therefore label eigenstates of \(J^2\) and one chosen component \(J_z\) simultaneously, while generally leaving the other components uncertain.

Define \(J_\pm=J_x\pm iJ_y\). Their commutators with \(J_z\) are \(\pm\hbar J_\pm\); with \(J^2\) they vanish. If \(J_z|m\rangle=\hbar m|m\rangle\), the two ladder operations change \(m\) by one without changing total angular momentum. To determine their coefficients, use \(J_-J_+=J^2-J_z^2-\hbar J_z\) and \(J_+J_-=J^2-J_z^2+\hbar J_z\). Nonnegative squared norms bound the ladder above and below. Let \(j\) be its maximum magnetic quantum number. Vanishing raising norm there fixes the \(J^2\) eigenvalue at \(\hbar^2 j(j+1)\); vanishing lowering norm at the bottom gives

\[m_{\min}=-j.\]

The number of unit steps between the two endpoints is \(2j\), so \(j\) is an integer or half-integer. With a consistent phase convention

\[J_\pm|j,m\rangle=\hbar\sqrt{j(j+1)-m(m\pm1)}\,|j,m\pm1\rangle .\]

Spatial orbital eigenfunctions on an ordinary single-valued scalar wavefunction carry integer orbital \(l\). Spin-\(\tfrac12\) has two states that cannot be modelled by adding another spatial coordinate angle to a scalar orbital. The operator algebra describes both. In \(|j,j\rangle\), \(J_z\) is definite but \(J_x,J_y\) fluctuate. Their variance sum is \(\hbar^2 j(j+1)-\hbar^2j^2=\hbar^2j\); rotational symmetry around \(z\) splits it evenly. Drawing a classical vector with precisely fixed components perpendicular to the axis would miss these fluctuations.

Construct a coupled basis from the stretched state

For independent angular momenta \(\mathbf L,\mathbf S\), the total generator is \(\mathbf J=\mathbf L+\mathbf S\). Operators on different factors commute. In the product basis \(|m_l,m_s\rangle\), \(J_z\) is diagonal with eigenvalue \(\hbar(m_l+m_s)\). The Hamiltonian may instead depend on \(J^2\), for example through \(\mathbf L\cdot\mathbf S\). Then states with the same \(m_l+m_s\) can mix. Resolving that degeneracy requires a unitary change of basis, whose entries are Clebsch–Gordan coefficients.

Take \(l=1,s=1/2\). The unique state \(m=3/2\) is \(|1,\uparrow\rangle\), so it must be \(|3/2,3/2\rangle\). Lower once with \(J_-=L_-+S_-\). The left side is \(\hbar\sqrt3|3/2,1/2\rangle\); the right side is \(\hbar(\sqrt2|0,\uparrow\rangle+|1,\downarrow\rangle)\). Normalising and constructing an orthogonal state in the same \(m\) plane gives

\[|3/2,1/2\rangle=\sqrt{2/3}|0,\uparrow\rangle+\sqrt{1/3}|1,\downarrow\rangle,\qquad\]
\[|1/2,1/2\rangle=\sqrt{1/3}|0,\uparrow\rangle-\sqrt{2/3}|1,\downarrow\rangle .\]

The sign of an entire coupled vector is conventional. The relative sign of its two components matters whenever a later measurement recombines amplitudes. Applying \(J_+\) to the second combination gives zero, which verifies the top of the \(j=1/2\) multiplet independently of orthogonality. Lowering produces four states for \(j=3/2\) and two for \(j=1/2\), exhausting the six-dimensional product space. Merely listing both possible \(j\)'s does not supply the amplitude with which a prepared product state belongs to each, which is what spectroscopy needs.

There is a counting derivation for arbitrary \(j_1,j_2\). Start with one stretched state at \(m=j_1+j_2\) and remove its entire ladder. The next highest unused \(m\) supplies the next multiplet and so on down to \(|j_1-j_2|\). The identity \(\sum_{j=|j_1-j_2|}^{j_1+j_2}(2j+1)=(2j_1+1)(2j_2+1)\) checks completeness. Distinct coupling schemes for three angular momenta are different bases of a common space. An intermediate angular momentum may be a reliable state label when a corresponding interaction dominates, yet cease to be conserved if an external field favours another axis or coupling.

Derive magnetic and parity selection rules

Consider a weak optical field coupled by the electric dipole interaction \(-\mathbf d\cdot\mathbf E\), where \(\mathbf d\) transforms as a vector and has odd parity. Its spherical components relative to \(z\) are

\[d_0=d_z,\ d_{\pm1}=\mp(d_x\pm i d_y)/\sqrt2.\]

For a definite tensor component \([J_z,d_q]=q\hbar d_q\). Sandwiching the commutator between initial and final \(J_z\) eigenstates yields

\[(m_f-m_i-q)\langle f|d_q|i\rangle=0.\]

Thus nonzero amplitudes require

\[m_f=m_i+q.\]

Coupling \(j_i\) with a rank-one tensor also requires \(|j_i-1|\le j_f\le j_i+1\), and \(0\to0\) is excluded. Parity gives a separate test: under inversion the matrix element gains factor \(-\pi_f\pi_i\), so it vanishes for equal initial and final parity in a parity-symmetric Hamiltonian. For purely orbital motion with parity \((-1)^l\), the triangle condition plus odd parity gives

\[\Delta l=\pm1.\]

A direction label on a photon depends on propagation and polarisation conventions. Stating the driven operator component \(q\) is less ambiguous than calling a beam “right circular” without defining the viewing direction.

For a spin-independent dipole transition the spin factor is \(\langle m_s'|m_s\rangle\). Begin in \(l_i=0,m_l=0,m_s=+1/2\) and drive \(q=0\) to \(l_f=1,m_l=0\). In the product basis there is one equally normalised orbital amplitude \(D\). Its projections on the two coupled final levels have squared amplitudes \(2|D|^2/3\) and \(|D|^2/3\), giving a 2:1 line-strength ratio under equal field intensity, comparable detection and negligible population saturation. For \(q=+1\) the product state is \(|1,\uparrow\rangle\). The transition to final \(j=1/2\) vanishes because no \(m=3/2\) belongs to that multiplet. These statements predict relative angular factors. Actual count rates can also depend on frequencies, radial matrix elements, initial populations and how fluorescence is collected.

As an explicit orbital check,

\[Y_{00}=1/\sqrt{4\pi},\]
\[Y_{10}=\sqrt{3/(4\pi)}\cos\theta.\]

The angular factor of \(d_0\propto r\cos\theta\) is \(\int Y_{10}^*\cos\theta Y_{00}\,d\Omega=1/\sqrt3\), while for \(Y_{1,\pm1}\) the azimuthal integral vanishes. A radial integral can still vanish accidentally even when all angular tests permit the transition. Symmetry rules predict zeros robust to changes preserving that symmetry; a permitted channel has an amplitude that must still be calculated.

Separate atomic structure from analyser orientation with a reduced matrix element

The angular selection rule from the \(J_z\) commutator identifies which transitions are forced to zero but does not alone give their relative strengths. A rotationally covariant way to connect the allowed amplitudes is to view an irreducible tensor component \(T_q^{(k)}\) as carrying angular momentum rank \(k\). Under rotations its \(2k+1\) components transform among themselves with the same representation matrices as the magnetic substates of an angular momentum \(k\). Coupling \(T_q^{(k)}\) to an initial \(|jm\rangle\) therefore furnishes the same product representation as coupling \(|jm\rangle\) to \(|kq\rangle\). The component projecting onto a final \(j'\) must transform as \(j'\). When this representation occurs once in the product, its relative magnetic amplitudes are fixed by Clebsch–Gordan coefficients up to one common factor independent of \(m,q,m'\).

In one standard convention the Wigner–Eckart theorem writes

\[\langle\alpha' j'm'|T_q^{(k)}|\alpha jm\rangle=\frac{\langle jm;kq|j'm'\rangle}{\sqrt{2j'+1}}\langle\alpha'j'\Vert T^{(k)}\Vert\alpha j\rangle .\]

Additional labels \(\alpha,\alpha'\) specify the radial, spin or configuration information absent from the angular labels. Conventions placing a different square-root factor in the reduced element are equally common, so a table of reduced strengths should be checked before combining it with an external coefficient table. The theorem predicts ratios within a fixed \(j,j'\) multiplet. It does not remove differences in radial integrals between unrelated configurations, nor does it assert the reduced element is nonzero. This factorisation explains why angular data alone may fix a branching ratio while offering no absolute radiative lifetime.

For a vector operator connecting \(j\) to the same \(j\), there is an especially transparent example. Within a given irreducible \(j\) space, any vector operator has matrix elements proportional to the corresponding \(J_q\) matrix elements, provided additional degeneracies are separated. For the conventional spherical components \(J_0=J_z\), \(J_{+1}=-J_+/\sqrt2\), ask for the ratio of line strengths out of \(j=2,m=1\) into \(m'=2\) via \(q=+1\) and into \(m'=1\) via \(q=0\), with equal field amplitudes in the specified components. The squared matrix elements of \(J_q\) are \(\hbar^2[6-1(2)]/2=2\hbar^2\) and \(\hbar^2\), hence the ratio is two. The same numerical angular ratio can apply to any appropriate nonzero reduced vector coupling between these specified manifolds. For an electric dipole transition parity must still allow the initial and final configurations; using \(J_q\) here computes angular ratios and does not turn angular momentum itself into an electric dipole operator.

Summing over a complete set of final magnetic substates and all tensor components supplies a useful normalisation audit. For a fixed initial \(m\), rotational invariance of the scalar sum \(\sum_q T_q^\dagger P_{j'}T_q\) makes its diagonal expectation independent of \(m\). A detector with a preferred polarisation or direction samples only a subset and may exhibit strong dependence on \(m\). This difference is essential in interpreting “unpolarised” line strengths: an unpolarised pump that populates all \(m\) equally, an unanalysed detector that sums final polarisations, and a source with a definite \(m\) viewed along one axis are three different experimental ensembles.

Compare successive Stern–Gerlach measurements with addition of spin

Two spin-\(1/2\) particles provide a minimal addition experiment with no spatial angular wavefunction needed. The coupled triplet basis is \(|1,1\rangle=|\uparrow\uparrow\rangle\),

\[|1,0\rangle=(|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle+|\downarrow\uparrow\rangle)/\sqrt2,\]
\[|1,-1\rangle=|\downarrow\downarrow\rangle;\]

the singlet is \((|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle-|\downarrow\uparrow\rangle)/\sqrt2\). An apparatus measuring the total \(z\) component alone cannot distinguish the two \(m=0\) states. Measuring \(J^2\) along with \(J_z\) can. Their local one-spin reduced matrices are both \(I/2\), so even complete tomography of either spin alone fails to reveal the difference. The distinction lies in a joint correlation.

The singlet projector can be obtained as a polynomial in \(\mathbf S_1\cdot\mathbf S_2\). That scalar has eigenvalues \(-3\hbar^2/4,+\hbar^2/4\), so \(P_s=I/4-\mathbf S_1\cdot\mathbf S_2/\hbar^2\). Expressing \(\mathbf S_i=(\hbar/2)\boldsymbol\sigma_i\) gives

\[P_s=[I-\sum_i\sigma_i\otimes\sigma_i]/4.\]

If three independently prepared ensembles yield measured same-axis correlations \((-0.80,-0.78,-0.82)\), their inferred singlet fraction is

\[(1+2.40)/4=0.85.\]

This result uses all three axes. Perfect \(z\)-anticorrelation alone constrains the population to a two-dimensional \(m=0\) space but says nothing about the relative phase within it. If each measurement has uncertainty 0.03 and the estimates are independent, the resulting statistical uncertainty in singlet fraction is \(\sqrt3(0.03)/4=0.013\), before calibration uncertainties.

For a coherent initial product \(|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\), a Hamiltonian \(H=J_{\rm ex}\mathbf S_1\cdot\mathbf S_2/\hbar^2\) evolves the singlet and triplet amplitudes with distinct phases. Writing the initial state as \((|1,0\rangle+|0,0\rangle)/\sqrt2\), the probability to find spins reversed later is \(\sin^2(J_{\rm ex}t/(2\hbar))\). The total \(m\) remains exactly zero at all times. If \(J_{\rm ex}/h=5.00\) MHz, the first full reversal occurs at \(t=1/(2\times5\,\mathrm{MHz})=100\) ns. An incoherent mixture with the same singlet and triplet populations has a constant reversal probability one half. This calculation makes the signs in the coupled basis observable and distinguishes a true exchange evolution from random spin flips inferred solely from individual populations.

Rotate the quantisation axis without changing the atomic Hamiltonian

A transverse magnetic or polarisation analyser can interrogate a state prepared relative to \(z\) along a different laboratory axis. For spin \(1/2\), \(e^{-i\theta S_y/\hbar}=\cos(\theta/2)I-i\sin(\theta/2)\sigma_y\), from \(\sigma_y^2=I\). To obtain the rotation probabilities for a spin-one state without memorising a rotation matrix, represent its stretched state as the symmetric product of two up spinors. Under a common rotation each spinor transforms as \(\cos(\theta/2)|\uparrow\rangle+\sin(\theta/2)|\downarrow\rangle\). Expanding the two-spin product gives the triplet amplitudes \(\cos^2(\theta/2),\sqrt2\cos(\theta/2)\sin(\theta/2),\sin^2(\theta/2)\) for the three \(m\) values. This construction exploits the representation and applies to any angular momentum one, regardless of its microscopic origin.

At \(\theta=\pi/2\), a stretched \(m=1\) state projected on the rotated axis gives probabilities \((1/4,1/2,1/4)\). The expectation of its rotated component vanishes, but outcomes \(+\hbar\) and \(-\hbar\) still occur and the variance is \(\hbar^2/2\). A source prepared in \(m=0\) along \(z\) gives a different test. Rotate its symmetrised up–down form on both spins and project on \(m=0\); the amplitude is \(\cos\theta\) and the probability \(\cos^2\theta\). At a right angle the “zero” outcome is absent, although the mean along either axis is zero. A single mean value cannot distinguish alignment from an unpolarised mixture. Measuring the full resolved substate distribution, or its quadrupole moment, can.

The general stretched state \(|j,j\rangle\) can be represented by \(2j\) symmetrised spinors all up. Its rotated \(m\) outcome distribution is binomial:

\[P(m)=\binom{2j}{j-m}\cos^{2(j+m)}(\theta/2)\sin^{2(j-m)}(\theta/2).\]

Its mean angular momentum component is \(\hbar j\cos\theta\), with variance \(\hbar^2(j/2)\sin^2\theta\). At fixed nonzero \(\cos\theta\), the relative fluctuation falls like \(j^{-1/2}\) as \(j\) grows. This supplies a precise sense in which a large stretched state can approximate a classical oriented angular momentum while retaining quantum projection noise. A state with the same large \(j\) but \(m=0\) need not resemble an oriented classical arrow. The representation dimension alone does not determine the suitability of a classical approximation.

Distinguish an angularly forbidden line from coherent cancellation

Suppose two degenerate lower magnetic states \(|g_1\rangle,|g_2\rangle\) both couple to one excited state \(|e\rangle\) under controlled coherent drives. In a rotating-frame model the raising part is \(|e\rangle(\Omega_1\langle g_1|+\Omega_2\langle g_2|)\hbar/2\), with the lowering term its adjoint. A normalised superposition proportional to \(\Omega_2|g_1\rangle-\Omega_1|g_2\rangle\) has zero excitation amplitude because the two terms cancel. Each angular channel can be individually allowed and strong, yet this particular coherent combination is dark. Its orthogonal bright state is proportional to \(\Omega_1^*|g_1\rangle+\Omega_2^*|g_2\rangle\) and has effective Rabi coupling \(\sqrt{|\Omega_1|^2+|\Omega_2|^2}\).

For equal real drives and an initially balanced antisymmetric superposition, the transition ideally vanishes. A randomly prepared mixture of the same two basis states populates the bright and dark subspaces equally. If a resonant pulse transfers the bright component completely, that mixture produces 50% excitation.
