Boundary conditions that carry a current around a ring

Quantum mechanics

An electron on an ideal narrow ring can sustain current in a stationary state. Work out how the topology of its configuration space and a magnetic flux through the hole select the allowed currents.

Recover the allowed state space from a boundary term

Wrap a coordinate \(s\) of length \(L\) around the ring. The points \(s=0\) and \(s=L\) label the same physical place. For a uniform one-dimensional kinetic operator, integration by parts shows that the difference between \(\langle\phi,H\psi\rangle\) and \(\langle H\phi,\psi\rangle\) is proportional to

\[[\,\phi^*\partial_s\psi-(\partial_s\phi)^*\psi\,]_{0}^{L}.\]

Periodic values and periodic first derivatives make this expression vanish for any two admissible states. They also make the probability current continuous at the join. A plane wave \(e^{iks}/\sqrt L\) then requires \(kL=2\pi n\) with integer \(n\). The labels \(n\) and \(-n\) have equal energy at zero flux but circulate oppositely. A real combination of the pair has zero net current. An energy measurement on its own does not tell us which combination was prepared.

Put flux through the hole

For a particle of charge \(q\), the kinetic momentum along the ring is \(-i\hbar\partial_s-qA_s\). Choose a constant tangential vector potential \(A_s=\Phi/L\) giving the required circulation \(\oint A_s ds=\Phi\). Define signed flux \(\varphi=q\Phi/h\). Periodic plane waves remain eigenfunctions, while their kinetic momenta and energies now change:

\[p_{\rm kin}=\frac{h}{L}(n-\varphi),\]
\[E_n=E_L(n-\varphi)^2,\]
\[E_L=\frac{h^2}{2mL^2}.\]

A gauge transformation can eliminate the constant \(A_s\) locally. It multiplies a wavefunction by a phase whose value after one circuit differs from its initial value by \(e^{-2\pi i\varphi}\) in this gauge. The transformed wavefunction must carry that twist at the join. Imposing both zero vector potential and unchanged periodicity would discard the physical flux dependence. Increasing \(\varphi\) by one reorders the spectrum by a shift of the integer labels.

Work out what a current measurement sees

One particle of velocity \(v=p_{\rm kin}/m\) goes around the ring in a time \(L/|v|\). The signed charge passing a fixed section per unit time is \(I=qv/L\). Differentiating \(E_n\) at fixed \(n\) gives the same result:

\[I_n=\frac{qh}{mL^2}(n-\varphi)=-\frac{\partial E_n}{\partial \Phi}.\]

Take \(\varphi=0.30\). In units of \(E_L\), the \(n=0\) energy is 0.09 and the \(n=1\) energy is 0.49. The currents in units of \(qh/(mL^2)\) are −0.30 and +0.70 respectively. A probe that selects the lower-energy branch here sees a circulating state even though its probability density is uniform around the ring.

At \(\varphi=0.50\) the two energies agree at \(0.25 E_L\). Following a given eigenstate through this crossing and allowing a system to relax into its lowest energy at every flux describe different experiments. An ideal isolated perfectly uniform ring conserves its angular momentum label. Weak imperfections or a reservoir can change the populations and modify the behaviour near degeneracy.

Inspect the neighbouring branches

Change the signed flux continuously. Compare the slope of the branch containing \(n=0\) with its current. Predict where the lowest pair changes ordering and check that the complete set repeats with unit flux.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a ring of another size

Double the circumference at fixed mass and flux while preparing the same integer state. Predict the changes of its wave number, energy and current.

Work through the solution

The canonical and kinetic wave numbers each halve because the integer label and signed flux are fixed. \(E_L\) scales as \(L^{-2}\), and so does the current unit \(qh/(mL^2)\). Energy and current both fall to one quarter. The flux period \(h/|q|\) stays unchanged.

A ring with fixed flux is enlarged to twice its original circumference without changing the particle mass. How do energy gaps and branch currents change? Does the location of a crossing in flux move?

Work through the solution

\(E_L=h^2/(2mL^2)\) falls by a factor of four. The current scale \(qh/(mL^2)\) falls by the same factor. The signed flux variable contains no circumference when total flux is held fixed, so crossings stay at half-integer flux. Holding the magnetic field fixed as ring area grows would instead change the enclosed flux.

At \(\varphi=0.50\), prepare an incoherent mixture with equal populations on \(n=0\) and \(n=1\). Calculate its current and energy. Can observing zero average current distinguish this mixture from zero flux?

Work through the solution

The currents are opposite, each of magnitude half the current unit, and the mean vanishes. Both energies are \(0.25E_L\). A zero-flux ground state also has zero current, but its energy is zero in the same calibration. Spectroscopy or a change of population separates the two cases.

Further reading · Tong, Particles in Magnetic Fields, gauge transformations and Aharonov–Bohm effect · Fließbach, Quantenmechanik, Eigenwerte und Eigenfunktionen.

Adiabatic following and a finite sweep through an avoided crossing

Quantum mechanics

Two unperturbed energies cross as a control rises. A nonzero coupling opens a gap. Whether a prepared particle follows the lower energy through the crossing depends on how quickly the eigenvectors rotate compared with the dynamical phase they accumulate.

Find the gap and the rotation rate

Let \(\hbar=1\) temporarily, with Hamiltonian \(H(t)=\begin{pmatrix}vt/2&g\\g&-vt/2\end{pmatrix}\), \(v>0\), \(g>0\). The fixed basis is called diabatic because each basis vector keeps its identity as the detuning \(vt\) changes sign. Squaring the Hamiltonian gives \([(vt/2)^2+g^2]I\); its energies are \(\pm h(t)\) with \(h=\sqrt{(vt/2)^2+g^2}\). The minimum gap is \(2g\). The corresponding Bloch axis points along \((g,0,vt/2)\); write its polar angle as \(\theta=\operatorname{atan2}(2g,vt)\).

Differentiating the angle gives

\[\dot\theta=-2gv/[(vt)^2+4g^2].\]

A spinor aligned with this axis rotates through half its geometric angle in Hilbert space. The magnitude of the off-diagonal derivative coupling is \(|\dot\theta|/2\), largest at the crossing where it equals \(v/(4g)\). Comparison with the minimum gap frequency \(2g/\hbar\) gives a local adiabatic ratio \(\hbar v/(8g^2)\). A slow sweep or large coupling suppresses transitions between instantaneous energy states. Following the lower adiabatic state exchanges the two fixed basis labels across the crossing.

Derive the fast-sweep scale independently

In the interaction picture of the diagonal part, the energy difference \(vt\) gives an accumulated relative phase \(vt^2/(2\hbar)\). First-order perturbation theory gives a transition amplitude proportional to \((g/\hbar)\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{ivt^2/(2\hbar)}dt\). The magnitude of this Fresnel integral is \(\sqrt{2\pi\hbar/v}\), obtained by continuing the convergent Gaussian integral from a positive real damping parameter. The leading probability to change fixed basis states is therefore \(2\pi g^2/(\hbar v)\), valid only when small. The exact infinite linear-sweep Landau–Zener solution resums this dependence: the probability of retaining the initial diabatic label is \(\exp[-2\pi g^2/(\hbar v)]\). It refers to endpoints asymptotically far from the avoided crossing with constant coupling and no decoherence.

For \(g=0.5\) and \(v=1\) in units where \(\hbar=1\), the infinite-sweep retention probability is

\[e^{-\pi/2}=0.2079.\]

Halving \(v\) squares it to 0.0432. The slow sweep therefore gives greater transfer between fixed basis states while preserving the lower-energy identity more faithfully. This distinction prevents reversing the interpretation of a measured probability.

Evolve a finite preparation numerically

Prepare the instantaneous ground state at detuning \(vt=-10\) and finish at \(+10\). Over each short step freeze \(H\) at its midpoint. Since \(H^2=h^2I\), the constant-step propagator is exactly \(\cos(h\Delta t)I-i\sin(h\Delta t)H/h\) in the chosen units. The product is unitary up to roundoff; finite time resolution still changes accumulated phases. Compare the population of the original fixed basis state with its population in the instantaneous ground state. Their separation through the crossing directly shows where ideal adiabatic following fails. The calculated final population can differ from the infinite-sweep expression because of endpoint mixing and phases.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on measurement choices

The coupling doubles while all else is fixed. How must the sweep rate change to leave the ideal infinite-sweep probability unchanged? Does a sweep over fixed detuning endpoints remain exactly equivalent?

Work through the solution

Keep \(g^2/v\) fixed by multiplying \(v\) by four. The infinite-sweep exponent is unchanged. At fixed endpoints the ratios \(|vt|/g\) at preparation and readout are now smaller, so the finite endpoints are less asymptotic. Their state mixing can change the observed result.

At zero detuning an energy measurement finds the lower eigenvalue with certainty. What would an immediate measurement in the fixed basis return?

Work through the solution

The lower eigenstate there is \((1,-1)/\sqrt2\) in the chosen convention. Each fixed-basis outcome therefore occurs with probability one half. Certainty in the instantaneous energy is compatible with maximal uncertainty concerning the diabatic label.

Further reading · Tokmakoff, time-dependent quantum mechanics and Landau–Zener transitions · Rubbmark and colleagues, experimental passage through an avoided crossing.

When a periodic dielectric has no propagating Bloch wave

Fields and the response of matter

Derive a band gap using two lossless transparent materials and distinguish evanescent Bloch propagation from absorption of optical power.

Construct a unit cell whose waves interfere

Stack two transparent, lossless, nonmagnetic dielectrics periodically in one dimension. Their refractive indices are \(n_1,n_2\), their physical thicknesses \(d_1,d_2\), and the period \(a=d_1+d_2\). Work at normal incidence, so polarisation directions are equivalent for isotropic layers. Each separate material admits a propagating wave at the chosen optical frequency, yet the periodic stack may not. The effect comes from coherent interface reflections, not from a negative refractive index or an absorptive constituent.

Describe the local field by its tangential electric and magnetic components. Propagation through one homogeneous layer connects them by a \(2\times2\) transfer matrix with diagonal \(\cos\phi_j\), off-diagonal terms proportional to \(iZ_j\sin\phi_j\) and \(i\sin\phi_j/Z_j\), where \(\phi_j=\omega n_jd_j/c\) and \(Z_j=Z_0/n_j\). Changing the chosen propagation direction or time convention may reverse the two imaginary signs together. The real trace and unit determinant of the cell matrix below are unaffected.

Read the dispersion relation from the trace

At an interface both tangential components are continuous in the absence of imposed sheet currents. Multiply the two propagation matrices to form one cell. Its determinant is one because each layer's determinant is

\[\cos^2\phi+\sin^2\phi=1.\]

Thus the two eigenvalues are reciprocal. For a Bloch mode they are \(e^{\pm i k_Ba}\) on a pass band. Taking half the trace gives

\[\cos(k_Ba)=\cos\phi_1\cos\phi_2-\frac12\left(\frac{n_1}{n_2}+\frac{n_2}{n_1}\right)\sin\phi_1\sin\phi_2 .\]

When the right side lies between −1 and 1 a real Bloch phase exists. If its magnitude exceeds one, a decaying and a growing Bloch factor replace a pair of unit-modulus factors. For a bounded excitation of a semi-infinite stack choose the decaying direction. The attenuation per cell is \(\operatorname{arcosh}|\operatorname{trace}(M)/2|\). A lossless semi-infinite crystal in a stop band reflects incident power. Spatial attenuation of a Bloch amplitude in this region is compatible with zero volumetric heat production.

Obtain a quantitative stop-band width

Choose \(n_1d_1=n_2d_2=\lambda_0/4\). Writing \(s=\omega/\omega_0\), both layer phases are \(\phi=\pi s/2\). At \(s=1\) the half trace is \(-\tfrac12(n_2/n_1+n_1/n_2)\). Recognising \(\cosh(\log r)=(r+r^{-1})/2\) gives centre attenuation \(|\log(n_2/n_1)|\) per cell. For \(n_1=1.5,n_2=2.0\) this is 0.2877, so a decaying eigencomponent has amplitude factor 0.75 per cell. This statement about an eigencomponent should not be confused with the exact transmittance of a specific finite stack, which also depends on entrance and exit impedances.

The first gap edges obey half trace −1. With \(\cos^2\phi=1-\sin^2\phi\), solve for the edge sine or cosine. The two edge phases are symmetric about \(\pi/2\) and satisfy

\[|\cos\phi|=|n_2-n_1|/(n_1+n_2).\]

Hence the fractional angular-frequency width is \((4/\pi)\arcsin(|n_2-n_1|/(n_1+n_2))\), about 0.183 for the example. The lowest gap width tends continuously to zero as index contrast vanishes. A gap in a plotted numerical spectrum at \(n_1=n_2\) would indicate an implementation or plotting problem.

Compare a frequency scan with a finite mirror

Open the live experiment

The graph plots half the transfer-matrix trace against normalised frequency and marks the admissible interval for a real Bloch phase. Change the index contrast first, keeping the layers quarter wave at the same reference frequency. The physical layer thickness adjusts in this family; it is a different experiment from changing a material while manufacturing all thicknesses fixed. Use the frequency control to read an attenuation exponent at the centre and just outside the gap.

At low frequency a wavelength spans many cells and a homogenised description can predict the long-wave speed. At the first gap the field changes phase by order one across a cell, exactly where the homogenisation scale separation fails. Conversely, an electromagnetic calculation showing multiple bands at very low frequency should be checked against a uniform-medium or long-wavelength effective-index limit. Compare a finite stack's reflection with the Bragg-mirror experiment. The bulk trace determines whether a propagating state can exist. A finite sample adds interference between boundaries and possible surface or defect effects.

Tutorial

If the two refractive indices become equal, simplify the trace formula for arbitrary thicknesses.

Work through the solution

The impedance factor becomes one, so the half trace is \(\cos\phi_1\cos\phi_2-\sin\phi_1\sin\phi_2=\cos(\phi_1+\phi_2)\). It never exceeds unit magnitude. Labelling a uniform medium by an artificial period folds the dispersion into zones but does not create a physical gap.

At the central quarter-wave frequency for index ratio 2, how many periods reduce a decaying Bloch component's amplitude by a factor of 100?

Work through the solution

The amplitude factor per cell is

\[e^{-\log2}=1/2.\]

Require \(2^{-N}\le0.01\), giving \(N\ge7\). A prediction for transmitted optical power also needs termination and impedance matching.

A measured stack exhibits both a transmission minimum and measurable heating. Can the minimum be attributed entirely to a band gap?

Work through the solution

A lossless band gap can reflect power without heating. Measure reflected and transmitted flux under consistent normalisation, then infer absorption from \(1-R-T\) after accounting for diffuse scattering and other exit channels. The bulk stop-band model and material absorption can coexist.

Compare with band calculations

MIT's mathematical nanophotonics course computes full periodic Maxwell modes. The one-dimensional trace above is a small benchmark with known pass and stop boundaries that a numerical eigenproblem should reproduce.

Measuring magnetic core loss by a hysteresis loop and by calorimetry

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

A closed loop on a graph of magnetic induction against field encloses an area with units of energy per unit volume. A thermometer measures kelvins. Bring these two observations onto the same scale, account for the winding heat, and determine when the two experiments actually test the same material loss.

Infer the field and the induction from independent windings

Wind a toroidal specimen with a primary winding of \(N_p\) turns and a lightly loaded pickup of \(N_s\) turns. Let \(A\) be the representative iron cross-sectional area, \(\ell\) the mean magnetic path length and \(V_c=A\ell\) the magnetic volume. Neglect a gap and leakage initially. Ampère's law around a path through the core gives

\[H\simeq N_pi_p/\ell.\]

This relation is accurate when the toroid is slender enough that the spread of path radii is small and the pickup current is negligible. In a thick toroid \(H(r)=N_pi_p/(2\pi r)\), so one point on a nominal loop can actually represent a range of local field values.

Faraday's law supplies the second observable. Choose winding polarity so the reported voltage is

\[v_s=N_sA\,dB/dt.\]

Integrating the voltage gives the change in cross-section-averaged magnetic induction. The pickup cannot determine a constant induction offset, because static flux induces no voltage. A closed loop area is insensitive to adding a constant to \(B\) or \(H\), but a dc offset in the voltage integrates into a time-dependent ramp and prevents the reconstructed trajectory from closing. In steady periodic operation one can subtract the voltage mean over a complete period before integration. Applying that correction to a genuinely evolving magnetisation process would remove part of a real flux change.

Multiply the two measured field quantities in the appropriate order. The field work supplied per unit material volume on a small induction change is \(H\,dB\). The source term associated with the magnetising flux is \(i_p v_{p,{\rm induced}}\,dt\). Using \(v_{p,{\rm induced}}\simeq(N_p/N_s)v_s\), its value is

\[i_pN_pA\,dB=V_cH\,dB.\]

Integrating around a settled cycle gives the magnetic loss

\[W_c=V_c\oint H\,dB,\]
\[P_c=fV_c\oint H\,dB.\]

The field can absorb positive work and return some of it later in the same cycle. The area measures the algebraic remainder. Reversing the polarity of a single signal flips the loop orientation. A passively heated specimen must receive positive net energy when signs are defined for power entering the core. Simply taking the absolute value of all instantaneous products would count reversible storage twice and can grossly overestimate the heat.

Work an elliptical loop from time-domain signals

Suppose a nearly sinusoidal field is \(H=H_p\cos\omega t\) and the induction lags it by a positive angle \(\delta\), \(B=B_p\cos(\omega t-\delta)\). Differentiate the latter and multiply by \(H\). Over one cycle \(\cos\omega t\sin\omega t\) averages to zero, and \(\cos^2\omega t\) averages to one half. The resulting mean power density is \(\omega H_pB_p\sin\delta/2\), giving \(\oint H\,dB=\pi H_pB_p\sin\delta\). With zero lag the two signals trace a line and the area vanishes, even if the peak values are large. A saturation-distorted loop need not be elliptical; the original time integral remains valid with synchronised data.

Take \(H_p=120\,{\rm A/m}\), \(B_p=0.200\,{\rm T}\) and \(\delta=12^\circ\). The energy per cycle and volume is \(15.68\,{\rm J/m^3}\). A toroid with \(\ell=0.20\,{\rm m}\), \(A=2.0\times10^{-4}\,{\rm m^2}\) and \(V_c=40\,{\rm cm^3}\) dissipates \(0.627\,{\rm mJ}\) per cycle. At \(f=200\,{\rm Hz}\) its power is \(0.125\,{\rm W}\). With \(N_p=100\), the necessary current peak is \(0.240\,{\rm A}\). With \(N_s=50\), the pickup voltage has peak \(N_sA(2\pi f)B_p=2.51\,{\rm V}\). These last two predictions allow the raw oscilloscope traces to be checked before a calculated loss in watts is trusted.

For sampled periodic data use \(w=\sum_n(H_n+H_{n+1})(B_{n+1}-B_n)/2\), including the segment from the last point back to the first. This trapezoidal line integral computes an oriented polygon area. Plot the starting and ending points before adding the closing segment. Connecting widely separated endpoints by hand hides drift and can generate a fictitious wedge whose area depends on acquisition timing. Repeat at higher sampling rates to see whether corners or switching features carry appreciable extra area. When a periodic excitation has harmonics the time increment must resolve the highest significant harmonic in both measured signals.

Compare a thermal measurement with the magnetic area

Consider first a calorimeter that receives heat only from the core. Let the heat capacity of specimen, holder and sensor be \(C_{\rm eff}\). Let the leakage conductance to an ambient bath be \(G\), and let \(\Delta T=T-T_a\). The thermal balance \(C_{\rm eff}\dot{\Delta T}=P_c-G\Delta T\) predicts a steady offset \(P_c/G\) and an initial heating slope \(P_c/C_{\rm eff}\). A small thermometer lag or poor thermal contact can make the earliest measured slope misleading even though environmental losses are then small. A separate electrical heater placed where the core deposits its heat can calibrate the effective thermal response.

For \(C_{\rm eff}=20\,{\rm J/K}\), \(G=0.015\,{\rm W/K}\), and a \(2.0\,{\rm K}\) rise above ambient, the magnetic loss \(0.125\,{\rm W}\) would give a slope of about \(0.00477\,{\rm K/s}\). The heat already leaking to the bath is \(0.030\,{\rm W}\), too large to discard in a ten-percent comparison. If the winding is inside the same calorimeter, its Ohmic heat must enter the balance as well. With primary resistance \(1.50\,\Omega\) and the computed sinusoidal current, winding heating is

\[(0.240/\sqrt2)^2(1.50)=0.0432\,{\rm W}.\]

The expected slope of the combined package at the same temperature is now \(0.00693\,{\rm K/s}\). Subtraction of winding heat recovers the core result only when the estimated resistance represents the winding at its operating temperature and frequency.

A thermal estimate drawn from a whole interval is often more robust than a derivative from two adjacent temperature samples. Integrating gives

\[Q_{\rm deposited}=C_{\rm eff}[\Delta T(t_2)-\Delta T(t_1)]+G\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\Delta T\,dt.\]

Divide by the duration for mean power, then remove other measured heat sources. A drifting ambient must be recorded explicitly because the calorimeter stores energy relative to its own absolute temperature. If the core and coil equilibrate slowly with each other, a single fitted heat capacity can depend on the fitting time window. A two-temperature model or a longer dwell then supplies an independent diagnostic.

Separate reversible exchange from hysteretic and induced currents

A single measured loop at finite frequency gives the total energy dissipated by the core at that prescribed waveform and temperature. The loop can include irreversible domain processes, eddy currents and other rate-dependent effects. It does not by itself split these contributions. To probe their different scaling hold the peak induction and waveform shape fixed, then change frequency. Under a restricted low-frequency model the quasi-static hysteresis energy per cycle is approximately constant, while classical thin-lamination eddy-current power scales as \(f^2B_p^2\). Its energy per cycle therefore scales roughly as \(f\). Plotting the measured loss per cycle and volume against \(f\) tests whether such an intercept-plus-slope description fits the chosen range.

Suppose the area is \(12\,{\rm J/m^3}\) at \(100\,{\rm Hz}\) and \(20\,{\rm J/m^3}\) at \(300\,{\rm Hz}\), at the same peak induction. A straight line gives intercept \(8\,{\rm J/m^3}\) and slope \(0.040\,{\rm J\,s/m^3}\). It predicts \(16\,{\rm J/m^3}\) at \(200\,{\rm Hz}\), near the elliptical-loop calculation. Two points always determine a line. A third and fourth frequency, together with uncertainties, are required to test the law. Different losses can share similar frequency scaling over a short interval, and temperature-dependent conductivity can bend a nominally simple curve.

Use the pickup to reject the reversible part of terminal power

Measuring primary terminal voltage and current with a power analyser provides a separate electrical route to loss, but the wanted number can be a small difference between terms. The applied voltage contains winding resistance, induced magnetising voltage, leakage-inductance voltage and possible parasitic contributions. In steady periodic operation the energy stored in a fixed linear leakage inductance returns after each cycle, while winding resistance dissipates energy permanently. The secondary pickup preferentially measures the core-linked flux. Forming \(p_c(t)=(N_p/N_s)i_p(t)v_s(t)\) and averaging over a full cycle estimates core power without directly including the primary's copper voltage drop. It relies on both windings linking the same relevant flux and on negligible power drawn by the pickup.

For the elliptical example the primary magnetising voltage has peak \(5.03\,{\rm V}\) and leads the induction by ninety degrees. Its phase relative to the current is

\[90^\circ-\delta=78^\circ.\]

The core branch's apparent power is \(V_{\rm rms}I_{\rm rms}\approx0.603\,{\rm VA}\); its mean dissipative part is about \(0.125\,{\rm W}\), and the reactive part is about \(0.590\,{\rm var}\). Only the mean part contributes net heating of the magnetic volume. Including the winding voltage adds about \(0.043\,{\rm W}\) of real power to the terminal reading. A simple product of peak current and peak secondary voltage, without the turns ratio, factor one half and phase factor, has no reason to agree with either measured loss.

Consider a nearly ideal core with the same peak signals but a lag of only one degree. Its loss is about \(10.5\,{\rm mW}\), while the much larger reversible power exchange is almost unchanged. A time offset of \(10\,\mu{\rm s}\) at \(200\,{\rm Hz}\) adds \(0.72^\circ\), causing a relative loss error of approximately \(72\%\) for that small lag. Calibrate the relative channel delays using a reference signal through the actual current transducer and voltage chain, including their filters. Adjusting traces on screen until a core loop appears narrow assumes the very loss one is trying to measure. A synchronous measurement over many periods suppresses random noise but does not remove a persistent delay bias.

Test reconstruction on a waveform with a third harmonic

Let \(H(t)=H_1\cos\omega t+H_3\cos3\omega t\) while \(B(t)=B_1\cos(\omega t-\delta_1)+B_3\cos(3\omega t-\delta_3)\). With these definitions \(\delta_n\) is the phase lag of the \(n\)th harmonic in its own sinusoid. Multiplying \(H\) by \(\dot B\) and integrating over an integer number of fundamental periods eliminates products of different harmonics. Each surviving harmonic contributes the number of its own cycles in one fundamental period. The area per fundamental period is therefore \(\pi[H_1B_1\sin\delta_1+3H_3B_3\sin\delta_3]\). This identity tests whether a numerical area calculation tracks the full waveform. It does not assume that a real material is linear; here the two signals are supplied as measured periodic functions.

Use the earlier fundamental values and add \(H_3=20\,{\rm A/m}\), \(B_3=0.030\,{\rm T}\) and \(\delta_3=30^\circ\). The third harmonic contributes \(2.83\,{\rm J/m^3}\) in each fundamental period. This represents an additional \(0.0226\,{\rm W}\) for the chosen specimen and frequency. A fundamental-only sine fit would miss nearly fifteen percent of the resulting total area. An apparent jagged loop can also arise from asynchronous sampling or an offset. Distinguish genuine harmonics by checking that they are phase locked to the drive and persist under a change in sampling rate and analogue bandwidth.

Sweep phase and read the oriented loop

The live experiment reconstructs the \(H,B\) trajectory and compares it with a zero-lag reference. It uses the same peak field and induction for the two curves. At fixed peaks changing frequency leaves an assigned phase lag and therefore the prescribed energy per cycle unchanged, while watts rise with the number of cycles per second. A real core can change its lag and waveform with frequency; the graph separates this independent variable so that the meaning of the two scaled quantities is clear. Add a timing offset and predict the direction of the area error before checking its reported size. Near zero lag even the direction of an apparent loop can be controlled by a small electronic offset.

Open the live experiment

Two toroids are made of the same nominal material with equal cross sections but different mean path lengths. How should a result reported only as watts be compared?

Work through the solution

Determine actual magnetic volume for each toroid and calculate the energy per unit volume per cycle, \(P/(fV_c)\). Also check that both experienced the same induction waveform, frequency and temperature. Equal primary currents need not give equal field when the path lengths differ; equal primary applied voltages need not give equal core induction if turns, winding voltage drops or area differ. Reproducing the actual \(B(t)\) with feedback on the pickup is a more direct way to match excitation. Report dimensions and peak induction with the result so a reader can reconstruct what material condition was tested.

A saturated sample shows a small loop during a biased excitation that never crosses zero induction. Does the area formula still apply?

Work through the solution

A settled periodic minor loop encloses an area representing the net cyclic energy under exactly that bias and excursion. Adding a static induction offset does not change \(\oint H\,dB\), but the bias can alter the trajectory, differential permeability, and width of the minor loop itself. Do not infer the full major-loop energy from its area. The pickup returns only changes of induction unless an independent dc measurement or a known magnetic preparation supplies the offset. A bias winding may additionally heat the calorimeter, so include its measured power when comparing with thermal loss.

Tutorial on comparing two loss measurements

A data logger reports \(H_p\) and \(B_p\) correctly but adds an uncorrected \(10\,\mu{\rm s}\) delay to the induction channel. Estimate the fractional area error of the elliptical example.

Work through the solution

At \(200\,{\rm Hz}\) the extra apparent lag is

\[2\pi f\Delta t=0.01257\,{\rm rad}=0.72^\circ.\]

The reported area is multiplied by \(\sin(12.72^\circ)/\sin(12^\circ)\), about \(1.059\). An uncorrected delay of only one hundred-thousandth of a second overstates the core loss by about six percent. For a very low-loss specimen with much smaller physical lag, the same delay produces a far larger relative error.

A thermal run on the combined winding and core reports a slope \(0.0070\,{\rm K/s}\) at \(\Delta T=2.0\,{\rm K}\). Recover the core power and the volume-specific loss area at \(200\,{\rm Hz}\).

Work through the solution

Heat deposition in the package is

\[20(0.0070)+0.015(2.0)=0.170\,{\rm W}.\]

Subtract the estimated \(0.0432\,{\rm W}\) of winding heating to obtain \(0.1268\,{\rm W}\) in the core. Dividing by \(200\times4.0\times10^{-5}\) gives \(15.85\,{\rm J/m^3}\) per cycle. Its closeness to \(15.68\,{\rm J/m^3}\) should be evaluated against the uncertainties of heat capacity, thermal conductance, resistance, dimensions and electrical phase alignment.

Vary the magnetic circuit and identify the induction scale used by the specimen.

From a Coulomb orbit to the fine-structure multiplet

Quantum states, dynamics and measurement

Separate the hydrogenic Schrödinger equation, identify the origin of its large degeneracy and evaluate the three dominant relativistic corrections. The cancellation among them predicts an equality that spectroscopy can then test.

Separate centre-of-mass and relative motion

A light particle of mass \(m_e\) and charge \(-e\) interacts with a pointlike nucleus \(+Ze\) of mass \(M\). Use centre of mass \(\mathbf R=(m_e\mathbf r_e+M\mathbf r_N)/(m_e+M)\) and separation \(\mathbf r=\mathbf r_e-\mathbf r_N\). Expanding the sum of the two kinetic energies cancels all cross terms and gives a free centre-of-mass kinetic energy plus relative energy \(p^2/(2\mu)\), with reduced mass \(\mu=m_eM/(m_e+M)\). The relative potential is \(-k/r,\ k=Ze^2/(4\pi\epsilon_0)\). Its rotational symmetry implies \([H,L_i]=0\). Use a spherical harmonic \(Y_{lm}\) and write \(\psi=u(r)Y_{lm}/r\), so the probability in a thin radial shell is \(|u(r)|^2dr\) for angular harmonics of unit norm.

Substituting in the Laplacian separates the radial equation

\[-\frac{\hbar^2}{2\mu}u''+\left[\frac{\hbar^2l(l+1)}{2\mu r^2}-\frac{k}r\right]u=Eu .\]

For the regular three-dimensional solution \(u(0)=0\) and a bound state also decays at infinity. The centrifugal term is an effective energy associated with angular variation, not a separate force added by hand. For \(E<0\) define \(\kappa=\sqrt{-2\mu E}/\hbar,\ \rho=2\kappa r\), and \(a=\hbar^2/(\mu k)\). The small-radius behaviour of \(u\) is \(r^{l+1}\); the large-radius tail contains \(e^{-\kappa r}\). Pull both out by \(u=\rho^{l+1}e^{-\rho/2}v(\rho)\). Straight differentiation leaves

\[\rho v''+(2l+2-\rho)v'+(1/\kappa a-l-1)v=0 .\]

For \(v=\sum c_j\rho^j\), coefficient matching gives

\[c_{j+1}/c_j=(j+l+1-1/\kappa a)/[(j+1)(j+2l+2)].\]

The regular series must terminate to avoid an exponential asymptotic part that defeats the decaying prefactor. Thus \(1/(\kappa a)=n_r+l+1=n\), and \(E_n=-\mu k^2/(2\hbar^2 n^2)\). The normalised radial polynomials are associated Laguerre functions. This gives \(l=0,\ldots,n-1\) and \(\sum_l(2l+1)=n^2\) states before including spin. Rotational symmetry guarantees the degeneracy in \(m\), but a different central potential can split levels of different \(l\). The additional equality is specific to the Coulomb potential.

Calculate the radial moments needed for a correction

To isolate the leading relativistic corrections now take an infinitely heavy nucleus and set \(m=m_e\), \(a=\hbar^2/(mk)\). Including reduced mass in the leading energy is straightforward; a complete recoil correction to the fine structure requires additional interactions. A useful identity is \(\langle1/r\rangle=1/(an^2)\), obtained by differentiating \(E_n\) with respect to \(k\). The derivative of an eigenvalue equals the expectation of \(\partial H/\partial k=-1/r\) for the normalised eigenstate, which follows by differentiating \(H|\psi\rangle=E|\psi\rangle\) and cancelling terms using its adjoint. The same method applied to the radial centrifugal parameter yields \(\langle r^{-2}\rangle=1/[a^2n^3(l+1/2)]\) at fixed radial node count. Treat \(l\) as a continuously varied radial parameter during differentiation, then evaluate at its physical integer value.

For \(l>0\), the stationary radial force identity \(\langle dV_{\rm eff}/dr\rangle=0\) with vanishing endpoint term gives

\[\langle r^{-3}\rangle=1/[a^3n^3 l(l+1/2)(l+1)].\]

Here \(V_{\rm eff}\) is the potential in the radial equation. The step uses sufficient behaviour of \(u,u'\) at the origin. For \(l=0\), the integral of \(r^{-3}\) diverges in an \(s\) state and the neglected endpoint term need not vanish. Substituting \(l=0\) formally into this expression would therefore be invalid. The contact density of an \(s\) state needed below is \(|\psi_{n00}(0)|^2=1/(\pi a^3 n^3)\), while states with \(l>0\) vanish at the origin.

Track the kinetic, spin–orbit and contact terms separately

The small parameter is \(Z\alpha=k/(\hbar c)\), with \(\alpha\) the fine-structure constant for \(Z=1\). Expand the relativistic dispersion about small momentum after subtracting rest energy: \(p^2/(2m)-p^4/(8m^3c^2)+\cdots\). For an energy eigenfunction \(p^2\psi=2m(E_n+k/r)\psi\), so \(\langle p^4\rangle=4m^2\langle(E_n+k/r)^2\rangle\) follows by taking the squared norm of \(p^2\psi\). Squaring the two operators as if \(p^2\) and \(1/r\) commute is unnecessary and would not be an operator identity. Inserting the first two radial moments gives

\[\Delta E_{\rm kin}=-C_n\left[\frac1{l+1/2}-\frac3{4n}\right],\]
\[C_n=\frac{mc^2(Z\alpha)^4}{2n^3}.\]

The Pauli expansion of the Dirac Hamiltonian adds \(H_{\rm so}=(1/[2m^2c^2r])V'(r)\mathbf L\cdot\mathbf S\) and the Darwin contact \(H_D=\hbar^2\nabla^2 V/(8m^2c^2)\). These are effective interactions through order \((Z\alpha)^4\); a purely nonrelativistic scalar equation does not generate the spin or contact term. For \(V=-k/r\), \(V'=k/r^2\) and \(\nabla^2 V=4\pi k\delta^3(\mathbf r)\). Thus the contact shifts only \(s\) states by \(+C_n\). For \(l>0\) insert the third inverse radial moment and the angular identity \(\mathbf L\cdot\mathbf S=\hbar^2[j(j+1)-l(l+1)-3/4]/2\). For the branch \(j=l+1/2\) the bracket is \(l\); for \(j=l-1/2\) it is \(-(l+1)\). Reducing fractions then gives

\[\Delta E_{\rm so}=C_n[1/(l+1/2)-1/(j+1/2)].\]

For \(l=0\) the spin–orbit operator vanishes and the Darwin term supplies the required difference. The sum in either case is

\[\Delta E_{nj}=-C_n\left[\frac1{j+1/2}-\frac3{4n}\right].\]

At fixed \(n\) the answer depends on \(j\) but no longer separately on \(l\). The cancellation cannot be inferred by merely attaching a phenomenological \(\mathbf L\cdot\mathbf S\) splitting to a nonrelativistic \(p\) state. In particular it predicts identical energies for \(2s_{1/2}\) and \(2p_{1/2}\) in this approximation, while \(2p_{3/2}\) lies higher. Radiative, recoil and finite-nuclear-size contributions can resolve some of these residual equalities. An experimentally resolved residual is evidence that the specified Hamiltonian omitted a physical term; it does not remove the cancellation inside that Hamiltonian.

Compare a small energy correction with a resolvable line

For hydrogen with \(mc^2\simeq511\,000\) eV and \(\alpha\simeq1/137.04\), the \(2p_{3/2}\)–\(2p_{1/2}\) interval is \(C_2/2\simeq4.53\times10^{-5}\) eV or \(10.95\) GHz. Compare with the leading \(n=2\) binding energy 3.40 eV. A small correction to total binding can give a prominent splitting when a measurement subtracts nearly equal large energies. Resolving it in a Fourier-limited time-domain experiment requires a coherent observation time at least of order the inverse frequency difference, here around 0.09 ns. Actual linewidths, lifetimes and pulse shapes affect how two spectral components can be distinguished.

A useful numerical audit is to fit the radial spectrum with three successive physical models. First solve the nonrelativistic radial boundary problem with the correct near-origin power and check that 2s and 2p converge toward the same energy. Then evaluate expectation values of the three corrections on those numerical states. Finally combine terms. Converged binding energies alone do not guarantee a converged contact density, which samples one spatial endpoint. A coarse radial grid can imitate an anomalous splitting between states which the analytical correction predicts to be equal. Check the origin slope of \(u_{n0}\), not only its norm or far tail.

Find where the electron is most likely to be detected

Confusion between spatial density and radial probability can lead to very different-looking descriptions of the same atom. For a hydrogenic ground state the normalised position-space wavefunction is \(\psi_{100}=(\pi a^3)^{-1/2}e^{-r/a}\). Its density is largest at the centre, but a thin spherical shell at radius \(r\) contains probability \(P(r)dr=4r^2 e^{-2r/a}dr/a^3\). The shell volume tends to zero at the origin. Differentiating \(\ln P\) gives \(2/r-2/a=0\), so the most likely shell radius is \(a\). The mean radius \(\int rP(r)dr=3a/2\) is different again because the shell distribution has a long tail. Neither number alone gives the probability that a finite spatial detector clicks.

Integrating the shell probability between zero and \(R\) gives

\[\Pr(r<R)=1-e^{-2R/a}\left(1+\frac{2R}a+\frac{2R^2}{a^2}\right).\]

At the most likely shell radius \(R=a\), the probability inside is \(1-5e^{-2}=0.3233\), substantially less than one half. At \(R=2a\) it is

\[1-13e^{-4}=0.7619.\]

Taylor expansion at a small radius \(R\ll a\) gives \(4(R/a)^3/3\), equal to the ball volume times central density to leading order. This is the local scaling underlying finite-nucleus corrections to a contact-sensitive observable. Using the most likely radius as though the electron followed a thin circular orbit would give none of these cumulative probabilities, even though the same Coulomb length scale appears in the Bohr model.

For \(2s\) and \(2p\), both states have principal index two and the same leading energy. The \(2s\) radial amplitude is proportional to \((2-r/a)e^{-r/(2a)}\); its node at \(2a\) separates regions of opposite amplitude sign. The \(2p\) radial amplitude is proportional to \(r e^{-r/(2a)}\), with no positive-radius radial node. At short distance, the probability inside \(R\) scales as \(R^3\) for a nonzero \(s\) amplitude and as \(R^5\) for the \(p\) wave. A sufficiently localised weak perturbation near the nucleus can therefore split the nonrelativistic degeneracy even if it is rotationally symmetric. Rotational symmetry still protects equal shifts within the \(m\) multiplet. A radial perturbation and a field selecting a direction leave different patterns of protected degeneracy.

Diagonalise a degeneracy before expanding a weak electric field

The \(n=2\) Coulomb degeneracy creates a striking response to a weak uniform electric field along \(z\). In the purely nonrelativistic model with spin ignored the relevant spatial states are \(2s,\ 2p_0,\ 2p_{\pm1}\). The perturbation for an electron in electric field \(\mathcal E\hat z\) can be written \(e\mathcal E z\) up to an additive constant. Each unperturbed parity eigenstate has zero diagonal expectation of this odd operator. Applying ordinary nondegenerate first-order perturbation theory separately to all four states would therefore predict zero for every shift. But first-order perturbation theory in a degenerate manifold requires diagonalising the projected perturbation, including off-diagonal amplitudes.

The \(z=r\cos\theta\) operator has \(q=0\) and changes parity, so within \(n=2\) it couples only \(2s\) and \(2p_0\). Its angular matrix element between \(Y_{00}\) and \(Y_{10}\) is \(1/\sqrt3\). In a conventional phase convention the radial functions are \(R_{20}=(2-r/a)e^{-r/(2a)}/(2\sqrt2\,a^{3/2})\) and \(R_{21}=(r/a)e^{-r/(2a)}/(2\sqrt6\,a^{3/2})\). The remaining radial integral is \(\int r^3R_{20}R_{21}\,dr\). Setting \(s=r/a\) gives

\[a(2\cdot4!-5!)/(8\sqrt3)=-3\sqrt3a.\]

Multiplying the angular factor gives \(-3a\). Rephasing one basis state would change this sign but leave all level shifts invariant.

The projected matrix has zero diagonal and off-diagonal \(-3ea\mathcal E\). Its symmetric and antisymmetric combinations have linear shifts \(\mp3ea\mathcal E\), while \(2p_{\pm1}\) remain unshifted to this order. The new states have definite first-order dipoles along opposite directions even though their zero-field basis states had zero dipoles. For \(a=5.29\times10^{-11}\) m and \(\mathcal E=1000\) V/m, an individual linear shift has magnitude \(1.59\times10^{-7}\) eV, corresponding to 38.4 MHz. This field is weak compared with the gross level separation, but whether the degenerate result is accurate in a real atom also depends on much smaller fine, hyperfine and radiative splittings. “Weak field” must specify the comparison energy.

A more precise two-state model retains a zero-field splitting \(\Delta\) between the coupled states. After subtracting their mean the Hamiltonian is \((\Delta/2)\sigma_z+d\mathcal E\sigma_x\), with \(d=3ea\) in magnitude. The energies are \(\pm\sqrt{(\Delta/2)^2+d^2\mathcal E^2}\). At \(d|\mathcal E|\ll|\Delta|\) the shift from each initial level is quadratic, \(\pm d^2\mathcal E^2/|\Delta|\), while at \(d|\mathcal E|\gg|\Delta|\) the slopes approach the linear-degenerate prediction. An observed crossover distinguishes a small unresolved internal splitting from a strictly zero projected dipole matrix element. Tracking energies over both positive and negative fields also tests for a stray dc field shifting the centre of the response.

Estimate a finite-size correction from the potential itself

The contact term derived from the Pauli expansion is not the same physical effect as giving the nucleus a finite charge radius. A simple electrostatic model separates the two scalings. Replace a point nucleus by a uniformly charged sphere of radius \(R_N\). Inside it the electron potential becomes

\[V_{\rm sph}(r)=-k[3-(r/R_N)^2]/(2R_N);\]

outside it is unchanged at \(-k/r\). The perturbation \(\delta V=k[1/r-3/(2R_N)+r^2/(2R_N^3)]\) is positive for interior points and zero at the surface. The smoothed potential is less attractive than the point law near the centre.

For an \(s\) state and \(R_N\ll a\), the unperturbed density varies little across the tiny sphere. First-order perturbation theory therefore gives

\[\delta E\simeq4\pi k|\psi(0)|^2\int_0^{R_N}\left(r-\frac{3r^2}{2R_N}+\frac{r^4}{2R_N^3}\right)dr\]
\[\frac{2\pi}{5}\,k|\psi(0)|^2R_N^2 .\]

The first two radial integrals cancel and leave the \(R_N^2/10\) contribution before multiplication by \(4\pi\). Equivalently write \(\delta E=(2\pi/3)k|\psi(0)|^2\langle r_N^2\rangle\), since the uniform sphere has mean-square charge radius \(3R_N^2/5\). The result is a positive shift, decreasing binding. Corrections to the constant-density approximation involve additional powers of \(R_N/a\). A different microscopic charge distribution changes higher moments, while the leading small-size shift depends on its mean-square charge radius.

For a model \(R_N=1.0\) fm and the ordinary electronic hydrogen length \(a=5.29\times10^{-11}\) m, \(R_N/a=1.89\times10^{-5}\). For the ground state compare with \(|E_1|=k/(2a)\) and \(|\psi(0)|^2=1/(\pi a^3)\). The relative shift is \((4/5)(R_N/a)^2=2.86\times10^{-10}\), equivalent to roughly \(3.9\times10^{-9}\) eV. This illustrative uniform charge radius is input data of the model; do not equate it directly to a measured rms radius. For a heavier orbiting lepton a shorter Bohr radius increases the fractional sensitivity strongly. A complete precision spectrum would additionally include radiative and recoil effects and a realistic nuclear response. The present derivation isolates how one local perturbation scales and why \(s\) levels are especially sensitive.

Use dilation to audit stationary Coulomb energies numerically

A variational scaling argument gives a powerful independent check on a computed wavefunction. For any normalised trial state \(\psi\), define the normalised rescaling \(\psi_\lambda(\mathbf r)=\lambda^{3/2}\psi(\lambda\mathbf r)\). The Coulomb potential is homogeneous of degree minus one, and the gradient acquires a factor \(\lambda\). Its energy expectation is therefore

\[E(\lambda)=\lambda^2\langle T\rangle+\lambda\langle V\rangle.\]

For an exact stationary bound state the energy functional is stationary with respect to norm-preserving variations, giving \(2\langle T\rangle+\langle V\rangle=0\) at \(\lambda=1\). Hence \(\langle T\rangle=-E,\ \langle V\rangle=2E\), consistent with the independently derived inverse-radius mean. The argument requires appropriate normalisation and boundary behaviour so the scaled trial function is allowed.

Suppose a numerical \(1s\) reconstruction yields \(\langle T\rangle=13.2\) eV and \(\langle V\rangle=-26.7\) eV. Its energy is \(-13.5\) eV, yet its virial residual is \(-0.3\) eV. Along the scale family the minimum occurs at \(\lambda_*=-\langle V\rangle/(2\langle T\rangle)=1.0114\), a slightly more concentrated state. The resulting energy reduction is only about 0.0017 eV, much smaller than the change in individual kinetic and potential terms. A small total-energy error can coexist with a noticeably incorrect radial scale because first-order energy errors cancel near a stationary solution. Monitoring separate expectation values, the virial residual and a contact-sensitive observable provides a more informative test of a numerical atomic calculation than reporting the total binding energy alone.

Distinguish orbital density from radial probability

Compare radial states at the same principal quantum number. Inspect their nodes before considering which additional interactions can lift their degeneracy.

Open this experiment online

Appraise a finite-grid Coulomb spectrum from convergence and degeneracy

To compute the radial Schrödinger eigenvalues directly, work in dimensionless radius \(s=r/a\), energy unit \(\hbar^2/(2ma^2)\), and write \([-d^2/ds^2+l(l+1)/s^2-2/s]u=\varepsilon u\). On a uniform grid \(s_j=jh\), use \(u_0=0\) and a far boundary \(u_{N+1}=0\). The diagonal approximation is \(2/h^2+l(l+1)/s_j^2-2/s_j\) and its neighbours are \(-1/h^2\). All potential terms are evaluated at positive interior grid points. Inserting an arbitrary finite value for \(1/s\) at the origin and treating it as an ordinary interior point changes the boundary approximation. A tridiagonal symmetric eigensolver returns orthogonal numerical vectors; physical radial norm is \(h\sum_j|u_j|^2\), so normalise appropriately before reconstructing spatial densities.

For \(l=0\) on an infinite grid the lowest mode permits an unusually informative exact lattice check. Try \(u_j=j q^j\) for \(0<q<1\). The finite-difference kinetic row divided by this trial value is \([2-q-q^{-1}]/h^2-(q-q^{-1})/(jh^2)\). Adding potential \(-2/(jh)\), the \(1/j\) coefficient vanishes when \(q^{-1}-q=2h\), or \(q=\sqrt{1+h^2}-h\). The eigenvalue is then

\[\varepsilon_h=[2-2\sqrt{1+h^2}]/h^2=-2/[1+\sqrt{1+h^2}].\]

It tends to the exact continuum ground value \(-1\) as \(-1+h^2/4+O(h^4)\). This analytic discrete answer separates algebraic solver correctness from discretisation error for the exact same potential and boundary treatment.

At \(h=0.20\) the infinite-grid ground value is approximately \(-0.99020\); at \(h=0.10\) it is \(-0.99751\). Their errors differ by nearly a factor of four as predicted. A finite far wall shifts both energies further; repeat first at fixed \(h\) and increasing boundary radius to isolate that effect. An excited \(n=2\) state has a more extended tail and requires a larger radius than the ground state for comparable truncation bias. Solving \(l=0\) and \(l=1\) on a small common box can yield a spurious nonrelativistic 2s–2p splitting. Check separately that both energies tend to \(-1/4\) before interpreting a much smaller physical fine splitting. Subtracting independently computed energies amplifies absolute numerical biases even if each number appears visually close to its expected value.

The distinctive near-origin behaviour supplies a second check. Since \(\psi_{100}(0)\) is proportional to the slope of the reduced radial function at zero, a first forward difference \(u_1/h\) typically incurs a first-order error even when the energy error is second order. For the lattice shape above \(u_j\propto j q^j\), the first two positive grid values can be used to estimate the endpoint derivative by \((4u_1-u_2)/(2h)\). Test its convergence under the physical radial normalisation. For a contact perturbation, squaring an endpoint derivative doubles its small relative error to leading order. This is why an atomic calculation should include an observable-specific refinement criterion in addition to an energy tolerance.

Use an eigenvalue benchmark to separate numerical and physical shifts

A report on the infinite-grid hydrogenic ground state quotes energy \(-0.99750\) at \(h=0.10\) and attributes the difference from \(-1\) to a short-range interaction. Predict the lattice-only change on refinement to \(h=0.05\), and propose a test that could reveal a real interaction independently.

Derive and check the result

The lattice expression gives \(-2/[1+\sqrt{1.0025}]\simeq-0.999376\) at 0.05, so the discretisation alone accounts for the original difference at the stated accuracy. A local physical correction with fixed continuum strength should converge toward a fixed nonzero shift as \(h\to0\), while the lattice bias vanishes approximately as \(h^2/4\). Compute control and perturbed energies on exactly the same grids and compare their difference under refinement, with consistent treatment of the perturbation's physical range. An unresolved delta perturbation represented as a fixed diagonal value on one grid point changes its effective continuum strength upon refinement and would produce another artificial shift. Cross-check with the first-order expectation of a resolved physical potential or with the independently converged contact density.

Use an isotope pair to cancel a common transition-energy reference

High-precision spectroscopy often obtains its most reliable insight from combinations of frequencies rather than absolute energies. Consider two hydrogenic model isotopes with the same nuclear charge, different reduced masses \(\mu_1,\mu_2\) and different mean-square charge radii \(R_1^2,R_2^2\). For a specified transition the leading point-Coulomb frequency can be written \(C\mu\), since its \(n\)-dependent dimensionless numbers are fixed. A local nuclear-size perturbation shifts each participating \(s\) level by a factor proportional to \(|\psi_{ns}(0)|^2 R_i^2\propto\mu_i^3 R_i^2/n^3\), while a \(p\) level receives a much smaller finite-size correction in the same limit. The different powers of reduced mass and radial quantum number permit differences of transitions to cancel selected terms.

For illustration compare a 1s–2s optical frequency with a 1s–2p frequency within one model isotope, assuming the fine, radiative and differing upper-state structures have been independently accounted for in the desired order. Subtracting these frequencies cancels the entire common lower 1s energy and the gross equal 2s/2p Coulomb energy, leaving the upper 2s–2p differences. If instead one sought the large 1s contact contribution, subtracting these same two lines would remove precisely the effect of interest. An apparently convenient common reference can therefore eliminate physical signal as well as noise. Write the coefficient of each contributing level explicitly before selecting a frequency combination.

Suppose two independently fitted line frequencies \(\nu_a,\nu_b\) each have a 10 MHz common reference error and independent residual standard errors of 0.30 and 0.40 MHz. The covariance from the shared reference is \(100\,\mathrm{MHz}^2\). The difference has variance \(0.30^2+0.40^2=0.25\,\mathrm{MHz}^2\), hence 0.50 MHz standard error. Treating both total errors as independent instead would suggest roughly 14 MHz. Conversely, an error in a calibration scale proportional to frequency leaves a residual proportional to the frequency difference; its cancellation is only approximate. Reporting the provenance of uncertainties and their covariance can matter more than recording an extra digit of each absolute transition.

Recover a contact shift from two excited-state probes

In a diagnostic model a local perturbation gives an unknown positive 1s shift \(D\), an \(ns\) shift \(D/n^3\), and zero shifts of \(p\) levels. Relative to a matched reference, how do 1s–2s, 1s–2p and 1s–3s frequencies change? Which experimental difference removes the unknown common lower-state shift yet recovers \(D\)?

Derive and check the result

The changes are \((-7D/8)/h,\ -D/h,\ (-26D/27)/h\), respectively. Subtracting the 1s–2p shift from the 1s–2s shift gives \(D/(8h)\), since the common lower-state contribution cancels. For a measured difference of \(0.90\pm0.05\) MHz the implied \(D/h=7.2\pm0.4\) MHz. The 1s–3s shift should then be \(-6.93\) MHz in this stipulated model. If unavailable corrections also differ among the upper states, that value is not an absolute nuclear-radius extraction. The third line gives an independent test of the \(1/n^3\) contact scaling before attributing all measured differences to the single parameter \(D\).

Tutorial · measure degeneracy through independent calculations

Verify two inverse moments directly in the \(2p\) state

Use \(R_{21}(r)=r e^{-r/(2a)}/(2\sqrt6\,a^{5/2})\) and normalised \(Y_{1m}\). Evaluate \(\langle r^{-2}\rangle,\langle r^{-3}\rangle\) without parameter differentiation.

Work through the solution

The radial measure is \(r^2dr\), so after inserting \(R\) the general moment is \((24a^5)^{-1}\int_0^\infty r^{4-q}e^{-r/a}dr\) for \(r^{-q}\). For \(q=2\) the integral is \(2a^3\), giving \(1/(12a^2)\). For \(q=3\) it is \(a^2\), giving \(1/(24a^3)\). Both agree with the formulas for \(n=2,l=1\). Dropping the radial Jacobian would change both powers and could falsely suggest singular behaviour. The result is independent of \(m\) because the operator is rotationally scalar.

Calculate the complete \(n=2\) shifts in units of \(C_2\)

Resolve the kinetic, spin–orbit and Darwin contributions for \(2s_{1/2}\), \(2p_{1/2}\) and \(2p_{3/2}\). Which comparison isolates the effect of the spin–orbit term cleanly at this order?

Work through the solution

For \(s\), kinetic gives \(-[2-3/8]=-13/8\) and Darwin \(+1\), summing to \(-5/8\). For both \(p\) states the kinetic value is

\[-2/3+3/8=-7/24.\]

The spin–orbit corrections are \(2/3-1=-1/3\) for \(j=1/2\) and \(2/3-1/2=1/6\) for \(j=3/2\), giving \(-5/8\) and \(-1/8\). Subtracting the two \(p\) levels removes their shared kinetic term and zero contact term. Their interval is \(C_2/2\). Comparing 2s with either \(p\) instead involves two different radial wavefunctions and does not isolate the same correction.

Separate a reduced-mass shift from fine structure

Consider two otherwise identical hydrogenic nuclei with \(M/m=1836\) and 3672. Predict the ratio of gross transition frequencies and compare its fractional change with the order of a fine-structure correction. Which common interpretation would conflate them?

Work through the solution

Gross transition frequencies scale as reduced mass \(\mu=m/(1+m/M)\). The heavier-to-lighter ratio is \((1+1/1836)/(1+1/3672)=1.000272\) approximately. The order of a relative fine-structure correction for \(Z=1\) is \(\alpha^2\simeq5.3\times10^{-5}\) with state-dependent coefficients. A change of isotope can shift a gross line by more than a particular resolved fine splitting, even with identical nuclear charge. Centroid measurements across isotopes and intervals within one multiplet are distinct observables. Recoil and nuclear magnetism require further inputs for precision predictions.

Further reading
